Stinson distinguishes interpretation from amendment; Martin identifies responsible interpretive authority; Bowen excludes unsupported litigation rationalizations. None is overruled or given an employer-factfinding effect.

### Review of completed biological consultation

#### Bennett v. Spear

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-813.
**Decided:** March 19, 1997.

##### Whether APA review is available for the §1536 claims and these interests

**Holding:** The consultation claims may proceed under APA §§702 and 704 because the ESA does not preclude that review and its citizen-suit channels supply no other adequate court remedy for the administrative claims. Alleged water restrictions unsupported by the best available scientific and commercial data implicate interests at least arguably protected by the particular §1536(a)(2) data requirement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), unanimous; both APA holdings have nine direct joins.

**Material application:** The interest inquiry concerns the provision sued upon; sound scientific decisionmaking also protects affected persons against unsupported restrictions. The §1540(g)(5) savings clause preserves available rights without itself satisfying every APA condition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 702 concerns relief other than money damages and retains other legal or equitable limits and relief forbidden by another consent-to-suit statute. A mandatory or injunctive decree must identify responsible officers and successors by name or title. Section 704 retains final-action and no-other-adequate-remedy conditions; preliminary or intermediate action ordinarily receives review with final action. Unless a statute expressly requires otherwise, final action remains final despite an application for a declaratory order or reconsideration; an administrative appeal postpones finality only when an agency rule requires it and makes the action inoperative meanwhile. No decree or unproved exhaustion prerequisite is established.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Dalton’s integrated base-closure scheme supported its confined preclusion holding; no comparable scheme is shown here. Its four-Justice recommendation-finality rationale creates no general bar.

##### Whether the Biological Opinion completes agency action with legal consequences

**Holding:** This Biological Opinion is final because it completes the Service’s consultation decision and its incidental-take statement changes legal exposure under §1536(o)(2) when the statement’s conditions are met. The Bureau’s retained operating discretion does not reduce that completed, legally consequential action to tentative advice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), unanimous.

**Material application:** Practical influence and the Bureau’s announced compliance support causation; the conditional change in taking liability separately establishes legal consequences. Under §1536(a)(2), agencies must use the best available scientific and commercial data to avoid likely jeopardy and destruction or adverse modification of designated critical habitat unless the Committee grants an (h) exemption; none is found here. Under (b)(4), the Secretary must find no (a)(2) violation or offer reasonable and prudent alternatives believed to avoid it, and conclude incidental taking will not violate that subsection. Marine-mammal taking additionally requires §1371(a)(5) authorization. The statement specifies taking impact, reasonable and prudent minimization, necessary marine-mammal measures and implementing conditions including reporting.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section (o)(2) protects only taking compliant with those conditions, not every ESA violation; the separate (o)(1) exemption is not the source of this consequence. No marine-mammal finding is required for these fish. Not every report or prediction is final, and the biological merits, triggered duties, merits application of review standards and final relief remain open.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The reviewed dismissal is reversed and remanded, leaving the separate NEPA dismissal undisturbed and ordering no substantive operational relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Franklin permits review of a completed subordinate-agency determination despite a later governmental step; its reserved presidential-status question is not decided.

For Bennett v. Spear, No. 95-813, decided March 19, 1997, see Federal Courts, “Standing to challenge a biological consultation constraint,” and Environmental Law, “Endangered Species Act citizen-suit channels,” for the separate constitutional and citizen-suit holdings.

### Privacy Act retention and continuing pertinence

#### J. Roderick MacArthur Foundation and Lance E. Lindblom v. Federal Bureau of Investigation

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit No. 95-5386; no separate Supreme Court docket stated.

**Decided:** 1998-02-13.

For statutory Article III standing, the authorized agency action and sovereign consent, and constitutional standing and dismissal form, see J. Roderick MacArthur Foundation and Lance E. Lindblom v. Federal Bureau of Investigation, D.C. Circuit No. 95-5386; no separate Supreme Court docket stated, decided 1998-02-13, Federal Courts — Privacy Act standing and jurisdictional dismissal.

##### The protected personal retention invasion can itself be an adverse effect

**Holding:** Actual unwanted maintenance of identified records describing this individual's protected associations, if outside §552a(e)(7)'s lawful alternatives, can itself supply the adverse personal effect required by §552a(g)(1)(D). A merely abstract or technical violation with no adverse personal consequence is insufficient; an adverse agency determination, public disclosure, lost employment or pecuniary loss is not independently required as such.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II, joined by Breyer, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Scalia and Thomas reserve this construction; Stone-Zsela does not join it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The personal adverse effect is not a finding of distress or actual damages, nor is every statutory violation sufficient. No actual nonpertinence is found.

##### Continued retention must satisfy the complete Continuing Pertinence Test

**Holding:** Section 552a(e)(7) regulates continued maintenance as well as original collection. Unless retention is expressly authorized by statute or by the individual, the information must remain pertinent to and within the scope of an authorized law-enforcement activity; lawful original collection is not conclusive permission to retain it after every qualifying connection has ceased. The Court adopts the following complete rule:

1. **Does the challenged record fall within the statutory protection?**

   The plaintiff identifies records describing their First Amendment activities and establishes that the government continues to maintain them. The court first checks whether retention is independently authorized by statute or by the individual.

2. **Can the government identify an authorized law-enforcement activity?**

   The agency must establish the legal authority for that activity. It need not prove the plaintiff committed a crime. Intelligence investigations, criminal investigations, and authorized civil enforcement may qualify.

3. **Does the information remain pertinent to that activity?**

   The court examines the actual relationship, not a hypothetical future possibility. Relevant considerations include:

   - Why the information was collected.
   - What investigative purpose it still serves.
   - Whether it preserves necessary context or evidence.
   - Whether subsequent events have changed its relevance.
   - Whether continued use or indexing exceeds the justified investigative scope.

   No single consideration automatically decides the case.

4. **What disposition and remedy does the law authorize?**

   If a qualifying connection remains, the agency may retain the information under the statutory exception. If no qualifying connection remains, the court determines the violation and available relief under the Privacy Act.

   The court separately accounts for lawful evidence-preservation and federal records-management duties.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III, joined by Breyer, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Each joins the entire four-step rule, including every listed consideration and the separate preservation/relief requirements. Scalia and Thomas reject the complete rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Authorized activity may include genuine historical intelligence value, connected or later inquiries and necessary evidentiary context; no active investigation of the plaintiff is required. General agency mission or hypothetical future usefulness is insufficient by itself. Age, closure, innocence and name retrieval do not control. The considerations are nonexclusive and impose no necessity or least-restrictive-recordkeeping test, periodic audit, deadline, universal consultation or disclosure of classified reasons.

##### The statutory judgment requires record-specific further proceedings

**Holding:** The conclusive original-collection rule cannot sustain summary judgment on Lindblom's retention claim. That component is vacated and remanded for the complete test's application to the actual records, with any independent statutory or individual authorization and lawful preservation obligation considered separately; the Court awards neither expungement nor damages.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part V-A has Breyer, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Scalia and Thomas oppose statutory remand.

##### Each remedial route retains its independent requirements

**Holding:** The retention restriction does not create unlimited relief: monetary liability under §552a(g)(1)(D)/(g)(4), amendment relief under (d)/(g)(1)(A)/(g)(2), access relief, and any independently permissible prospective equity retain their distinct conditions. Neither an automatic monetary award nor a general expungement entitlement follows from the Court's construction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's opinion: Part V-B has all eight Associates. Stone-Zsela agrees that no final award issues and joins Part V-A's separate lawful-relief and preservation directions, without adopting V-B's additional grounds.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Money retains intentional/willful noncompliance, causally compensable actual damages and statutory-minimum questions. Amendment relief retains request, refusal, review and exemption predicates; exhausted access requests do not establish amendment exhaustion. Sections (j)/(k) cannot exempt (e)(7), though a valid (d) exemption may defeat derivative amendment relief. Prospective equity retains a lawful action, preclusion and ordinary limits; no separate APA claim is created. If authorized, a mandatory §702 decree must identify the responsible officer or successor by name or title.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate Lindblom’s statutory summary judgment and remand, 7–2, under the complete test; sustain both constitutional standing rejections and remove their with-prejudice effect unanimously. No damages, expungement, discovery reversal, new hearing, agency audit or destruction is ordered. Original collection and unreviewed components remain untouched.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Steel Co. (June 2, 1997):** Applied for constitutional power before disputed statutory merits and the distinction between substantial claims and recovery; its fully corrected reporting default does not decide this ongoing personal-custody controversy. Its confined already-controlled-merits exceptions are not used.
- **Bell v. Hood:** Applied to the substantial federal claim; preserved as neither a sovereign waiver nor permission to assume personal injury.
- **Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife:** Applied for concrete personal injury, direct traceability, likely redress and summary-judgment evidence; no particular-document or additional downstream-loss requirement is added.
- **Warth v. Seldin; Havens Realty Corp. v. Coleman:** Applied for actual personal statutory interests and the distinction from abstract illegality; neither is treated as deciding Privacy Act remedies or its adverse-effect text.
- **Arizonans for Official English:** Applied to exclude legally impossible remedies and to permit limited jurisdictional correction; no magic-word damages rule is inferred.
- **Lee v. Harcleroad:** Applied to claim-specific proof and jurisdictional dismissal; its unsupported future patient injury does not erase an established personal statutory occurrence.
- **Alexander v. Whitman:** Applied for meaningful partial redress despite uncertain ultimate recovery, preserving procedural-stage and defendant-specific limits; its controlled-merits setting is not extended.
- **FDIC v. Meyer:** Applied to distinguish sovereign consent, an authorized action and relief; its ban on direct agency Bivens damages remains intact.
- **Department of Defense v. FLRA:** Applied to the distinct Privacy Act disclosure protection and enumerated exceptions; neither its disclosure balance nor §(b)(7) is substituted for retention's three alternatives.
- **Laird v. Tatum; Meese v. Keene:** Applied respectively to subjective constitutional chill and a supported governmental mechanism for reputational injury; neither categorically excludes the personal statutory interest decided here.
- **Chamber of Commerce v. Reich:** Preserved for qualified prospective equitable restraint; no automatic bypass of the Privacy Act's remedial scheme is adopted.

The circuit decisions Albright, Doe v. FBI, Nagel and Clarkson inform the distinct statutory elements and remedial routes at their actual scope. Albright's damages questions and Doe's case-specific adverse-effect finding are not imported as findings here. No Supreme Court precedent is overruled.

### Medicare reimbursement bases and payment finality

#### Regions Hospital v. Shalala

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1375.

**Decided:** 1998-02-24.

##### Authority to determine the lawful base

**Holding:** Section 1395ww(h)(2)(A) permits the Secretary to correct the allowable fiscal-1984 graduate-medical-education cost figure used in the later statutory per-resident reimbursement formula. This authority to determine a lawful historical input does not itself reopen the final payment for the base year or authorize departure from the cost rules governing that year.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), Part I-A: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Part I-B’s construction and permissible-deference rationale independently controls with Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Authority to correct the historical input is distinct from reopening the annual payment. The Secretary must apply the cost rules governing the base year, not a newly invented allowability standard.

**Material application:** The reasonable-cost context and the new enduring reimbursement formula mean that the statutory recognition language does not unambiguously freeze every expense previously reimbursed. The five-Justice construction permits excluding costs unallowable under existing principles while preserving the closed annual payment.

##### Antecedent facts, later payments and finality

**Holding:** Applying the existing base-year cost principles to antecedent data for future reimbursement does not impose a new legal consequence on the closed 1984 payment and is not impermissibly retroactive. A later already-paid year may be corrected only if its own payment determination remains independently open under governing reopening rules; closed determinations cannot be revised or recouped under this holding.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), Part II: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, on the specific later-payment analysis.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirmance permits a corrected base for future calculations and later determinations independently open under their own reopening rules. It permits no recoupment or revision of a closed payment, including the 1984 award.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This later-payment application recognizes neither general retroactive-rulemaking authority nor general power to revise final agency decisions.

##### Reasonableness and the asserted reliance burden

**Holding:** The challenged reaudit rule is reasonable on the supported administrative record because it corrects an enduring formula input under existing cost law and provides the stated two-direction corrections and documentation accommodations. This facial conclusion does not validate every particular expense disallowance, allocation or future evidentiary accommodation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), Part III: Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Material application:** The rule permits requested inclusion of GME costs misclassified as operating expenses and an upward hospital-specific operating adjustment for costs improperly classified as GME. When original physician-time records are unavailable, later reporting-period evidence may document allocation; Regions used 1989 and 1990 studies.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The facial reasonableness determination approves no particular expense disallowance, indefinite serial reaudit, new base-year standard or defective procedure. Reliance and evidentiary accommodation in a materially different application remain open.

##### The intermediary notice does not establish adjudicated issue preclusion here

**Holding:** On this record, the intermediary's final annual reimbursement notice and payment-finality regulations do not conclusively adjudicate every underlying cost fact for the distinct statutory base determination. No Provider Reimbursement Review Board adjudicatory hearing resolved the reasonableness of these costs, so the asserted issue-preclusion objection does not bar this use.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), Part IV: Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No adjudicatory Board hearing resolved the cost reasonableness issue on this record. The Court does not hold administrative adjudications universally nonpreclusive or permit reconsideration of completed Article III judgments.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Brown requires statutory authority before deference; the agency may not add a condition contrary to the governing text.
- Landgraf is applied to the particular consequence for later payments; its rule against unauthorized new retrospective burdens remains intact.
- Bowen preserves the limit on retroactive legislative rules and rejection of unsupported litigation rationalizations; neither authority is expanded.
- Good Samaritan preserves correction within authorized methods and legal boundaries, not an entitlement to every claimed historical expense.
- Thomas Jefferson supplies the distinct limits on interpreting an agency's own regulation and on reliance on its actual ground; that standard does not replace statutory construction here.
- Guernsey preserves lawful accounting allocation without permission to contradict a binding regulation.
- Chenery preserves review on the agency's actual rationale.
- Allentown requires use of the actual announced standard and the whole record; no substituted base-year cost standard is approved.


### APA review of Forest Service sale authorizations under the MBTA

#### Newton County Wildlife Association v. Rogers

**Docket or dockets:** 97-774; Eighth Circuit Nos. 96-1994 and 96-3463.

**Decided:** 1998-09-28.

##### The APA authorization challenge requires its own statutory inquiry

**Holding:** A court cannot sustain dismissal of an APA challenge to the Forest Service's affirmative sale authorization solely because the MBTA supplies no implied private action, the sovereign is not subject to the asserted criminal punishment, or FWS has not demanded a permit. The lower courts must examine the challenged authorization under the applicable MBTA text and regulations, the APA's retained conditions and the actual preservation and remedial record; the present judgment establishes no MBTA violation or permit obligation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Ginsburg). Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, has five direct rationale votes. The four dissenters agree that an implied private action and a demand for FWS enforcement are distinct from APA review, but would sustain the agency-permit coverage ground; they do not join this remand holding.

**Material application:** Bennett separates an available APA route from a particular statute's private enforcement mechanism and retains §§702 and 704's actual conditions. The pleading challenges completed Forest Service authorizations as contrary to applicable law. It is not an action to punish the United States criminally or to compel FWS to prosecute. Heckler v. Chaney protects discretionary nonenforcement within its proper setting; silence by the enforcement agency does not transform every challenge to another agency's affirmative act into a nonenforcement suit. Lincoln v. Vigil likewise preserves enforceable statutory boundaries.

The penalty provision's list of covered persons and the contemporaneous permit regulations require consideration, but they do not alone answer what substantive constraints apply to the challenged authorization through the APA. Cooper and Will recognize a context-sensitive sovereign presumption; the criminal actor question cannot replace examination of the distinct legal act and asserted obligation. The contrary reading treats absence of sovereign punishment as conclusive of a different review question. That is insufficient to sustain this dismissal.

Sweet Home requires attention to the statute actually governing the alleged injury; its ESA definition of harm cannot be transplanted into the MBTA. The remand must address §703's operative prohibition, causal and regulatory limits and any properly presented preservation issue. The conceded prospect of bird deaths alone does not prove a violation. The Court decides neither the Act's reach to incidental killing nor private-logger or individual-officer liability, and assumes no contractor theory was preserved.

APA review retains injury and redressability, the provision-specific protected-interest inquiry, final agency action, no other adequate court remedy, applicable preclusion and committed-to-discretion limits, and relief other than money damages under §702. Any decree must satisfy the ordinary legal and equitable conditions. No treaty-based action, permit system or immediate operational decree is created.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Bennett is applied to the separate APA route and its conditions; Chaney and Lincoln retain their actual discretion boundaries; Cooper and Will are distinguished from a conclusive answer to this different APA authorization question; Sweet Home's statute-specific method is preserved without importing ESA coverage.

Related questions in Newton County Wildlife Association v. Rogers, 1998-09-28, dockets 97-774; Eighth Circuit Nos. 96-1994 and 96-3463, appear under Federal Courts — Expired preliminary relief and necessary review of an entered dismissal.


### I. National implementing rules coexist with state determinations

#### AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 97-826, 97-829, 97-830, 97-831, 97-1075, 97-1087, 97-1099 and 97-1141; including California respondents in the AT&T, MCI and FCC petitions.
**Decided:** 1999-01-25.

##### I. National implementing rules coexist with state determinations

**Holding:** Section 201(b) authorizes FCC rules implementing the local-competition obligations added to the Communications Act by §§251–252. That authority includes national standards governing the statutory tasks while state commissions retain the negotiations, arbitrations, individual rates and other determinations Congress assigned to them.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Parts I–II are joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Six Justices control the general-authority and associated remand propositions; O'Connor, Thomas and Breyer dissent on their distinct allocation grounds.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Verizon Communications Inc. v. FCC, May 13, 2002, resolves the reserved TELRIC methodology question within the statutory pricing framework; particular state rates and as-applied confiscation remain separate. The national-implementation and state-determination allocation is unchanged.

##### II. The rural-exemption and agreement rules require review within the correct allocation

**Holding:** The categorical absence-of-federal-authority grounds for vacating Rules 51.405 and 51.303 and displacing otherwise lawful federal rules from state implementation cannot stand. Unresolved substantive objections return for review with the actual statutory rural-exemption, agreement-approval and state-law preservation limits intact.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same six-Justice coalition controls Part II. The remand does not adopt a new rule resolving the returned substantive objections.

##### III. A network element need not be a detachable physical object

**Holding:** Section 153(29)'s network-element definition includes features, functions and capabilities supplied through facilities or equipment, as well as the physical facilities or equipment themselves. Eligibility under that definition does not establish that §251(d)(2) requires unbundled access to every eligible function.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part III is joined by all seven other Associate Justices. Those eight supply a controlling majority; Stone-Zsela agrees only in the judgment on the independent definition component.

##### IV. Necessity and impairment must perform independent limiting work

**Holding:** In selecting required network elements under §251(d)(2), the FCC must give independent limiting effect to necessity for proprietary elements and impairment for network elements generally, considering realistically available alternatives outside as well as inside the incumbent's network. Rule 51.319 is set aside because the adopted comparison and impairment standard did not perform that task.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part IV is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, giving the complete rule seven votes. Stone-Zsela additionally joins the invalidity holding that the rule must make element-specific judgments giving necessity and impairment independent constraining force, and joins vacatur and agency remand; he does not supply a separate join in the more specific outside-alternatives requirement. Souter dissents from invalidation.

##### V. The Act imposes no freestanding own-facilities prerequisite

**Holding:** A requesting telecommunications carrier need not own some local facilities merely to qualify to seek unbundled access under §251(c)(3). Its entitlement to particular elements remains governed by §251(d)(2) and the lawful conditions of access and combination.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part V is joined by all seven other Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees only in rejection of the independent own-facilities prerequisite, without a ground.

##### VI. Existing combinations need not be dismantled

**Holding:** Rule 51.315(b) lawfully prohibits an incumbent from separating requested network elements already combined in its network unless the requesting carrier asks for separation. Unbundled availability and valuation do not require physical destruction of an existing combination.

**Proposition-level authority:** All nine Justices join Scalia's Parts VI–VII; those parts control the existing-combination and individual-arrangement rules with their stated limits.

##### VII. An entrant may select an individual agreement arrangement with its pertinent conditions

**Holding:** Section 252(i) permits Rule 51.809's access to an individual interconnection, service or network-element arrangement in an approved agreement on the same pertinent rates, terms and conditions. The rule preserves proof of greater provision costs or technical infeasibility and limits availability to a reasonable period after public filing.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same nine-Justice coalition joins Part VII and controls this rule and its qualifications.

##### VIII. Dialing-parity authority and numbering-cost ripeness remain distinct

**Holding:** The categorical absence-of-FCC-authority ground for vacating intraLATA toll-dialing-parity rules is rejected, with remaining objections returned for review. The separate Rule 52.17 numbering-cost challenge remains unripe because its asserted disadvantage depends on particular cost-recovery rate decisions not before the Court.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part VIII parity-authority ground is joined by Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. O'Connor agrees on the narrower §251(b)(3)/§251(d)(1) implementation ground stated in her separate opinion. Stone-Zsela agrees in the parity disposition without an additional ground; the five-Justice lead rationale independently controls. All eight Associates join Part VIII's separate numbering-cost ripeness holding; Stone-Zsela agrees only in that disposition.

##### IX. Technical feasibility cannot replace the required access criteria

**Holding:** The partial vacatur of Rule 51.317's technical-feasibility presumption and Order paragraphs 278 and 281 is affirmed because the presumption and its actual exceptions fail to preserve the required necessity and impairment limits. The separately sustained definition of technical feasibility and other unaffected portions of the rule remain outside that invalidation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part IX is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Those seven control the substantive presumption holding. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the partial invalidation; Souter would remand this component without a final validity ruling.

##### X. Superior-quality duties return for review under the correct framework

**Holding:** The invalidation of Rules 51.305(a)(4) and 51.311(c) is vacated and remanded for reconsideration of the actual conditions and agency justification under the correct implementing authority. The Court does not decide their ultimate validity or order unconditional reinstatement.

**Proposition-level authority:** All eight Associates join Part X's corrected-standard remand and its confined review directions. Thomas and Breyer do so under the Court's governing authority framework while retaining their dissent from Part I. Stone-Zsela agrees in the remand without adding a ground.

##### XI. Affirmative new-combination duties require separate reconsideration

**Holding:** The invalidation of Rule 51.315(c)–(f) is vacated and remanded for review under the proper implementing-power standard. Section 251(c)(3)'s direction to enable requesting carriers to combine elements does not unambiguously assign every physical combination task exclusively to the requester; the particular affirmative duties and burdens remain undecided.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part XI is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Breyer's agreement is a remand under the governing authority framework, not a Part I join. Seven Justices control this remand; Stone-Zsela agrees only in its disposition, and Thomas dissents on his independent task-allocation ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T* is applied to identify actual delegation and preserve enacted limiting conditions; it is distinguished from a categorical ban on consequential implementing rules. Its tariff-filing holding is not changed.
- *Louisiana Public Service Commission v. FCC* retains its protection of matters Congress left wholly intrastate; the Court distinguishes the newly enacted federal local-competition obligations from those matters.
- *Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. NRDC* supplies review within actual delegation and statutory limits, not authority to remove necessity or impairment from the selection inquiry.
- *Allentown Mack Sales & Service v. NLRB* remains authority for administering the actual announced lawful standard; no judicial substitution of an economic rule is authorized.
- *Ohio Forestry Association v. Sierra Club* and *Toilet Goods Association v. Gardner* are applied to the difference between settled policy and presently consequential application, and the fitness/hardship inquiry.
- *Abbott Laboratories v. Gardner* retains its fitness-and-hardship framework; a presently burdensome rule remains distinct from this forum announcement.
- *Lee v. Harcleroad* is distinguished because present civil duties can create hardship without criminal penalties; the announcement here itself imposes no such duty.
- *Pic-A-State PA, Inc. v. Reno* is distinguished because its claimant's existing business was actually stopped by the challenged law and real enforcement risk.
- *Franklin v. Massachusetts* and *Bennett v. Spear* are distinguished as completed determinations carrying concrete legal consequences; finality alone supplies no section 208 ripeness holding.
- *NAACP, Los Angeles Branch v. Jones* and *Wisconsin Right to Life v. Paradise* require attention to the actual jurisdictional claim and remedy; neither commands another factual inquiry after this instrument's threshold status is ascertainable.

**Limits and questions not reached:** the FCC may issue national implementing rules for §§251–252 while respecting specifically assigned state functions. Required-element selection must give necessity and impairment independent effect and consider realistic alternatives; the existing Rule 51.319 list and identified feasibility presumption cannot supply the defective standard. Functional network elements and requests by carriers without their own facilities remain eligible subject to lawful access criteria. Existing combinations and qualifying individual agreement arrangements receive the protections and limits stated here. Superior-quality and affirmative new-combination requirements return for review without final merits approval. General rulemaking authority decides no particular rate or section 208 enforcement power. The general section 208 announcement and contingent California numbering-cost challenge receive their distinct unripe dispositions; no carrier proceeding, penalty or state order is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The two reviewed judgments are affirmed in part, reversed in part, and vacated in part, and the cases are remanded for the following distinct dispositions.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| General implementing/pricing authority and associated rural-exemption, preexisting-agreement and state-compliance grounds | Reversed in the identified respects, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg | O'Connor, Thomas and Breyer | Remove the categorical authority grounds; return unresolved substantive objections without deciding TELRIC or individual rates. |
| Section 208 general enforcement announcement | Vacated and remanded for dismissal without prejudice as unripe, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | No enforcement-authority merits ruling; no open-ended threshold remand. |
| Network-element definition and own-facilities prerequisite | Physical-only definition and freestanding ownership prerequisite rejected, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Sustain the challenged statutory eligibility construction; access still requires the enacted criteria. |
| Rule 51.319 required-element list | Sustaining judgment reversed; rule vacated, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Souter | Return to the Commission for lawful element-specific necessity/impairment application; no replacement list selected. |
| Rule 51.315(b), existing combinations | Invalidation reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Restore existing-combination protection, including separation at the requester's request; no automatic restoration of (c)–(f). |
| Rule 51.809, §252(i) individual arrangements | Invalidation reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Restore the rule with pertinent conditions, greater-cost/technical-infeasibility exceptions and reasonable availability period. |
| California intraLATA toll-dialing-parity authority | Authority vacatur reversed and residual issues remanded, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg | Thomas and Breyer | No particular parity implementation or rate is adjudicated. |
| California Rule 52.17 numbering costs | Unripe disposition retained, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | No particular cost allocation or rate-recovery injury decided. |
| Rule 51.317 technical-feasibility presumption; Order ¶¶278 and 281 | Identified partial invalidation affirmed, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Souter | The technical-feasibility definition and unaffected rule portions survive; defective selection criteria require reconsideration. |
| Rules 51.305(a)(4) and 51.311(c), superior quality | Invalidation vacated and remanded, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Corrected-standard review; no final merits approval or unconditional reinstatement. |
| Rule 51.315(c)–(f), affirmative new combinations | Invalidation vacated and remanded, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Thomas | Review actual duties, conditions and burdens under proper implementing authority; no compelled particular combination. |


For the other questions in AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, decided 1999-01-25, at the dockets stated above, see Federal Courts.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Verizon Communications Inc. v. FCC, May 13, 2002, sustains Rule 51.315(c)–(f) at its enacted conditions and supported scope, resolving the affirmative-combination question left open here. The earlier existing-combination protection and independent necessity-and-impairment requirements remain intact.

### APA review of PTO factual findings

#### Dickinson v. Zurko

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-377.
**Decided:** 1999-06-10.

##### APA review of PTO factual findings

**Holding:** Direct Federal Circuit review of PTO factual findings is governed by the applicable APA court-agency standard, not the clearly-erroneous standard used to review district-court findings. Section 559 preserves additional requirements imposed by statute or otherwise recognized by law, but the asserted pre-APA direct-review requirement has not been sufficiently established; the Federal Circuit must identify and apply the proper §706 category on the whole record.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Breyer.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Morgan v. Daniels retains its priority-dispute rule in the later equity-suit setting; it does not establish the claimed general direct-review clear-error standard.
- Universal Camera supplies meaningful whole-record scrutiny and preservation of agency choices among fairly conflicting views.
- Allentown Mack requires scrutiny under the actual announced governing standard; it supplies no patent exception or general de novo review.
- Greenwich Collieries requires identification of the statutory departure actually invoked; its persuasion-burden holding is not itself an appellate-review standard.
- Haggar preserves its distinction between lawful delegated legal interpretation and factual adjudication; it does not select the standard for direct PTO factual review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Federal Circuit must use the APA framework in this direct PTO appeal. Section 559 continues to preserve additional requirements actually established by prior law; the asserted general patent-review exception fails. Section 706(2)(E) applies on its statutory hearing-record predicates, while (A) and authorized trial-de-novo review under (F) retain their separate terms. The Court does not collapse those categories or govern district-court findings on a new evidentiary record.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Reversed and remanded, 7–2.** **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Breyer. **Opposing:** Kennedy and Ginsburg. The Federal Circuit must identify and apply the proper APA §706 category to the PTO's factual findings on the whole record. The Court chooses neither §706(2)(A) nor (E) and decides no patentability question.

### Trade-court factfinding does not eliminate lawful delegated interpretation

#### United States v. Haggar Apparel Co.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-2044; judgment below, 127 F.3d 1460.
**Decided:** 1999-04-21.

##### Trade-court factfinding does not eliminate lawful delegated interpretation

**Holding:** A formally promulgated Customs regulation implementing Congress's actual delegation receives Chevron review in tariff litigation: courts first enforce unambiguous statutory requirements and the delegation's boundaries, then assess a reasonable agency choice within genuine ambiguity. The Court of International Trade's duty to decide on its own record does not repeal that legal framework; agency labels and notice and comment alone do not establish authority or validity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion, Parts I–III, unanimous.

##### This regulation's statutory fit and actual application require the corrected inquiry

**Holding:** The judgment must be vacated because categorical nondeference left unresolved whether the regulation reasonably respects the tariff's incidental-assembly allowance and lawfully applies to this process. On remand the courts must enforce the enacted conditions, examine the regulation's actual introductory terms and examples, and resolve relevant application questions under the proper review standards; neither regulatory validity nor the claimed exemption is established in advance.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Breyer (seven Justices).

**Current-force modification:** United States v. Mead Corp., June 18, 2001, resolves the reserved informal-letter question for the transaction-specific, revisable Customs classification mechanism at issue there. Such a letter lacks delegated controlling interpretive force, but its reasons require independent consideration for their actual persuasive value. Haggar's treatment of genuinely delegated regulations and independent judicial factfinding remains intact; Mead neither excludes every informal interpretation nor fixes the disputed tariff classification.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The tariff requires United States fabricated components exported ready for assembly without further fabrication, retention of physical identity and no foreign advancement or improvement except assembly and incidental operations including cleaning, lubricating and painting. The allowance reduces duties by qualifying component cost or value, with proportional treatment of specific/compound rates; it does not exempt the entire article. Regulation §10.16(b) permits incidental operations before, during or after assembly, including ordinary final pressing/folding; §10.16(c)'s significant nonassembly processes and unrelated processes retain their introductory conditions despite chemical-treatment examples. Timing after sewing alone does not decide the issue. Informal letters and later administrative doctrines are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate and remand for the regulation's statutory fit and actual application without categorical nondeference. No regulatory validity, exemption, refund, duty amount, new hearing or finding of missing papers is established.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chevron supplies review within actual delegated authority and statutory ambiguity.
- NationsBank of North Carolina v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. permits reasonable administrative classification within statutory limits.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T and National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank require enforcement of enacted boundaries before deference.
- AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board separates general authority, particular rule validity and unresolved applications; it creates no automatic validation or universal remand rule.
- NFFE/FLRA v. Department of the Interior distinguishes legally available discretion from its lawful exercise; its distinct remedy does not compel a new Customs proceeding.

**Later-authority backlinks:** United States v. Mead Corp., June 18, 2001 — controlling authority and persuasive consideration of Customs classification letters under the mechanism reviewed.

### Telecommunications customer approval

#### U.S. West, Inc. v. Federal Communications Commission

**Docket or dockets:** Tenth Circuit No. 98-9518; judgment below, 182 F.3d 1224. No Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** February 7, 2000.

##### Whether the general statutory approval language permits affirmative consent

**Holding:** Section 222(c)(1), read with the confidentiality duty, permits the FCC to require an affirmative customer decision through oral, written or electronic means without unambiguously compelling that exclusive method for every general CPNI use. After independent rejection of the presented speech challenge, that choice receives ordinary Chevron deference; the asserted speech-based constitutional doubt cannot sustain vacatur, while the unreached takings and other arbitrary-and-capricious claims remain for review.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Breyer; seven direct votes. Stevens and Kennedy's abstract textual agreement is not a join in this part or the judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The construction concerns general section 222(c)(1), not every CPNI category. Constitutional validity receives independent review before ordinary statutory deference. Takings and other arbitrary-and-capricious objections, every collateral restriction, final ownership and compensation, and the later emergency-information cross-reference remain undecided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Chevron is applied to the general approval gap after independent constitutional review. AT&T preserves statutory limits and separate review of unresolved objections. No avoidance principle permits assumed speech invalidity to replace the analysis required by the governing cases.

For the independent commercial-speech holdings in U.S. West, February 7, 2000, Tenth Circuit No. 98-9518, see First Amendment — Confidential telecommunications information and marketing.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the general approval-construction ground as well as the speech ground and remand for unreached takings and arbitrary-and-capricious claims and relief conforming to intervening instruments. Reversal does not reinstate every former rule, prescribe a new consent system, or establish ownership, compensation, violation or sanctions.

Public Law 106-81 section 5 took effect October 26, 1999 without a section 5 delay. Section 222(f) separately requires express prior authorization for wireless call-location information except the specified section 222(d)(4) emergency uses, and for automatic-crash-notification information except use in operating an automatic-crash-notification system. The location exceptions permit provision to a public safety answering point, emergency medical or dispatch provider, public safety, fire-service or law-enforcement official, or hospital emergency or trauma-care facility to respond to the user's emergency-services call; informing the legal guardian or immediate family of the user's location in an emergency risking death or serious physical harm; and provision to information- or database-management providers solely to assist delivery of emergency services in response to an emergency. Those exceptions do not permit ordinary marketing; the crash-notification exception remains separate.

The amendment adds location to CPNI, moves definitions to subsection (h), and imposes a separate emergency-service information duty. Despite subsections (b), (c) and (d), new subsection (g) separately directs telephone-exchange-service carriers to provide the information it describes, including unlisted or unpublished subscriber information and other carriers' subscriber information in their possession or control, on a timely and unbundled basis, on nondiscriminatory and reasonable rates, terms and conditions, to emergency-service and emergency-support-service providers solely for delivering or assisting emergency services. Its enacted cross-reference is preserved in the exact source text and is not construed in this admission. The Court neither construes subsection (g)'s enacted cross-reference nor adjudicates those later provisions' constitutionality. They do not retroactively establish the FCC's 1998 explanation or permit a general assertion that opt-out is available for every CPNI category.

FCC 99-223, adopted August 16, released September 3 and published October 1, 1999, retains affirmative approval in paragraph 95. Paragraphs 185 and 190 condition amended information-collection rules on OMB approval and a later effective-date publication; no such publication is established in this record. Paragraphs 187 and 188 separately remove the winback and same-document notice restrictions. Paragraph 189 postpones enforcement of amended section 64.2009(a) and (c) safeguards until eight months after release. Equipment/information-service and safeguard amendments are not all treated as effective on publication, and reversal does not resurrect removed provisions. No compliance, default, sanction exposure or expired transition is determined.



### Statutory limits on customary-tobacco regulation

#### FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp., 529 U.S. 120 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-1152.

**Decided:** March 21, 2000.

##### Which FDCA version governs any tobacco authority after the 1997 Act

**Holding:** Public Law 105-115, section 422, leaves the question of tobacco authority unchanged and requires any such authority to be exercised under the FDCA as it stood on November 20, 1997. The Act's new mission and regulatory procedures therefore supply neither a new tobacco delegation nor a means to avoid a restriction of that earlier regime.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; five direct votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 501's generally applicable ninety-day effective date after November 21, 1997 yields to express provisions and excludes sections 111, 121, 125 and 307. It does not override section 422. Section 406's new mission and later classification, review and evidentiary procedures do not enter the earlier tobacco regime; preexisting qualified scientific-evidence and reclassification flexibility remains.

##### Whether the connected statutory scheme authorizes the asserted customary-tobacco drug/device regime

**Holding:** Read with its operative safety and marketing requirements and Congress's distinct tobacco enactments, the FDCA does not authorize the asserted drug/device regime for tobacco as customarily marketed. The FDA cannot obtain that power by construing broad coverage terms to include a continuing-market accommodation that the connected statutory requirements do not permit; the challenged rule is invalid on that ground.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; five direct votes.

**Material application:** Intended-use evidence is assumed for this inquiry. The chemical action of nicotine does not decide the delivery component's classification, and therapeutic advertising is not the sole possible intended use. Combination-product assignment of responsibility under section 353(g) does not waive independent drug, misbranding, safety, approval or cessation commands. The Court rejects the claimed continuing-market accommodation under this particular scheme; it adopts no magnitude-based major-questions rule or categorical bar to reasoned agency reinterpretation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The following distinctions limit the statutory reasoning; they are not new enforcement findings:

- **Directions and warnings:** Section 352(f)(1) requires an exemption by regulation when adequate directions are unnecessary for public-health protection. That directions exemption does not waive section 352(f)(2)'s separate necessary warnings concerning pathological conditions, childhood use, unsafe dosage, method or duration. Section 352(j) concerns danger under labeled dosage, manner, frequency or duration; existing tobacco warnings do not automatically establish FDCA compliance.
- **Classification and approval:** Device classes depend on their statutory control and information criteria, including the qualified low-risk route; risk alone does not compel an immediate judicial ban. The earlier scientific-evidence and reclassification procedures remain. No particular classification, new-drug application, grandfather status or approval is adjudicated. Section 321(p)'s pre-June 25, 1938 exception requires the drug's prior-Act status and unchanged labeling representations; the Court does not find those conditions satisfied or unsatisfied here.
- **Restricted devices:** Section 360j(e) permits practitioner-authorization or other sale, distribution or use conditions upon the required determination that harmful potential or necessary collateral measures prevent reasonable assurance of safety and effectiveness otherwise. Training or facility limits require their own safe-use determination; lack of specified board eligibility or certification alone cannot exclude a user. Required label statements remain. None of those permissions is a general exemption from another command.
- **Ban proceedings:** Section 360f permits rulemaking on findings of substantial deception or unreasonable and substantial risk. When the Secretary has determined that labeling can correct the problem and provided written notice specifying the change and time allowed, failure to make that change is separately required. Immediate effect additionally requires unreasonable, direct and substantial danger and advance manufacturer notice, followed by prompt notice, an informal-hearing opportunity and expeditious affirmance, modification or revocation.
- **Cessation and recall:** Section 360h(e)(1) requires immediate cessation of distribution and notification and cessation-of-use instructions to health professionals and user facilities after a reasonable-probability finding that a human-use device would cause serious adverse health consequences or death. An informal-hearing opportunity must be provided within ten days of the order, and inadequate grounds require vacatur. Recall requires a further determination, timetable and progress reports; it cannot reach devices held by individuals, and the greater-risk exception concerns recall from user facilities. Those recall limits do not make the initial cessation command elective. Individual risk-notice duties remain. The Court makes none of the required enforcement determinations itself.
- **Other enactments and State law:** The cigarette statutes' labeling and advertising limits and the smokeless-tobacco statute's distinct package/advertisement rules retain their own scopes, including the latter's outdoor-billboard exception. Section 360k requires a relevant federal device requirement and a different or additional State duty in the specified subject relationship. Its exemption procedure requires an application, regulation, notice and oral-hearing opportunity; greater stringency is one route, compelling local conditions without violating federal requirements another. Medtronic therefore prevents treating coverage alone as universal displacement of State youth-access laws. No particular preemption or exemption is decided.

The Court decides no advertising merits, constitutional objection, therapeutic-claim product, biological-license question, particular new-drug refusal ground, international coordination requirement or later classification procedure. It does not pronounce every risky device unmarketable, require therapeutic claims for every drug, create a general prohibition on agency change, or require Congress to use particular words in every delegation. Congress may confer tobacco authority expressly.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm invalidation of the August 28, 1996 rule for want of the asserted authority. Only genuinely necessary implementation or severability proceedings remain permissible. No replacement rule, tobacco ban, recall, damages or retrospective liability is ordered; the separately vacated advertising construction remains unadopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.:** Applies statutory construction before deference; agency reasonableness cannot supply a delegation absent from the enacted scheme.
- **United States v. Haggar Apparel Co.:** Preserves the distinction between genuine delegated ambiguity, regulatory validity and application; formal rulemaking alone does not establish authority.
- **MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co.:** Applies its requirement that a bounded agency power preserve an operative statutory obligation. It creates no universal magnitude-based exception to delegation.
- **Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon:** Preserves broad statutory coverage within actual delegated language and concrete limits; it does not authorize disregard of a connected command.
- **AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board:** Preserves separate analysis of general implementing power, particular statutory constraints and unresolved applications.
- **Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala:** Preserves considered interpretive change within the statute; the FDA's former position is not an automatic legal bar.
- **National Railroad Passenger Corp. v. Boston & Maine Corp.:** Preserves recognition of the agency's actual discernible ground without supplying missing findings or a judicial replacement policy.
- **Adams Fruit Co. v. Barrett:** Preserves the requirement of an actual congressional delegation; an agency cannot create its own authority.
- **United States v. Bacto-Unidisk:** Its broad protective reading of the FDCA remains, subject to the entire enacted scheme rather than isolated definitions.
- **United States v. Rutherford:** Preserves statutory safety and effectiveness requirements against a judicially created exemption; it is not a present tobacco ban or an adjudication of an application.
- **Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr:** Preserves the actual federal/state counterpart and subject conditions for device preemption; federal coverage does not automatically eliminate every State rule.
- **United States v. Locke:** Its particular national-vessel and local-waterway allocation remains distinct; overlap alone supplies no general preemption rule here.



### Advisory statutory interpretations and regulatory textual fit

#### Christensen v. Harris County, 529 U.S. 576 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-1167.
**Decided:** May 1, 2000.

##### II. This advisory letter receives persuasive consideration, not controlling statutory weight

**Holding:** The supported advisory opinion letter lacks the force of law and does not command controlling statutory deference under Chevron. Its interpretation remains entitled to consideration under Skidmore according to the quality of its reasoning, experience, consistency and persuasive force; the specific-consent restriction is unpersuasive for the statutory reasons the Court adopts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II has five votes: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas. Scalia's agreement with the judgment does not support this ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding concerns the established advisory status and reasoning of this letter, not every informal agency interpretation. No unprovided endorsement, internal delegation or changed policy is assumed.

##### III. Regulatory interpretation cannot turn optional agreement terms into a mandatory condition

**Holding:** Auer does not authorize adding a specific-consent requirement for compulsory use to section 553.23(a)(2)'s unambiguous permission to include compatible preservation, use or cashout terms. The responsible agency may interpret its regulation fairly within the text, but may not amend that permission into the additional prohibition asserted here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III has six votes: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Auer requires a responsible agency's fair and considered reading that is not plainly erroneous or inconsistent with its regulation. This decision imposes no universal rulemaking-format prerequisite and does not decide the Secretary's authority to adopt a valid future rule restricting compulsory use.

**Operative remedy or transition:** These grounds sustain the defense judgment on the categorical FLSA claim without ordering an agreement hearing, agency amendment or new award.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chevron is distinguished at the threshold of this letter's supported advisory authority; the Court does not treat every agency statement as binding.
- Skidmore remains an independent basis for persuasive consideration, including where controlling statutory weight is unavailable.
- Auer retains fair, responsible interpretation within regulatory text; it permits no amendment of the unambiguous permission at issue.
- NationsBank and Smiley retain their actual delegated and authoritative administrative constructions; their holdings do not automatically give this advisory response the same legal force.
- Boston & Maine preserves an agency's actual discernible ground and forbids judicial invention of its reasoning or authority.
- FDA and U.S. West retain distinct inquiries into statutory authority, permissible interpretation and independent legal constraints; neither eliminates this statute's implementing grant or makes it unlimited.

For the compensatory-time use holding in Christensen, May 1, 2000, No. 98-1167, see Labor and Employment — Public compensatory time and compulsory paid use.



### Mining-permit control, temporal operation and correction

#### National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior

**Docket or dockets:** No separate Supreme Court docket supplied; D.C. Circuit No. 98-5248; review of 177 F.3d 1.
**Decided:** May 8, 2000.

##### I. The ordinary permit rule requires current control; former control has separate statutory conditions

**Holding:** Section 510(c), 30 U.S.C. §1260(c), ordinarily bars issuance when the schedule or other information shows a currently violating surface coal mining operation currently owned or controlled by the applicant, including actual downstream control through intermediary entities. After ordinary control ends, the separate former-control provision requires a demonstrated pattern of willful violations of the chapter, of such nature and duration, and with such resulting irreparable environmental damage, as to indicate an intent not to comply; mere former ownership or control cannot substitute for those cumulative conditions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Stevens's opinion, joined by all eight other Justices. Nine Justices adopt this holding and its stated limits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The required three-year schedule lists notices, but the separate permit command reaches a current violation shown by that schedule or other information. The schedule period therefore does not supply a general age limit for violations considered. The block continues until the applicant supplies proof that the violation has been corrected or is in the process of being corrected to the satisfaction of the authority having jurisdiction. Completed correction is not invariably required.

##### II. Antecedent dates alone do not bar present eligibility rules addressing current control and a current violation

**Holding:** An otherwise lawful current-control permit condition is not categorically inapplicable merely because both the underlying violation and acquisition of control predate November 2, 1988, when the actual operative predicates are current control, a current violation and present eligibility for prospective operations. This does not authorize a genuinely new disability resting solely on completed conduct, backdate the rule, determine a displaced-clear-guidance claim or resolve the validity of a particular permit application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Stevens's opinion, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices. The common ground is the particular current-condition distinction and Smiley's qualified interpretation rule. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas do not adopt Landgraf's entire general method anew. Stone-Zsela dissents on this component.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The present application date alone cannot validate a past-conduct disability. The older-violation/post-rule-acquisition branch remains permissible at this temporal level. No displaced clear guidance or actual reliance finding is made; existing Smiley qualifications remain.

##### III. Express correction authority remains subject to lawful predicates and required procedures

**Holding:** Section 201(c)(1), 30 U.S.C. §1211(c), supplies authority to suspend, revoke or withhold a permit for failure to comply with the chapter or valid regulations, including correction of an improperly issued permit that failed a lawful eligibility requirement. Section 510(c)'s issuance language does not eliminate that express power, but the correction must rest on valid substantive and temporal predicates and comply with the applicable procedures; no unlimited implied reopening authority or reopening of a particular final adjudication is decided.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Stevens's opinion, joined by all eight other Justices; nine Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The state-primacy requirements retain their independent force. For ordinary non-imminent violations, the applicable sequence includes notice, an abatement opportunity, an opportunity for public hearing, a written finding after the abatement period that the violation remains unabated, and a cessation order stating the steps necessary for expeditious abatement. The separate imminent-danger provisions remain distinct. General correction authority cannot bypass the required state-program procedures.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not newly decide the officer/director, equity, asset-control, general-partner, operating or coal-right presumptions; additional schedule-information demands; section 2462 or whether permit blocking is punitive or remedial; the separate due-process or APA persuasion-burden challenge; a particular permit's factual or procedural validity; or a new replacement rule. Their separate existing dispositions or unresolved status are preserved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the ordinary former-control limit and express correction authority; reverse the categorical two-pre-rule-date bar only for actual current control and a current violation. The existing rule-correction remand must reflect these limits. No particular permit is denied, withdrawn or reopened, and no new liability or evidentiary program is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Landgraf v. USI Film Products supplies the distinction between actual new legal consequences and antecedent facts. Its controlling general framework and the different relevant-activity concurrence retain their respective force; the common current-condition holding does not merge them.
- Bowen v. Georgetown University Hospital, 488 U.S. 204, retains the prohibition on unauthorized retroactive legislative rulemaking. Present regulation of continuing statutory conditions and Smiley's qualified interpretive use do not confer general retrospective power.
- Smiley v. Citibank (South Dakota), N.A. supplies the qualified use of current authoritative interpretation for antecedent transactions without clear prior official guidance. Its reservation for displacement of clear guidance, protection of legitimate reliance in reasoned change, and prohibition on backdating remain intact.
- FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp. requires agency authority to fit the connected enacted scheme. Its tobacco-specific result supplies no universal major-questions rule or new mining exemption.
- Christensen v. Harris County preserves statutory authority, interpretive weight and textual fit as distinct inquiries. It does not convert every current agency interpretation into a binding or temporally valid rule.
- Greenwich Collieries and Allentown Mack retain their particular adjudicatory-burden and review rules. The Court does not import them into the ungranted procedural-rule litigation or decide a new control presumption here.

### Issue exhaustion in Social Security Appeals Council review

#### Sims v. Apfel, 530 U.S. 103 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-9537.
**Decided:** June 5, 2000.

##### The actual Council scheme does not forfeit these omitted issues

**Holding:** A Social Security claimant who requests Appeals Council review and obtains a final decision does not forfeit judicial review of an issue merely by omitting it from that request where no statute or regulation requires issue exhaustion and the actual nonadversarial review scheme supplies no fair notice of such forfeiture. The permitted short request, optional briefing, whole-decision notice and plenary informal review together establish that rule here; it is not a general notice prerequisite for every prudential exhaustion rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court has six direct joins: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg. Each adopts this complete actual-scheme ground; no narrower-ground aggregation is necessary.

**Limits and questions not reached:** ALJ-stage preservation, failure to request Council review, intentional waiver or deliberate sandbagging, materially new evidence and a valid future regulation remain open. No sandbagging is found. This is neither a jurisdictional defect nor a universal exemption for every nonadversarial agency.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Representation by counsel does not alter the actual scheme or its notice. The broader agency-responsibility rationale has four supporters and supplies no separate controlling exemption.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The case returns for ordinary judicial review of the vocational-hypothetical and consultative-examination objections. Any subsequent relief depends on their merits and governing review law. The Court orders neither benefits nor a new administrative hearing, and the separate selective-record decision remains undisturbed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **McCarthy v. Madigan:** Applies statutory primacy and assessment of actual agency design and interests; its damages-only BOP exception is not transplanted.
- **Darby v. Cisneros:** Preserves its APA-specific superior-review rule; it is not a blanket bar to all issue-exhaustion requirements.
- **Shalala v. Illinois Council on Long Term Care, Inc.:** Preserves prescribed channels and meaningful review while distinguishing a claimant who has already completed the required route.

### FOIA interested-claimant communications

#### Department of the Interior v. Klamath Water Users Protective Association, 532 U.S. 1

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1871.
**Decided:** March 5, 2001.

##### Whether interested-claimant exchanges satisfy FOIA Exemption 5 through federal trust representation

**Holding:** Assuming without deciding that an outside adviser can sometimes function within an agency's deliberative process, communications between an agency and an outside claimant advancing its own scarce-resource entitlement against competing claimants are not inter-agency or intra-agency communications under Exemption 5 merely because the agency consults or represents that claimant. The rule reaches both the claimant's submissions and the agency's communication to representatives acting for that claimant in the same exchange.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), unanimous for both propositions.

**Material application:** All seven communications concern tribes pursuing scarce water against competing claimants. The category fails in both directions, including the federal-authored communication to tribal counsel; a governmental sender does not make the interested recipient an internal agency participant.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The general validity and outer reach of an outside-consultant construction are assumed, not decided. A viewpoint or incidental interest alone is not disqualifying; no general rule concerning later transmission of an independently internal paper is adopted. Water entitlements, trust-duty merits and tribal immunity remain outside review.

##### Whether litigation protection can substitute for the agency-communication requirement

**Holding:** Exemption 5 requires both an inter-agency or intra-agency communication and protection from disclosure to a nonagency party in litigation with the agency. Because all seven exchanges fail the first condition, possible deliberative-process or work-product protection cannot justify their withholding under this exemption, and no ruling on those privileges or their waiver is necessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), unanimous for both propositions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Actual deliberative-process or work-product protection and waiver are not reached because the category defect independently resolves Exemption 5.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment rejecting withholding of all seven communications is affirmed. Ordinary FOIA relief includes release of nonexempt and reasonably segregable material, subject only to independently supported and properly preserved other grounds; this decision establishes or revives none.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Landano is applied for the particular-exemption burden and segregable disclosure; its Exemption 7(D) confidentiality rule supplies no Exemption 5 consultant holding.
- Department of Defense v. FLRA is applied for preserving enacted FOIA boundaries despite an independent representational purpose; its privacy balance is not extended to these documents.
- Tax Analysts's general disclosure entitlement and John Doe Agency's respect for enacted exemption requirements remain operative. Neither is overruled or converted into a privilege rule.

### Review of a settled implementation framework

#### Whitman v. American Trucking Associations, Inc. / American Trucking Associations, Inc. v. Whitman, 531 U.S. 457

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 99-1257 and 99-1426.
**Decided:** February 27, 2001.

For this decision of February 27, 2001, Nos. 99-1257 and 99-1426, Environmental Law contains standard-setting costs and implementation requirements; Constitutional Structure contains congressional guidance and the agency-cure question; Administrative Law contains the independent review routes.

##### Whether the settled ozone implementation framework is final and ripe for review

**Holding:** EPA's settled choice of the statutory implementation framework accompanying the final ozone standard is final action reviewable under section 307(b), and the present legal question is ripe. The determination selects the governing implementation obligations and has present legal consequences; tentative guidance, influence or planning expense alone does not satisfy this rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Kennedy), unanimous.

**Material application:** After comment EPA completed its choice of the legal framework governing required state implementation and refused to reconsider it. Those legal consequences, rather than planning expense or persuasive influence alone, satisfy finality. The legal issue needs no further site-specific facts, and the statutory preenforcement channel does not require a sanction, approved plan or existing violation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The earlier proposal remained preliminary. Tentative guidance and unresolved future choices do not receive review under this rule. The authorized reviewability and implementation questions encompass their necessary subsidiary issue under Rule 14.1(a); that question-scope rule does not independently confer section 307 jurisdiction.

**Independent alternative holding:** A court reviewing the concededly final ozone standard under section 307(b) may decide the implementation relationship actually placed in issue by industry's claim that Subpart 2 displaced the general implementation regime and therefore barred revision. That necessary statutory premise supplies an independently sufficient review route; it does not bring every future plan or implementation dispute into final-standard review.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Kennedy, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer adopt this independently sufficient route. Stone-Zsela supports the separate finality route without joining this ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The alternative reaches only the implementation premise necessary to decide the actual challenge to the final standard, not every future plan or hypothetical choice. No plain-error enlargement is invoked.
