**Operative remedy or transition:** Implementation review in No. 99-1257 is sustained on both independent routes. The governing section 307(b) qualifications are preserved in the Environmental Law entry for this case.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bennett v. Spear: applies completed decisionmaking and legal consequences to the binding implementation-framework choice; no universal sanction prerequisite is added.
- Ohio Forestry Association v. Sierra Club: distinguishes the deferred, fact-dependent logging-plan challenge; its limited ripeness holding does not bar this completed legal determination.


### Interpretation of payroll-tax regulations

#### United States v. Cleveland Indians Baseball Co., 532 U.S. 200

For the application of Auer to Treasury's established interpretation of the separate employer-tax rate and annual-base regulations, see Federal Taxation, United States v. Cleveland Indians Baseball Co., No. 00-203, decided April 17, 2001. The seven-Justice ground rests on the lawful regulatory construction, not independent statutory compulsion or categorical binding force for revenue rulings.

### Reviewability of binding agency guidance

#### Appalachian Power Co. v. EPA

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit Nos. 98-1512, 98-1536, 98-1537, 98-1538, 98-1540 and 98-1542; judgment below, 208 F.3d 1015.
**Decided:** May 29, 2001.

##### Whether EPA's settled national monitoring directive is presently reviewable

**Holding:** EPA's completed 1998 Periodic Monitoring Guidance is final agency action reviewable under Clean Air Act section 307(b) because its implementation through the statutory permit-objection mechanism has present legal consequences: an objection prevents permit issuance. A disclaimer does not negate those consequences, and this facial legal challenge is ripe without an individual permit determination; the separate route for permit review does not displace review of the national action.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Expense, influence and expected compliance alone would not establish finality. The holding rests on the actual completed position and statutory permit consequences, not the document's label.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Bennett v. Spear supplies the separate completion and legal-consequence requirements. Whitman v. American Trucking Associations, Inc. applies those requirements to a settled Clean Air Act implementation framework before an individual sanction or plan decision.

For Appalachian Power Co. v. EPA, decided May 29, 2001, D.C. Circuit Nos. 98-1512, 98-1536, 98-1537, 98-1538, 98-1540 and 98-1542, on the monitoring regulation's limits and whole-guidance vacatur, see Environmental Law - Periodic monitoring and substantive amendment by guidance.



### Customs classification letters and judicial interpretive authority

#### United States v. Mead Corp., 533 U.S. 218

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1434.
**Decided:** June 18, 2001.

##### This ruling mechanism does not exercise authority to bind the reviewing court

**Holding:** Controlling Chevron treatment requires congressional conferral, expressly or through the statutory scheme, of authority to make interpretations carrying the force of law and an interpretation issued in exercise of that authority. This transaction-specific, revisable Customs classification letter does not satisfy that requirement; general administrative responsibility, expertise and official institutional endorsement do not themselves confer controlling interpretive force on the mechanism.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts I and II of Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, supply eight direct votes. Scalia does not join; Stone-Zsela's disagreement with the open remand does not qualify his join to these interpretive rules.

**Material application:** The revisable, transaction-specific letters issue from many offices and do not govern unrelated importers as regulations do. Their place in protest and judicial review shows administration rather than delegated interpretive lawmaking. The Solicitor General's representation, cosigned by Treasury's General Counsel, establishes Customs' official position but does not establish congressional delegation to this mechanism.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Formal rulemaking and adjudication are evidence of delegation, not exclusive prerequisites. A label or independent judicial factfinding does not alone defeat lawful deference. Later ruling-change procedures do not retroactively govern the 1993 action. Other statutory mechanisms and interpretation of an agency's own regulations remain outside the grant.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.: retained for reasonable choices within actual congressionally delegated interpretive authority; its threshold is applied to this Customs mechanism.
- United States v. Haggar Apparel Co.: its actual-delegation and statutory-boundary requirements are applied; its reserved informal-letter issue is resolved for this mechanism, while its regulation and independent-factfinding holdings remain intact.
- Christensen v. Harris County: its distinction between controlling authority and persuasive reasoning is extended to this classification letter without excluding every informal agency interpretation.
- Appalachian Power Co. v. EPA: its operational-legal-consequence reviewability holding and prohibition on interpretive amendment remain distinct; reviewability does not itself establish authority to bind a court's statutory interpretation.

##### Lack of controlling force does not eliminate persuasive consideration

**Holding:** A court independently construing the tariff must consider this ruling letter's reasons according to their actual thoroughness, expertise, logic, consistency, commercial understanding and other persuasive force under Skidmore. No fixed weight or result follows; the Federal Circuit erred by excluding that consideration merely because the letter lacked controlling force.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts I and II of Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, supply eight direct votes. Scalia does not join; Stone-Zsela's disagreement with the open remand does not qualify his join to these interpretive rules.

**Material application:** Customs' dictionary, commercial-usage and ring-binding reasons must be evaluated. Changed treatment may weaken consistency without eliminating all persuasive force; expertise and official endorsement likewise establish neither persuasion nor the answer.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Skidmore consideration is independent judgment, not a lesser mandatory deference or fixed weight. No choice between bound diaries and the residual duty-free category is made.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Christensen v. Harris County: its distinction between controlling authority and persuasive reasoning is extended to this classification letter without excluding every informal agency interpretation.
- Skidmore v. Swift & Co.: applied as an independent persuasive inquiry, with no predetermined weight or mandatory classification outcome.

##### The judgment returns classification under the corrected rules

**Holding:** The Federal Circuit judgment is vacated and the case remanded for independent classification of the imported planners with consideration of the ruling letter's reasons according to their actual persuasive force under Skidmore. The remand establishes neither the bound-diary classification nor the residual duty-free classification, and it directs no particular assessment, refund amount, new agency proceeding or rulemaking.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III has seven direct supporters: Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, including Kennedy. Stone-Zsela and Scalia oppose the open classification remand and would sustain the challenged classification on different grounds.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Neither definitive classification commands a majority. The return rests on exclusion of permissible agency reasoning, not a missing product fact, a general right to first lower-court interpretation or a need for agency action. The distinct noncontrolling textual and deference grounds for sustaining the category do not combine into a Court construction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Federal Circuit must independently classify the planners while considering the actual persuasive force of Customs' reasons. Any assessment or refund depends on that classification and entry calculations; no amount, new hearing, signature search or future rulemaking is required.

### FCC pole-attachment coverage and mandatory rate formulas

#### National Cable & Telecommunications Assn., Inc. v. Gulf Power Co., 534 U.S. 327

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 00-832 and 00-843.
**Decided:** 2002-01-16.

##### Mixed cable-Internet attachments within section 224's general coverage

**Holding:** An attachment by a cable television system that satisfies 47 U.S.C. §224(a)(4) remains within the FCC's authority under §224(b) when it carries Internet service together with cable television. Sections 224(d) and (e) prescribe mandatory rates within their respective terms; they do not exhaust the broader attachment definition or permit the agency to evade an applicable formula.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Kennedy's opinion has the full support of Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplying seven votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Internet-service classification and a particular charge are not decided. Coverage does not permit evasion of either statutory rate formula when its conditions apply.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board (January 25, 1999): applies its separation of congressionally conferred FCC authority from the independent limits on particular implementing rules; its local-competition holdings remain intact.
- United States v. Mead Corp. (June 18, 2001): preserves its actual-delegation requirement; the Court resolves section 224's coverage from its text without adding a deference holding.

##### Qualifying wireless telecommunications equipment under the attachment definitions

**Holding:** Section 224 covers an attachment by a provider offering telecommunications to the public for a fee, without excluding the provider's equipment because it serves wireless transmission. Both the attachment and its owner must satisfy the statutory definitions; the utility's wire-use condition does not impose a separate wire-only restriction on the attached provider's service.

**Proposition-level authority:** All nine Justices join Part II of Kennedy's opinion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The exclusions for railroads, cooperatively organized persons and federal or state ownership remain. Section 224(a)(5)'s incumbent-local-exchange-carrier limit and section 224(c)'s allocation to qualifying state regulation remain operative. The holding covers qualifying poles, ducts, conduits and rights-of-way, not every separate wireless tower or property.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both categorical exclusions are reversed and remanded. Mixed-service reversal has seven votes; wireless reversal is unanimous. Properly remaining classification and rate-method objections must be resolved under the applicable statutory commands.

### Federal electric-transmission authority and the scope of corrective orders

#### New York v. FERC / Enron Power Marketing, Inc. v. FERC, 535 U.S. 1

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 00-568 and 00-809.

**Decided:** March 4, 2002.

##### I. The transmission grant includes unbundled interstate service for retail purchasers

**Holding:** The Federal Power Act's separate grant over transmission of electric energy in interstate commerce includes independently supplied interstate transmission serving retail purchases. The wholesale limitation governs the separate sales grant; it does not narrow the transmission grant, and FERC may regulate the covered transmission's rates, terms and conditions without ordering a utility to begin retail wheeling.

**Proposition-level authority:** All nine Justices join Part I of Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court. That direct unanimous coalition controls the category and the following limits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Independent interstate transmission already supplied voluntarily or under state direction is distinct from compelled retail wheeling. State siting, local reliability and retail-service functions remain. No particular tariff or facility classification is adjudicated, and no general deference rule is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm No. 00-568 unanimously; the reviewed transmission requirements remain effective within their statutory boundaries.

**Limits and statutory qualifications:** Section 201(b)(1) retains state retail-sale authority and, except as the Act specifically provides otherwise, excludes facilities used for generation, local distribution, intrastate-only transmission or transmission of energy consumed wholly by the transmitter. Its preservation of lawful state authority over hydroelectric exports remains. Section 201(b)(2) applies sections 210, 211 and 212 and their enforcement to the entities those provisions cover; compliance with a section 210 or 211 order does not otherwise subject an entity to general FPA jurisdiction. Section 201(f)'s exclusion of federal, state and local governments, their agencies, authorities and instrumentalities, corporations wholly owned directly or indirectly by one or more of them, and their officials acting in duty remains unless a provision specifically refers to them.

Section 212(g) prohibits orders inconsistent with state retail-marketing-area law. Section 212(h) prohibits an order conditioned on, or requiring, direct transmission to an ultimate consumer. Its separate rule for transmission to or for a resale entity permits the covered exception only when both conditions hold: the entity is a federal power marketing agency, TVA, a State or political subdivision or its agency, a corporation or association that has ever received a Rural Electrification Administration loan to provide electric service under the Rural Electrification Act of 1936, a person with a state- or local-law public electric-service obligation arising independently of contract alone, or a corporation or association wholly owned directly or indirectly by one or more of those listed entities; and it either served that consumer on October 24, 1992 or would deliver all the energy through transmission or distribution facilities it owns or controls. State and local authority over direct-to-consumer transmission remains preserved. Section 212(e) makes use of sections 210, 211, 213 or 212 unnecessary as a substitute for other lawful authority and, except as those sections provide, prevents them from limiting other Commission authority; it does not eliminate the retail-wheeling restrictions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board: applied to distinguish an affirmative federal statutory assignment from the independent constraints on its exercise and the functions Congress preserves for States.
- National Cable & Telecommunications Assn. v. Gulf Power Co.: applied to separate coverage from the validity of a particular regulatory method; its actual formula and classification limits remain.
- United States v. Mead Corp.: its actual-delegation requirement remains; independent statutory construction resolves the transmission category without a new general deference holding.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co.: its prohibition on replacing an enacted mandatory obligation remains. This order's sufficient remedy does not dispense with section 206's triggered duty.
- Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala: applied to considered administration within statutory commands and to the requirement of actual agency reasons; counsel's litigation assertions receive no substitute status.
- Wisconsin Department of Health and Family Services v. Blumer: applied to distinguish permission to use a method from the independent circumstances in which the statute commands action; no general exemption from a corrective duty follows.

##### II. This proceeding did not compel Enron's additional bundled-service remedy

**Holding:** FERC could remedy the wholesale transmission-access discrimination established in this proceeding through the adopted open-access requirements while declining compulsory unbundling of bundled retail service on its independently sufficient determination that this additional remedy was unnecessary. Section 206 does require a lawful prospective determination and order after a hearing and finding that a jurisdictional rate, charge, classification or affecting rule, regulation, practice or contract is unjust, unreasonable, unduly discriminatory or preferential; the burden to prove that unlawfulness lies on the Commission or complainant, and the agency may not leave established discrimination uncorrected.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Stone-Zsela's opinion has the direct support of Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Those six votes control. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would remand this component for corrected agency analysis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The agency actually gave both a bundled-retail jurisdictional characterization and an independently sufficient unnecessary-remedy determination. The latter sustains this refusal without adoption of a permanent bundled-transmission exemption. Counsel's litigation reasons cannot replace agency reasons. The universal allocation of bundled-service jurisdiction is not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm No. 00-809, 6–3, and deny compulsory additional unbundling in this proceeding without an agency remand. The order supplies no approval of an individual tariff, retail price or facility classification.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board: applied to distinguish an affirmative federal statutory assignment from the independent constraints on its exercise and the functions Congress preserves for States.
- National Cable & Telecommunications Assn. v. Gulf Power Co.: applied to separate coverage from the validity of a particular regulatory method; its actual formula and classification limits remain.
- United States v. Mead Corp.: its actual-delegation requirement remains; independent statutory construction resolves the transmission category without a new general deference holding.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co.: its prohibition on replacing an enacted mandatory obligation remains. This order's sufficient remedy does not dispense with section 206's triggered duty.
- Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala: applied to considered administration within statutory commands and to the requirement of actual agency reasons; counsel's litigation assertions receive no substitute status.
- Wisconsin Department of Health and Family Services v. Blumer: applied to distinguish permission to use a method from the independent circumstances in which the statute commands action; no general exemption from a corrective duty follows.

### Medicaid spousal income and resource protection

#### Wisconsin Department of Health and Family Services v. Blumer, 534 U.S. 473

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-952.
**Decided:** 2002-02-20.

##### Permissible income-first calculation at the community-spouse resource hearing

**Holding:** At the preeligibility hearing under 42 U.S.C. §1396r-5(e)(2)(C), a State may consider the community spouse's expected posteligibility income, including support that may be transferred under subsection (d)(1)(B), before increasing the community spouse resource allowance. The statute permits income first; it does not require nationwide adoption or independently adjudicate every resources-first program.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer, supplies five direct votes for this construction, its statutory qualifications and the reversal.

**Material statutory qualifications:** For the separate posteligibility attribution under (b)(2), a governing nontrust instrument may specify the allocation; otherwise income solely payable to one spouse is available to that spouse, jointly payable income is divided equally, and income shared with others follows the spouse's interest or half of the spouses' unspecified joint interest. Trust income follows the governing Medicaid trust provisions and the trust's allocation, or the parallel payment rules when no specific allocation appears. Nontrust income without an ownership instrument is divided equally. The institutionalized spouse may rebut the nontrust payment and no-instrument presumptions by a preponderance of evidence of different ownership. These rules do not turn a forecast into payment. Under (e)(2)(B), if either spouse establishes that exceptional circumstances causing significant financial duress require community-spouse income above the ordinary minimum monthly maintenance needs allowance, an amount sufficient to supply the necessary additional income must replace that allowance. Under (d)(5), when a court orders the institutionalized spouse to provide monthly income for the community spouse's support, the community spouse monthly income allowance must be at least the ordered amount. Those income-allowance adjustments remain distinct from (e)(2)(C)'s resource-increase command; none substitutes another provision's trigger or establishes that its conditions are met here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A forecast neither transfers income nor commands a transfer. Section 1396r-5(d)(1)(B) permits a posteligibility deduction only to the extent income is actually made available to or for the community spouse. Once inadequacy is shown under the lawful calculation, the resource increase is required. No extra consent, escrow or guarantee condition is imposed; no later payment, eligibility date or benefit amount is found.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment mandating resources first is reversed 5–4. Remaining calculations must use supported income and resources and respect the distinct attribution and actual-payment requirements.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala, June 7, 1993: considered administration must fit the enactment, and a change of interpretation alone does not establish invalidity.

##### Persuasive weight rather than controlling force of the agency materials

**Holding:** The agency materials here receive the weight their reasoning earns rather than automatic controlling force. The Court independently concludes that income first is permissible; no new exception to, or abolition of, lawful delegated interpretive authority is adopted.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer: five Justices.

**Material application:** The 1993 instruction required income first, the March 1994 clarification permitted income first or another reasonable interpretation pending a final rule, and the September 2001 publication remained proposed. They are not a single binding final command.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Mead Corp., June 18, 2001: congressional delegation and exercise of interpretive authority remain prerequisites for controlling statutory force; these materials are considered for persuasion.
- Christensen v. Harris County, May 1, 2000: an advisory interpretation's authority and persuasive quality remain distinct; optional conduct is not converted into a compulsory transfer.
- Skidmore v. Swift & Co., 323 U.S. 134: the actual reasoning and experience receive the persuasive weight they earn.
- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837: its role for lawful delegated choices remains; the independent construction here creates no broader revision.

### Pending grievances and review of prior federal employee discipline

#### United States Postal Service v. Gregory, 534 U.S. 1

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-758.

**Decided:** November 13, 2001.

##### Whether a pending grievance excludes earlier discipline from adverse-action review

**Holding:** When reviewing a removal or other serious adverse action, the agency and the Board may consider earlier discipline while a contractual grievance remains pending, provided the independent review affords a meaningful opportunity to challenge the predicate and its material use. The Board's authority over the later adverse action includes incidental examination of relevant earlier discipline even when that minor action was not itself directly appealable to the Board.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; eight Justices. Ginsburg concurs in the judgment on separate grounds.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not decide the actual statutory or constitutional adequacy of Bolling's review formula. Seven Justices—Breyer, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter—adopt nonpreservation as the reason for leaving that question open; Thomas does not join that rationale.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The categorical exclusion judgment is vacated. A completed arbitral expungement must receive its legally required effect under the agreement and available procedures; the reviewing court must examine the warning's material role and the consequences of its late presentation and return the penalty to the Board where necessary. The own-motion reopening power is discretionary. Neither reopening, restoration of removal, waiver nor a replacement penalty is automatically ordered.

##### Judicial review does not dilute the agency proof burden

**Holding:** Under §7703(c), a court reviews the Board's administrative choice for arbitrary or capricious action, abuse of discretion, legal error, required procedures and substantial evidence; it may not replace a permissible review policy with its preferred categorical rule. That standard does not diminish the agency's own statutory proof obligation or make agency expertise controlling on statutory meaning.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; eight Justices. Ginsburg concurs in the judgment on separate grounds.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Darby v. Cisneros (June 21, 1993): applied by analogy against an extra procedural prerequisite; its APA provision is not directly imposed on this personnel statute.
- Citizens to Preserve Overton Park v. Volpe, 401 U.S. 402 (1971), and Motor Vehicle Manufacturers Assn. v. State Farm, 463 U.S. 29 (1983): applied to the bounds of judicial review of administrative judgment.
- Director, OWCP v. Greenwich Collieries (June 20, 1994): applied to keep proof burdens distinct from reviewing deference.
- Brown v. Gardner (December 12, 1994), Christensen v. Harris County (May 1, 2000), and United States v. Mead Corp. (June 18, 2001): preserved at their actual limits on administrative interpretation; no general conclusive-deference rule is created.
- United States v. Chemical Foundation, 272 U.S. 1 (1926): regularity does not establish that a particular predicate or collateral-review formula is valid.

### Disability duration and initial entitlement

#### Barnhart v. Walton, 535 U.S. 212

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-1937.
**Decided:** March 27, 2002.

##### The agency may require twelve months of disabling inability

**Holding:** Under the twelve-month branch of the adult disability definitions in 42 U.S.C. §§423(d)(1)(A) and 1382c(a)(3)(A), the agency may require that the impairment cause inability to engage in substantial gainful activity lasting, or expected to last, continuously for at least twelve months. The expected-death branch remains distinct; the Court sustains a permissible delegated construction rather than declaring it the only statutory implementation Congress allows.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice Souter's Opinion of the Court, Part I, is joined by all eight other Justices. All nine directly adopt the formal delegated-regulation ground, the functional-duration rule and its temporal qualification. Part II's additional contextual support is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; Scalia does not join that additional ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Actual delegation under §405(a), incorporated into SSI by §1383(d)(1), and its exercise in binding regulations supply authority; longevity or expertise alone does not. The final July 2000 rule took effect August 10, 2000. Its authoritative interpretation may be used for antecedent statutory events without backdating a new legislative condition where clear contrary prior official guidance is absent. The sustained duration approach and 1995 proposal establish continuity here; no contrary assurance to Walton is shown. Title II's ordinary waiting condition is not imported into SSI.

##### Actual work before a favorable disability finding may defeat the initial duration claim

**Holding:** When work demonstrating substantial gainful activity resumes within twelve months after onset of the disabling impairment and before notice of a determination or decision finding disability, the agency may use that known event to reject an initial claim based on an earlier expectation of twelve-month inability. Section 422(c)'s trial-work protection presupposes qualifying Title II entitlement and does not independently establish a missing initial duration condition.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Souter's opinion is joined by all eight other Justices. All nine directly adopt the permissible timing construction, the qualifications below, and the disposition of Walton's two legal theories.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A claimant returning to work more than twelve months after onset but before adjudication may qualify for trial-work protection if actual duration and the other entitlement conditions are satisfied; passage of twelve months alone does not cure disabling inability that ended earlier. A claimant returning within twelve months after onset, but after notice finding disability, may qualify even when that notice precedes the benefit-award notice. A disability finding, its notice, an award, payment and statutory entitlement are distinct; neither qualification requires prior payment. The separate exclusion for work during a required waiting period remains separate, and the pre-application exclusion concerns months before application, not permanent disqualification for every earlier work episode.

Trial-work services are distinct from substantial gainful activity. The operative nine service months occur within sixty consecutive months and need not themselves be consecutive. New medical or other evidence independent of protected trial work may establish cessation under the governing rules before nine months; a repeated trial-work period is not created within the same period of disability or entitlement. Ordinary Title II insured status, application, age and the five-month waiting condition, including the qualifying prior-entitlement or cessation route within the statutory sixty-month window, remain separate conditions. The Title II trial-work scheme is not transferred to SSI. Statutory blindness, the separate child definition, genuinely unsuccessful work attempts and distinct reopening or reliance questions concerning a claimant with an existing favorable determination are outside the presented adult eligibility dispute.

**Material application:** Walton's established substantial gainful activity resumed in October 1995 before a favorable finding notice. The later closed-period award was superseded on administrative review; the established work finding is not reopened.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The challenged legal rulings are reversed and remanded to implement denial on the reviewed duration and trial-work eligibility grounds.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.:** Applied to a permissible choice within the agency's actual statutory assignment; the Court does not require its own sole preferred construction.
- **United States v. Mead Corp.:** Applied through identified statutory delegation and actual exercise in binding implementing regulations; complexity, expertise or notice and comment alone does not establish authority.
- **Smiley v. Citibank (South Dakota), N.A.:** Applied to qualified interpretive use for antecedent events without backdating a new legislative condition or disregarding clear contrary prior official guidance.
- **Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala:** Preserves reasoned administrative choice within the assigned statutory scheme.
- **Christensen v. Harris County:** Preserves the distinction between advice and delegated lawmaking; no blanket controlling status is conferred on every Social Security Ruling.
- **Auer v. Robbins:** No extension is required; statutory authority and the actual regulations supply the ground.
- **Wisconsin Department of Health and Family Services v. Blumer, Barnhart v. Sigmon Coal Co., Edelman v. Lynchburg College and Ragsdale v. Wolverine World Wide, Inc.:** Their distinct limits on agency authority and statutory consequences remain. Independent construction in another statute does not eliminate lawful delegated choice here.

### Telecommunications cost methodology and necessary network-combination assistance

#### Verizon Communications Inc. v. FCC, 535 U.S. 467

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 00-511, 00-555, 00-587, 00-590 and 00-602.
**Decided:** May 13, 2002.

For Verizon Communications, May 13, 2002, Nos. 00-511, 00-555, 00-587, 00-590 and 00-602, on the facial confiscation objection and preserved actual-rate review, see Property and Economic Rights.

##### Whether section 252(d)(1) requires historical-cost reimbursement

**Holding:** Section 252(d)(1) permits the FCC to adopt a reasonable forward-looking economic measure of the cost of providing an interconnection or network element; it does not require reimbursement of the incumbent's embedded investment or every historical expenditure. Rates must remain just, reasonable, cost-based and nondiscriminatory, with cost determined without reference to a rate-of-return or other rate-based proceeding; reasonable profit is permitted rather than independently guaranteed.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The statutory exclusion of rate-based proceedings does not forbid every consideration of historical expenditure under every lawful method. National implementation leaves the assigned state rate determinations intact.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** AT&T's allocation of implementing authority is applied; Mead and Chevron require actual delegation and a reasonable choice within it. Their statutory-boundary requirements remain unchanged.

##### Whether the efficient-technology and existing-wire-center methodology is lawful

**Holding:** Section 51.505(b)(1)'s use of efficient currently available technology and the lowest-cost network configuration, retaining the incumbent's existing wire-center locations, reasonably implements §252(d)(1). The rule is restored with its incremental-cost and reasonable common-cost components, forward-looking capital cost and economic depreciation, element-specific stand-alone-cost ceiling, aggregate common-cost allocation requirement, excluded-cost categories and cost-study protections; its validity does not establish the lawfulness of every resulting charge.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight direct votes. Breyer dissents from this component.

**Material application:** An element's incremental and allocated common costs cannot exceed the efficient firm's forward-looking stand-alone cost for that element, including corporate costs. Aggregate allocation must equal network-wide forward-looking common costs excluding retail costs. Embedded, retail and opportunity costs and subsidizing revenues are excluded. Incumbents must support offered rates with compliant cost studies. Rates outside proxy ranges or above ceilings require full and fair application in the prescribed proceeding, with notice, comment opportunity and a reviewable written record including studies considered.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No individual rate is approved and no use of TELRIC under other statutes is ordered.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The invalidation of §51.505(b)(1) is reversed and that methodology restored with its statutory and regulatory conditions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** AT&T's previously open methodology issue is resolved without changing its allocation of state functions. Hope and Duquesne support methodological latitude and preserve rate-result review. Mead, Chevron and Barnhart are applied to this explained exercise of delegated authority.

##### When an incumbent may be required to perform combination functions

**Holding:** Sections 251(c)(3) and 252 permit the compensated connecting functions required by §51.315(c) and (d) when the requester is not reasonably able to combine lawfully obtainable elements in the actual network setting and incumbent assistance is necessary to make access usable. This construction of the regulation together with the contemporaneous order applies to both incumbent-element combinations and connections to requester-held elements; it imposes no independent duty on an able entrant to perform the work and creates no own-facilities prerequisite, numerical cost threshold or new entrant-ability proof burden.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part IV-A, unanimous.

**Material application:** The reasonable-ability limit comes from reading the rule with the original order, not an exclusive condition printed in paragraphs (c) and (d). Actual network facilities and information matter; theoretical ability elsewhere is insufficient. A requester identifies the elements sought, while the incumbent must cooperate when missing network information prevents complete specification during negotiations or arbitration. That cooperation creates no entitlement to unidentified elements.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Lawful element entitlement remains independently subject to necessity and impairment. The vacated element list is not restored. Existing-combination protection, voluntary arrangements and just, reasonable, nondiscriminatory compensation remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** AT&T's nonexclusive task allocation and independent element-selection limits are applied; the particular assistance duty left open there is sustained. Mead, Barnhart, Gulf Power and New York v. FERC require actual authority and the agency's own applicable explanation, both present here.

##### Which conditions and proof burdens govern each combination rule

**Holding:** Paragraph (c) requires both technical feasibility and no impairment of other carriers' unbundled-element access or interconnection, including for nonordinary combinations; paragraph (d) independently requires technical feasibility for connections to requester-held elements, without importing (c)(2)'s separate condition. Under (e) the incumbent proves infeasibility to the state commission for denials under (c)(1) or (d), and under (f) it separately proves (c)(2) impairment to that commission; the clear-and-convincing requirement is confined to the described specific and significant reliability/security objection to requested-point access or interconnection, including the same kind of combination objection through the order's cross-reference.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part IV-B, unanimous.

**Material application:** The operative conjunction in paragraph (c) controls despite the explanatory disjunction. Technical feasibility concerns engineering and operations: novelty, modifications, recoverable costs, and billing or accounting adjustments alone do not establish infeasibility. Lack of any possible site expansion may establish infeasibility; where expansion is possible, the requester pays necessary costs. Successful connections using substantially similar facilities provide substantial, not conclusive, evidence of feasibility and supported quality. Clear and convincing proof applies only to the identified specific and significant reliability/security objection, including its contextual application through the combination cross-reference; it is not a universal burden for feasibility, other-carrier impairment or requester ability.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Physical and virtual collocation, §251(f) exemptions, suspensions and modifications, and carrier network-management and performance responsibility retain their own rules. Paragraph (d) does not lose statutory nondiscrimination or genuine network protection merely because (c)(2) is not imported.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The invalidation of §51.315(c)–(f) is reversed; the regulations are restored under these distinct conditions and the necessary-assistance construction. Concrete rates, requests, compensation and entitlement require application on their own facts.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Christensen preserves the operative rule's conjunction. AT&T preserves lawful access limits; Gulf Power and New York v. FERC require agency reasons for the actual burdens rather than judicial substitute policy. No earlier holding is overruled.

#### City of Columbus v. Ours Garage & Wrecker Service, Inc., 536 U.S. 424

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-419.
**Decided:** 2002-06-20.

##### A State may delegate its preserved motor-vehicle safety authority

**Holding:** Section 14501(c)(2)(A) preserves a State's ability to authorize political subdivisions to exercise its safety regulatory authority with respect to motor vehicles. A local rule must actually fall within that delegated safety authority; an economic restriction does not qualify merely because the municipality calls it safety regulation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, controls for seven Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The general motor-carrier price, route and service preemption remains effective. The separate statutory protections for vehicle size or weight and hazardous-cargo route controls, and for minimum financial responsibility or insurance, retain their own terms. Section 31141 federal supervision and independently established actual federal conflict remain operative.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The categorical exclusion of local regulation is reversed and remanded for examination of Ohio's delegation and the actual safety character of each challenged rule, with remaining preemption grounds preserved. The ordinances are not collectively sustained.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Wisconsin Public Intervenor v. Mortier supplies ordinary state use of authorized subdivisions. Medtronic v. Lohr preserves clause-specific preemption analysis; Geier v. American Honda preserves independently established actual conflict.

## Holdings volume: federal-indian-law

# SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES: HOLDINGS REGISTER

**Last completed October Term:** 2001
**Processed through:** June 28, 2002, after all 127 completed OT2001 Court events.
**Edition:** November 11, 2026

## Federal Indian Law

### State taxation of reservation fee land

#### County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes and Bands of the Yakima Indian Nation, 502 U.S. 251 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-408 and 90-577.
**Decided:** January 14, 1992.

##### Whether state ad valorem taxation may reach reservation land patented in fee under the General Allotment Act

**Holding:** The General Allotment Act's established fee-land rule and the Burke Act proviso authorize state ad valorem taxation of reservation land validly patented in fee under that Act, including qualifying land later held by the Nation. The tax follows the land, but patent source and independently applicable federal protection must be determined parcel by parcel.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas; these eight Justices support the ad valorem holding. Blackmun dissents on this component.

**Material application:** The stipulated record did not establish every parcel's patent source. The Indian Reorganization Act did not automatically restore every existing fee parcel to tax-protected status, and the specific land-tax authority did not create general personal taxing jurisdiction over reservation Indians.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding concerns qualifying General Allotment Act fee land and preserves independently applicable federal protections. It does not rest on alienability alone or authorize a governmental-impact inquiry under Brendale.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is affirmed and the case remanded with modified instructions for parcel-specific patent-source analysis and relief consistent with the two distinct tax holdings.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Goudy is reaffirmed for taxation of qualifying General Allotment Act fee land. Blackfeet supplies the requirement of unmistakably clear congressional authorization. Mancari requires compatible enactments to operate together. Moe's rejection of general personal taxing jurisdiction is preserved. Brendale's fractured analysis of tribal regulation of nonmember land supplies no governmental-impact test for this statutory tax question.

##### Whether permission to tax the land also authorizes a sales excise imposed on an Indian owner

**Holding:** Congressional permission to tax qualifying fee land ad valorem does not unmistakably authorize a tax imposed on an Indian owner's sale of that land. Securing the transactional tax with a lien does not change its legal incidence.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion states this proposition for all nine Justices; all nine Justices support the sales-excise holding.

**Material application:** The annual property levy is triggered by ownership and burdens the realty; the sales excise is triggered by a transaction. A collection lien cannot enlarge Congress's permission by transforming an unauthorized transactional charge into a land tax.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The sales excise remains prohibited, and remand relief must respect that prohibition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Blackfeet requires authorization clear at the level of the tax actually imposed. Capoeman's allotment-context discussion of taxation after fee patent does not supply blanket permission for every tax related to land.

### Kansas criminal jurisdiction

#### Negonsott v. Samuels, 507 U.S. 99 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-5397.

**Decided:** February 24, 1993.

##### Kansas-specific concurrent authority over state offenses

**Holding:** Under 18 U.S.C. §3243, Kansas may prosecute offenses committed by or against Indians on Indian reservations, including trust or restricted allotments, within Kansas to the same extent its courts may prosecute offenses elsewhere in the State under state law, even when the conduct also violates the Indian Major Crimes Act. Federal courts retain their jurisdiction over federally defined offenses; this concurrent authority over conduct does not authorize Kansas courts to adjudicate federal criminal charges.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's unanimous opinion, joined by the other eight Justices, controls the grant, the saving clause and the disposition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes and Bands of the Yakima Indian Nation (January 14, 1992): its requirement of specific congressional authorization is applied at that level; its land-tax holding is neither converted into criminal jurisdiction nor enlarged.
- No earlier decision is overruled. The ordinary federal allocation under the Indian Major Crimes Act remains subject to this express Kansas-specific enactment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Authority rests on this express Kansas enactment, not general state police power. Other States, tribal adjudicatory authority, double jeopardy and successive prosecutions are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of relief from Kansas’s aggravated-battery conviction; the state prosecution falls within §3243.

### State taxation of resident tribal members

#### Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Sac and Fox Nation, 508 U.S. 114 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-259.
**Decided:** May 17, 1993.

##### Member income protection depends on Indian-country residence and source

**Holding:** Absent express congressional authorization, a State may not tax a tribal member’s income earned in Indian country when the member resides there; tribal employment alone does not establish that protection. The residence inquiry includes §1151 reservations, dependent Indian communities and allotments whose Indian titles have not been extinguished, including rights-of-way, rather than only an undiminished formal reservation.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- McClanahan v. Arizona State Tax Commission, 411 U.S. 164: applied with both residence and income-source predicates.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe, 498 U.S. 505: applied against a reservation-label-only geography.
- County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes, January 14, 1992: distinguished; limited land-tax authorization supplies no income-tax authority.

##### The challenged vehicle levies cannot be sustained merely as off-country use charges

**Holding:** For tribal members residing in Indian country, the challenged vehicle excise and registration levies fall within the prohibition on state property taxation absent congressional authorization; collecting the same forbidden charges from later purchasers does not save them. Their application to the established residents must be reconsidered on remand; the Court does not decide a genuinely apportioned off-country road-use charge or every tax connected to a vehicle sale.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Tribal employment or registration alone does not establish protection. Outside-Indian-country self-government claims, genuinely apportioned off-country road-use charges and nonmember taxation are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate in the reviewed respects and remand for actual member residence, income source and the vehicle levies. Qualifying allotments or other Indian country may resolve residence without deciding every reservation boundary; no blanket member exemption or refund amount.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Moe v. Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes, 425 U.S. 463: applied to member vehicle property taxation.
- Washington v. Confederated Tribes of the Colville Indian Reservation, 447 U.S. 134: applied to the substance of the vehicle levies; genuinely tailored off-country use charges remain distinct.

#### Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-771.
**Decided:** June 14, 1995.

For the other questions in Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, decided June 14, 1995, No. 94-771. see Federal Indian Law — State fuel-tax incidence in Indian country (Legal incidence of the reviewed motor-fuels tax).

##### Resident taxation and the surviving treaty promise

**Holding:** Oklahoma may apply its generally applicable income tax to members residing outside Indian country who earn wages from tribal work in Indian country; the Treaty of Dancing Rabbit Creek does not exempt that employee income. Members residing and earning tribal income in Indian country retain the ordinary protection under McClanahan and Sac and Fox.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg’s income portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; five Justices adopt the off-country-resident treaty construction.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The treaty construction rests on the surviving territorial promise, not implied repeal at statehood. Article IV's retained council authority is conditioned on consistency with the federal Constitution, treaties and laws, and expressly permits congressional laws within constitutional Indian-affairs authority. Nonmember wages, nontribal-source income and the independent unpressed Williams infringement theory remain outside review.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- McClanahan v. Arizona State Tax Commission; Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Sac and Fox Nation: retain the residence-and-source protection within Indian country, including statutory Indian country beyond formal reservation labels.
- Mescalero Apache Tribe v. Jones: applies ordinary off-reservation state authority absent contrary federal law.
- Graves v. New York ex rel. O’Keefe: distinguishes employee income taxation from taxation of government as employer.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the bar on the reviewed tribal-retailer-incidence fuel tax; reverse the exemption for the specified off-country-resident tribal wages and remand for ordinary tax law and residence/source predicates. No tax amount, refund, individual residence finding or replacement-tax approval is entered.

### Treaty exclusion and acquired recreation lands

#### South Dakota v. Bourland, 508 U.S. 679 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-2051.
**Decided:** June 14, 1993.

##### Acquisition displaces exclusion-derived regulation

**Holding:** The Flood Control Act and Cheyenne River Act displaced the treaty right of exclusive occupation on the taken former trust lands and the non-Indian hunting-and-fishing regulation derived from that exclusion right. Retained tribal access and personal hunting-and-fishing privileges do not themselves preserve authority to license or regulate non-Indian public users.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Acquisition displaces exclusion-derived regulation portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dion, 476 U.S. 734 — clear congressional choice required, without an invariable express-words requirement.
- Montana, 450 U.S. 544 — exclusion-derived authority and effects of alienation applied.
- Brendale, 492 U.S. 408 — land-specific setting considered without inventing a universal rule from its fracture.
- Menominee, 391 U.S. 404 — protection against casual implied abrogation preserved; particular enactments distinguished.

##### Residual authority remains a distinct inquiry

**Holding:** Loss of treaty-based exclusion does not decide whether a Montana exception or properly established federal authorization permits particular regulation. Remand preserves the exceptions for consensual commercial dealings, contracts, leases or other arrangements with the Tribe or its members, and for conduct threatening or directly affecting tribal political integrity, economic security, health or welfare, within Montana’s limits. General inherent sovereignty alone does not restore the abrogated regulatory power.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Residual authority remains a distinct inquiry portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Taken former trust lands and formerly non-Indian fee lands retain distinct histories. Unreviewed Montana findings and properly established federal authorization remain for consideration; the undeveloped Corps litigating position receives no deference. No reservation-boundary, criminal-jurisdiction or universal exclusive-state-power ruling.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for residual authority and actual superior-federal-rule conflicts without reviving the displaced treaty exclusion. No result on the undeveloped questions is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Montana, 450 U.S. 544 — both consensual-relationship and serious tribal-interest exceptions expressly retained.
- United States v. Wheeler, 435 U.S. 313 — retained internal tribal powers do not themselves establish nonmember regulation after the abrogation found here.

### Reservation diminishment

#### Hagen v. Utah, 510 U.S. 399

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-6281.

**Decided:** 1994-02-23.

##### Congressional diminishment and the jurisdiction asserted at Myton

**Holding:** Congress diminished the Uintah Reservation as to the unallotted lands covered by the opening program: the 1902 operative direction restoring those lands to the public domain remained effective through the 1903–1905 amendments and implementation. Myton's location in that opened area is therefore outside the reservation on this record, and Utah may exercise the territorial criminal jurisdiction asserted over Hagen's offense; opening or fee ownership alone does not establish that result elsewhere.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg. These six Justices adopt the statutory construction, its clear-intent basis and its application to this prosecution. There is a direct majority rationale; no fractured-opinion inference is necessary.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The collateral-estoppel issue arising from Ute Indian Tribe v. Utah was omitted from the petition and expressly disavowed by Hagen; the Court does not decide its merits. This case does not adjudicate private titles, all remaining trust or allotted lands, every tract of the larger Uintah and Ouray territory, unrelated tribal powers, or every sovereign's jurisdiction over every offense. The $70,064.48 payment is not relied upon as a purchase of the surplus land. Later population and state administration do not independently change boundaries.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Solem v. Bartlett, 465 U.S. 463: applies its clear-intent inquiry and unequal weighting of text, contemporaneous context and later treatment; distinguishes its nonoperative public-domain reference from the surviving operative command here.
- Rosebud Sioux Tribe v. Kneip, 430 U.S. 584: applies statutory continuity across a sequence of enactments and preserves the rule that no particular formula or fixed payment is indispensable.
- Seymour v. Superintendent, 368 U.S. 351: preserves the distinction between opening and boundary change and the rule that fee ownership within retained boundaries does not itself remove Indian-country status.
- Mattz v. Arnett, 412 U.S. 481: preserves the need for congressional diminishment beyond mere sale and settlement; this sequence supplies the additional direction.
- DeCoteau v. District County Court, 420 U.S. 425: preserves clear congressional cession as sufficient evidence without making its precise words or payment structure mandatory.
- South Dakota v. Bourland, June 14, 1993: preserves its particular displacement of exclusion-derived regulation and its separate residual-authority inquiry; it does not itself establish reservation boundaries.
- Negonsott v. Samuels, February 24, 1993: preserves its express Kansas-specific criminal authorization; it supplies no independent Utah authorization within Indian country.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Sac and Fox Nation, May 17, 1993: preserves the distinct §1151 categories and the need to determine actual Indian-country status.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Utah Supreme Court is affirmed, 6–3, leaving its reinstatement of Hagen's conviction intact. No adjudication of private titles or general reallocation of regional jurisdiction is ordered.

#### South Dakota v. Yankton Sioux Tribe

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1581.

**Decided:** 1998-01-26.

For the concrete continuing declaration-and-permit controversy, see South Dakota v. Yankton Sioux Tribe, No. 96-1581, decided 1998-01-26, Federal Courts — Mootness and continuing territorial declarations.

##### The cession and purchase diminished the reservation as to the ceded lands

**Holding:** The Act of August 15, 1894 diminished the Yankton Sioux Reservation by removing the ordinary ceded, unallotted lands disposed of under its settler-opening program. The comprehensive surrender and unconditional federal purchase, read with the entire agreement, establish clear congressional intent. Article XVIII's treaty-saving language protects its annuity and associated entitlement assurances without preserving the 1858 boundary over those ceded lands.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The cession and unconditional $600,000 federal purchase control under the whole Act; no mandatory words, demographic threshold or quantified burden is adopted. Genuine unresolved ambiguity favors retained reservation status. Later administration, settlement and demographics remain subordinate evidence and cannot themselves change boundaries.

##### The supported parcel category determines the mandate's limits

**Holding:** The identified waste-site tract falls outside the reservation because it is established as ceded, unallotted land disposed of under this program, not merely because a non-Indian now owns it in fee. The declaration must be corrected on that ground. Total disestablishment and the status or authority associated with other land categories remain undecided.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Allotments, trust parcels, Article VIII agency/school lands, later acquisitions, other §1151 categories and surviving federal or tribal powers retain their own predicates. Fee ownership alone does not end reservation status.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reversed and remanded unanimously to recognize diminishment of the ceded lands, including the established waste-site tract, and address remaining parcel-specific or statutory questions. State primary territorial jurisdiction is not universal sovereign authority. The unappealed tribal regulatory/injunction ruling and EPA waiver stay undisturbed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Hagen is applied for an operative congressional-act inquiry, unequal evidentiary weights and geographic limits. Its surviving public-domain restoration command is distinguished from this Act's cession-and-purchase ground; neither mere opening nor fee ownership suffices.
- Solem is applied to the complete Act and its strongly weighted cession/guaranteed-compensation inference. Its sales-agency setting is distinguished; its tribal ambiguity canon and subordinate treatment of later evidence remain intact.
- DeCoteau's controlling clear-cession principle supports the reading without making its precise words, purchase structure or negotiations mandatory for every case.
- Dion retains the clear-and-plain requirement concerning the particular treaty right affected; no general cancellation of treaty obligations is adopted.
- Rosebud preserves the absence of a mandatory verbal formula. Seymour and Mattz continue to distinguish sale, settlement and fee ownership from congressional diminishment.
- Bourland's separate inquiry into exclusion-derived regulation and residual authority is not converted into a boundary rule. Sac and Fox's distinct Indian-country categories remain intact; Negonsott's Kansas-specific authorization supplies no general territorial rule here.
- Lee is applied to the actual continuing permit/declaration controversy, without treating corrective jurisdiction alone as power to decide merits.

### Reservation cigarette-tax collection

#### Department of Taxation and Finance of New York v. Milhelm Attea & Bros., Inc., 512 U.S. 61

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-377.

**Decided:** 1994-06-13.

##### Federal trader regulation does not displace reasonable collection of valid nonmember taxes

**Holding:** The Indian Trader Statutes do not categorically preempt state requirements reasonably directed to collecting cigarette taxes validly imposed on nonexempt consumers. New York's state-agent, stamping, advance-collection, certification, invoice, recordkeeping and reporting requirements survive this facial challenge insofar as they serve that collection function without taxing protected consumption or displacing the federal trader appointment.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's unanimous Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg adopt the limited federal-statute and collection holding.

**Material application:** Ultimate incidence is on the consumer. Protected purchases are by an enrolled member of an exempt New York nation or tribe within a qualified reservation for personal use or consumption there, not resale. Untaxed wholesale deliveries require a valid exemption certificate, intended distribution to exempt consumers and reservation delivery; the buyer shows its certificate initially and signs later invoices. Consumers separately certify exemption initially and show identity on later purchases. Wholesalers keep buyer-specific untaxed-sale records and report monthly. These duties do not authorize taxing protected consumption or overcoming tribal immunity.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No general state power to regulate reservation commerce, revoke a federal trader appointment, tax exempt consumption or sue an immune tribe is recognized. The Seneca treaty contention was not addressed below and is not decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Warren Trading Post Co. v. Arizona Tax Commission, 380 U.S. 685: preserves its protection against the particular direct tax on federally regulated reservation trade; its broader language does not create universal collection immunity.
- Moe v. Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes, 425 U.S. 463: applies valid nonmember taxation and reasonable collection assistance while preserving exempt member purchases.
- Washington v. Confederated Tribes of the Colville Indian Reservation, 447 U.S. 134: applies the distinction between taxable purchases and protected tribal trade, including reasonable documentation burdens.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe, 498 U.S. 505: preserves valid nonmember tax obligations and reasonable collection tools without overcoming the tribe's immunity from suit.
- White Mountain Apache Tribe v. Bracker, 448 U.S. 136: applies attention to the actual federal, tribal and state regulatory setting rather than a universal territorial or licensing rule.

##### Probable-demand limits and prior approval survive only at facial scope

**Holding:** New York may limit unstamped deliveries to probable demand by qualifying exempt purchasers and require advance approval as reasonably necessary collection devices, provided exempt purchases remain adequately available and the requirements remain connected to the valid nonmember tax. The challenged scheme survives the present facial objections because no inadequate implemented quota or denied exempt supply is established; stamped sales have no corresponding numerical ceiling, and unreasonable applications remain subject to challenge.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's unanimous Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg adopt the qualified quota-and-approval holding and reversal of the facial injunction.

**Material application:** A tribe may enter a regulatory agreement for tribal approval. Without an agreement, the Department calculates probable demand using submitted evidence where the tribe regulates, licenses or controls distribution, and otherwise enrollment and statewide consumption. Trade territory uses consultation with a regulating tribe and otherwise available Department information. Valid orders and quantities reasonably related to exempt demand govern approval through monthly coupons. Withholding approval from past or current violators and canceling noncompliant certificates remain tied to lawful collection, not destruction of the exempt channel. No particular quota is approved and stamped supply has no numerical ceiling.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Moe v. Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes, 425 U.S. 463: extends reasonable collection assistance to a qualified supply-control mechanism directed to valid taxes.
- Washington v. Confederated Tribes of the Colville Indian Reservation, 447 U.S. 134: applies the distinction between collection of nonexempt taxes and preservation of protected consumption.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe, 498 U.S. 505: leaves alternative lawful collection methods available while preserving sovereign immunity.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Sac and Fox Nation, May 17, 1993: preserves its residence, source and incidence distinctions; it supplies no exemption for every wholesale sale to a reservation retailer.
- County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes, January 14, 1992: preserves the exact authorization and legal-incidence analysis for its distinct land taxes; collection labels do not authorize prohibited taxes.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The New York Court of Appeals is reversed on both grounds supporting its facial judgment and the case remanded. The facial injunction cannot rest on categorical trader preemption or the rejected facial excessive-burden ground. Concrete challenges to inadequate exempt supply, arbitrary approval or allocation, and excessive compliance burdens remain available; no particular quota, recovery from a tribe or treaty claim is adjudicated.

### State fuel-tax incidence in Indian country

#### Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-771.
**Decided:** June 14, 1995.

For the other questions in Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, decided June 14, 1995, No. 94-771. see Federal Indian Law — State taxation of resident tribal members (Resident taxation and the surviving treaty promise).

##### Legal incidence of the reviewed motor-fuels tax

**Holding:** Without clear congressional authorization, Oklahoma cannot enforce a tax whose legal incidence falls on the Tribe or tribal members for Indian-country sales. Under the reviewed statute’s payment, remittance and credit arrangement, incidence falls on the tribal retailer, so the fuel tax is barred in that application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight other Justices; nine Justices support the fuel-incidence rule and application.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Retailer incidence is the supported construction of payment, remittance and credits, not an express statutory declaration. Hayden-Cartwright authorization was not raised below or in the petition and is not decided. A lawfully shifted non-Indian-incidence tax still requires separate preemption and collection analysis; proposed bills do not establish an enacted replacement.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Moe v. Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes; Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe: distinguish immune tribal incidence from valid nonmember taxation and collection.
- County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes: applies tax-specific congressional authorization, without transferring one tax’s permission to another.
- Department of Taxation v. Milhelm Attea & Bros.: preserves reasonable controls for valid nonmember taxes; supplies no tribal-incidence tax authority.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the bar on the reviewed tribal-retailer-incidence fuel tax; reverse the exemption for the specified off-country-resident tribal wages and remand for ordinary tax law and residence/source predicates. No tax amount, refund, individual residence finding or replacement-tax approval is entered.

### IGRA negotiation and enforcement

For Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44, March 27, 1996, No. 94-12, see Federal Courts — IGRA's binding good-faith adjudication, the connected prospective-officer route and its complete statutory sequence, and the fractured direct-State judgment with a separately majority-supported clear-statement proposition.

### Nonmember public-highway claims

#### Strate v. A-1 Contractors

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1872.
**Decided:** 1997-04-28.

##### This authorized public highway falls within Montana's nonmember framework

**Holding:** For ordinary public motorists using this lawfully open, state-maintained federal right-of-way, the Tribe lacks the exclusion power that would otherwise govern access; Montana's nonmember framework therefore applies to this road-use tort. That consequence lasts within the authorized public use and neither conveys fee title nor removes every compatible tribal power.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, unanimous in Parts I, III and IV.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The May 8, 1970 grant applies to trust lands only, excludes non-trust property, and acknowledges $5,546.50 plus other good consideration. Tenure is unlimited only while used for the specified highway purpose and remains subject to valid existing rights or adverse claims. The United States grantor may terminate all or part after thirty days' written notice and failure to correct a breach of grant terms or applicable regulations, two consecutive years of nonuse for the purpose, abandonment, or failure to file the completion affidavit when construction ends. Indian owners, lessees, successors and assigns retain reasonably necessary crossings, constructed and maintained at their risk with responsibility for resulting right-of-way damage. The stated conditions bind and benefit the grantee's successors. No termination trigger is established on this record. The header's zero tribal acres and 87.48 individually owned acres do not identify the collision parcel; missing incorporated maps and a separate consent instrument establish no grant defect.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Montana supplies the nonmember framework; Bourland supplies a particular public-use/exclusion consequence with residual authority preserved. Sac and Fox's Indian-country classification does not settle governing power over this claim.

##### A tribal forum requires an adequate source of governing power

**Holding:** Subject to controlling treaties or statutes and Montana's two exceptions, tribal adjudicatory authority over nonmembers does not exceed the corresponding substantive governing authority. A tribal court cannot compel these defendants to litigate this highway claim solely by proposing to apply another sovereign's law; completed exhaustion supplies no independent jurisdictional grant.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, joined by all seven other Associates; eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela does not join this proposition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** National Farmers Union and Iowa Mutual supply review and initial tribal examination, not unlimited ultimate authority. Williams's resident-member trading relationship differs. Merrion preserves sovereign taxation distinct from private exclusion and rejects surrender by silence.

##### The subcontract does not bring this ordinary highway claim within the consensual exception

**Holding:** Montana's first exception requires a meaningful relation between the asserted authority and consensual commercial dealings, contracts, leases or similar arrangements with the Tribe or its members. Even assuming Stockert's trip served A-1's tribal community-center subcontract and Fredericks resided on the reservation, this non-Indian motorist's ordinary highway-negligence claim lacks that relation; contracting with a tribal enterprise does not submit a contractor to every reservation claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, unanimous in Parts I, III and IV.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Express forum consent and contractual privity are not universally necessary for the exception. Injury to the Tribe, a member, the project or a participant in the consensual activity is reserved; the asserted extra-record forum clause supplies no ground.

##### The supported interests do not satisfy the self-government exception

**Holding:** Montana's second exception preserves authority over non-Indian conduct threatening or directly affecting tribal political integrity, economic security, health or welfare. The serious individual injury, family relationships, assumed residence and ordinary road-safety interests shown here do not establish the threat or direct tribal effect necessary to bring this nonmember highway claim within that exception.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, unanimous in Parts I, III and IV.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not deny the injury's real familial or community consequences, but they do not establish the required direct tribal effect. A possible state forum is not the independent reason tribal power is absent; no negligence or limitations result is decided.

##### The jurisdictional judgment does not itself establish every predicate for an injunction

**Holding:** Affirmance requires federal relief consistent with the absence of tribal jurisdiction over the reviewed claim, but does not itself issue an injunction or establish entitlement to every coercive remedy. On remand the federal court must respect the actual claims and lawful parties, immunity limits and ordinary equitable requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, joined by all seven other Associates; eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela does not join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The jurisdiction-only declaratory consent and resolution do not waive immunity for every claim or remedy.
