The sons' consortium claims, dismissed insurer, personal jurisdiction, tribal/internal roads, member conduct, express consent, superior federal authorization and distinct police/criminal powers remain outside decision.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm rejection of jurisdiction over Fredericks's reviewed claim unanimously, without further exhaustion or factfinding on the assumed work-trip and residence facts. Eight Associates require relief fitted to lawful parties, immunity and equity; no injunction automatically issues.

### Tribal property and prospective officer relief

#### Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho

For prospective enforcement of disputed federal tribal property rights against connected state officers, subject to sovereign-title and retrospective-relief limits, see Federal Courts, “Prospective officer suits and disputed tribal property rights,” Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, No. 94-1474, decided June 23, 1997.

### Dependent Indian communities

#### Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1577.

**Decided:** 1998-02-25.

##### Dependent-community status requires a federal land relationship

**Holding:** A dependent Indian community under 18 U.S.C. §1151(b) requires land set aside by the Federal Government for Indian use and federal superintendence over that community’s land relationship. Tribal recognition, Indian ownership and federal assistance to residents do not independently supply those territorial predicates.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Neither federal fee title, a reservation label nor a prescribed administrative form is essential. The federal set-aside and supervision predicates cannot be replaced by balancing cohesion, services and political dependence.

##### ANCSA removed both predicates from these lands

**Holding:** These former-reservation lands, conveyed in unrestricted fee after ANCSA expressly revoked the reservation and without continuing federal supervision of their use or disposition, are not a dependent Indian community. The Tribe’s asserted territorial authority to tax this nonmember construction consequently fails.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Material application:** ANCSA expressly revoked this reservation and authorized unrestricted fee conveyances; the corporations’ election and later transfer to the Tribe did not restore a federal set-aside. Section 1636(d) protects unsold, unleased and undeveloped lands from adverse possession, real-property taxes and certain judgments, but does not supply federal control of ordinary transfer or land use.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Other Native parcels, allotments, trust land, tribal identity, benefits, member-based authority and independently established consensual authority remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit’s dependent-community classification is reversed; the asserted territorial basis for this nonmember construction tax fails. No broader title or regional-jurisdiction decree follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sandoval and McGowan supply the federal set-aside and supervision predicates; their allowance for nonfederal fee ownership remains intact.
- Hagen and Yankton Sioux retain the operative-congressional-act inquiry and the distinction between title and territorial status; ANCSA’s express revocation and unrestricted settlement disposition supply the relevant change here.
- Cherokee Nation and Worcester do not convert recognized political dependence into territorial jurisdiction over every tribal-owned parcel.

### Tribal restitution of invalid state taxes

#### Montana v. Crow Tribe of Indians

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1829.
**Decided:** 1998-05-18.

##### 1. Invalidity of these taxes does not establish entitlement to every receipt

**Holding:** An invalid state tax does not automatically entitle a tribe to all sums a nontribal taxpayer paid. The claimant must establish entitlement to the particular fund or remedy; lawful tribal taxing authority and a protected interest in the taxed resource do not alone transfer ownership of the whole state collection.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion is joined in full by all eight Associate Justices. That coalition controls both holdings; the additional partial-restitution framework in Souter's concurrence is not adopted by the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The invalidity judgment does not negate all possible concurrent state taxation. Taxpayer refunds, economic burden, tribal entitlement, actual agreements and prior distributions remain distinct; no compensatory loss measure is adjudged.

##### 2. Equitable analysis must use the actual legal and procedural interests

**Holding:** The remand must distinguish lawful tribal tax coverage, actual payment arrangements, independent claims against the State and funds already distributed. None of the approval dispute, taxpayer refund history or Tribe-taxpayer agreement categorically extinguishes a properly supported independent tribal claim on this record; further relief must follow its lawful basis and proven scope.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The 1988 ruling established approval coverage and distributed registry funds under the 1982 agreement; it did not determine an earlier taxpayer debt. Practical approval and pass-through difficulties remain facts. Properly supported independent partial restitution remains open without a prescribed amount, compulsory amendment or reopening of proof. Souter and O’Connor’s additional affirmative restitution framework is not a Court holding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The compulsory whole-collection award is reversed unanimously and remanded for lawful claimant-specific remedial analysis. Tax invalidity and completed registry distributions remain intact. No amount, revived taxpayer refund claim or compensatory award is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Prior Crow Tribe tax-invalidity judgment:** Preserved; no relitigation of these taxes' invalidity.
- **Cotton Petroleum:** Preserves possible concurrent state and tribal taxation and distinguishes all receipts from an excessive share.
- **United States v. California:** Separates taxpayer refunds, economic burdens and another claimant's independent entitlement.
- **Fulton and Reich:** Preserve adequate, procedurally lawful correction for the appropriate claimant without predetermining this award.
- **Chickasaw Nation:** Its incidence analysis does not create a general right to another taxpayer's entire payments.
- **1988 registry-distribution ruling:** Retains its approval-coverage determination and distribution effect, without becoming a decision of pre-agreement taxpayer liability.

### Direct tribal contract immunity outside reservation land

#### Kiowa Tribe of Oklahoma v. Manufacturing Technologies, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1037.
**Decided:** 1998-05-26.

##### Whether off-reservation commercial character permits a direct contract suit against the Tribe

**Holding:** A federally recognized Indian tribe retains sovereign immunity from a direct suit on its contract, whether the activity is governmental or commercial and whether the contract concerns activity on or off reservation land, unless the tribe clearly waives immunity or Congress unequivocally authorizes the suit. No such waiver or authorization supports this action, so the direct contract claim against the Kiowa Tribe must be dismissed without deciding the debt's merits or resolving the disputed place of execution.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer’s opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter: six direct supporters including Breyer for the complete rule and dismissal.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Signing or defaulting on a note that preserves sovereign rights supplies no clear consent. Sovereign immunity does not extinguish substantive contract obligations or decide individual-agent liability, tort immunity, collateral security, asset execution or an alternative remedy’s actual availability. The disputed execution location is immaterial to this contract-immunity rule.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reversed, 6–3, and remanded to dismiss the direct action against the Tribe on immunity grounds, without adjudicating debt, breach, damages or any separate authorized route.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe, 498 U.S. 505:** Applied for immunity distinct from substantive obligation and its rejection of a commercial-business limitation in the trust-land setting; the present holding extends the direct contract-immunity rule to the off-reservation commercial setting.
- **Santa Clara Pueblo v. Martinez, 436 U.S. 49:** Applied to the requirement of an unequivocal waiver and Congress's authority over the immunity rule.
- **Puyallup Tribe, Inc. v. Department of Game of Washington, 433 U.S. 165:** Applied to the distinction between the tribal sovereign and individual members; jurisdiction over others supplies no automatic authority to bind the Tribe.
- **Mescalero Apache Tribe v. Jones, 411 U.S. 145:** Distinguished as a rule concerning substantive state-law application outside reservation borders, not a direct contract-suit immunity exception.
- **Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, June 14, 1995:** Its territorial and tax-incidence holdings remain intact and create no automatic contract remedy against the Tribe.
- **Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government, February 25, 1998:** Its separation of tribal recognition from Indian-country land status is preserved; loss of a territorial classification does not itself eliminate sovereign status or decide suit immunity.
- **Montana v. Crow Tribe of Indians, May 18, 1998:** Its requirement of an independently grounded restitutionary remedy is preserved; it establishes neither automatic tribal recovery nor an automatic private contract action.

### Nelson Act disposal and tribal reacquisition

#### Cass County v. Leech Lake Band of Chippewa Indians

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-174.

**Decided:** 1998-06-08.

##### Completed Nelson Act disposal and subsequent tribal repurchase

**Holding:** A parcel actually conveyed into the ordinary non-Indian fee estate through the Nelson Act's completed public-sale or homestead-disposal program is subject to county ad valorem land taxation; the Band's later ordinary fee purchase does not itself restore federal tax protection. The county must identify the qualifying statutory chain parcel by parcel, and an independently applicable federal exemption, retained restriction or legally effective trust protection remains controlling.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by all eight other Justices, supplies nine direct votes for this rule and explanation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule does not reach unsold, reserved, restricted or incompletely patented land by analogy. A retained reservation boundary is consistent with a particular congressional land-tax authorization. The Court decides neither boundary diminishment nor general personal or regulatory jurisdiction. The thirteen allotted parcels, transaction excises, income taxes, collection or execution immunity, and particular refund or penalty amounts are not decided. Applicability of the Nonintercourse Act to reacquired land is outside the presented question and remains unresolved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The exemption ruling for the eight Nelson Act sale parcels is reversed and remanded for proof of each qualifying statutory chain and application of independent federal protection. The thirteen allotted parcels are outside review; no refund, interest or penalty amount is fixed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- County of Yakima is applied for its statutory-source, land-tax-incidence and parcel-specific protection rules; its GAA/Burke Act holding is not converted into a general alienability rule or permission for transactional taxes.
- Montana v. Blackfeet Tribe supplies the clear-authorization requirement, satisfied here by the particular completed congressional disposal.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Sac and Fox Nation preserves distinct predicates for member income and vehicle taxation; it supplies no automatic exemption or authorization for this land tax.
- Hagen v. Utah preserves the distinction between fee ownership and congressional reservation diminishment. No boundary changes follow from today's tax holding.


### The reservation of coal does not include this distinct methane gas

#### Amoco Production Co. v. Southern Ute Indian Tribe

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-830; Tenth Circuit en banc judgment, 151 F.3d 1251.
**Decided:** 1999-06-07.

##### The reservation of coal does not include this distinct methane gas

**Holding:** The 1909 and 1910 Coal Lands Acts' reservations of coal do not reserve naturally occurring, chemically distinct methane held in coal by physical adsorption or in its fractures merely because the gas is associated with the coal reservoir. The reserved estate includes coal and its expressly accompanying rights; separate gas ownership does not decide lawful access, injury to coal, compensation or title created by a different gas reservation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer, controls directly.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not determine statutory or lease rights in gas separately reserved by another instrument, methane migrated into non-coal strata, quantitative source allocation, limitations or other common defenses, compensation for trespass to coal seams, actual extraction interference, surface access, tax or collusion theories, facilities claims, or remaining federal trust questions. No damages award or general extraction license follows.

The coal-access provisions continue to operate in their own terms. Under the 1909 Act, entry to prospect for, mine or remove coal requires the patent owner's prior consent or court-determined conditions securing and paying all resulting damage to that owner. The patent owner may mine coal for domestic use on the land before federal disposal of the deposit. Locators, selectors and entrymen retain a hearing on land character; satisfactory final proof of good faith and legal compliance supports an unreserved patent unless the land is then shown chiefly valuable for coal. Under the 1910 Act, a person qualified to acquire federal coal deposits or mining rights may prospect after the Secretary approves security for all resulting crop and improvement damage. One who has acquired the deposit or mining right may occupy the surface as reasonably required for mining and removal upon paying the owner's damage or providing sufficient security in a competent court's damages proceeding. Domestic coal use on the land remains available before federal disposal, and applications challenging a coal classification to obtain an unreserved patent retain prompt consideration. The Court does not convert these provisions into a gas-access code.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Western Nuclear is distinguished because its reservation covered coal and other minerals. Its sovereign-grant canon remains available for genuine uncertainty within the statutory transaction; it does not add a distinct gas reservation after this term's meaning is resolved.
- Leo Sheep supplies fidelity to the estate Congress actually conveyed and reserved; a general land-grant policy cannot enlarge the reservation beyond the enacted arrangement.
- Yankton's transaction-specific approach is consistent with preserving the original reservation's limits and questions concerning other interests; it does not itself decide this mineral-title question.
- Bourland concerns the consequences of a different federal land transaction for regulatory authority. Its distinction between transferred interests and residual rights is preserved, without importing its regulatory result into methane ownership.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The original federal coal reservations, and the Tribe's succession to them, do not by themselves establish title to the distinct coalbed methane at issue. This construction does not reduce the coal estate's independently protected rights or adjudicate a different gas reservation. Ownership, lawful access, extraction injury and compensation remain separate questions. No precedent is overruled, and no general rule governing every mineral or every later-developed use is announced.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reversed and remanded, 8–1. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer. Opposing: Ginsburg. The coal reservations themselves did not vest title to the distinct methane at issue in the United States and then the Tribe. Further proceedings must apply that construction without treating it as a blanket license to extract gas or as a disposition of every pleaded claim.

### Federal courts determine Price-Anderson coverage before imposing tribal exhaustion

#### El Paso Natural Gas Co. v. Neztsosie

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-6; judgment below, 136 F.3d 610.
**Decided:** 1999-05-03.

##### Federal courts determine Price-Anderson coverage before imposing tribal exhaustion

**Holding:** When the invoked Price-Anderson federal proceeding requires deciding whether pleaded claims are covered public-liability actions, the federal court must decide that predicate itself before imposing prudential tribal exhaustion. Covered claims follow the Act's specific federal channel; uncovered claims retain their own jurisdictional, tribal-authority and exhaustion analysis.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule addresses the invoked Price-Anderson federal channel, not every mining case or ordinary tribal-exhaustion question. Uncovered claims retain actual jurisdiction, substantive tribal-authority, proper-party, immunity and equitable conditions; no unexamined treaty right or ultimate claim is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate and remand for federal determination of Price-Anderson coverage; set aside the unappealed enlargement and restore partial injunctions to their preappeal position without a new merits adjudication of their propriety.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- National Farmers Union Insurance Cos. v. Crow Tribe and Iowa Mutual Insurance Co. v. LaPlante retain their ordinary prudential exhaustion rule; it yields to this specific federal coverage task.
- American Railway Express Co. v. Levee preserves alternative grounds for defending a decree but prohibits an unappealed enlargement.
- Strate v. A-1 Contractors requires a substantive source of tribal authority and preserves proper-party, immunity and equitable limits; it does not decide these mining claims by label.
- Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co. keeps jurisdiction and prudential abstention distinct.
- Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band preserves instrument-specific rights; no unexamined treaty right is extinguished here.

For the other questions and complete operative scope in El Paso Natural Gas Co. v. Neztsosie, 1999-05-03, No. 98-6; judgment below, 136 F.3d 610, see Federal Courts.

### The combined removal order did not terminate the reserved use rights

#### Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band of Chippewa Indians

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-1337.
**Decided:** 1999-03-24.

##### The combined removal order did not terminate the reserved use rights

**Holding:** An unauthorized compelled-removal order does not terminate reserved treaty use rights when its revocation and removal commands are inseparable parts of the same purpose and operation. The 1850 order therefore did not extinguish these rights; the Court does not decide the full extent of an independently exercised presidential power under the treaty's pleasure clause.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court, Parts I–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (six Justices).

##### The 1855 land cession did not surrender these independent use rights

**Holding:** A later land cession does not extinguish separately retained treaty use rights unless the agreement, read in its setting and as the tribal parties reasonably understood it, clearly relinquishes those rights. The 1855 agreement did not surrender the Band's retained hunting, fishing and gathering privileges.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court, Parts I–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (six Justices).

##### Statehood did not itself extinguish the treaty rights

**Holding:** Minnesota's admission to the Union did not itself terminate otherwise surviving federal treaty use rights. Those rights can coexist with lawful, necessary nondiscriminatory conservation regulation; the treaty's presidential-pleasure condition does not make state admission the terminating event.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court, Parts I–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (six Justices).

##### The affirmance preserves the rights at their actual scope

**Holding:** Recognition of the surviving rights within the ceded territory remains in force against the presented extinction claims, subject to the applicable treaty and conservation standards. Survival does not itself decide private access, unrelated criminal conduct, numerical allocations or rights beyond the treaty's territory.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court, Parts I–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (six Justices).

**Limits and questions not reached:** Independent presidential revocation under the pleasure clause, particular conservation measures, numerical harvest allocations, private-land access, unrelated criminal conduct and rights beyond the ceded territory remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm recognition of the surviving treaty rights and the reviewed declaratory/injunctive framework, subject to lawful, necessary nondiscriminatory conservation measures.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dion is applied: clear abrogation is required, but no mandatory form of express words is imposed.
- Menominee and Fishing Vessel are applied: independently reserved rights are not extinguished by casual implication.
- Klamath is distinguished: reservation-dependent exclusive rights do not determine the fate of these retained use privileges.
- Bourland is distinguished and preserved: exclusion-derived nonmember regulation and retained personal uses remain different rights.
- Hagen and Yankton retain their instrument-specific inquiry into actual federal displacement; land or ownership change alone does not decide every retained right.
- Race Horse is limited: its broader equal-footing incompatibility premise cannot terminate otherwise surviving rights merely at statehood; its distinct treaty-condition construction is not decided here.
- Winans and Tulee are applied with Fishing Vessel: treaty uses and lawful conservation regulation can coexist.

### Reserved water and compensation judgments

#### Arizona v. California, 530 U.S. 392 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 8, Original.
**Decided:** June 19, 2000.

##### III. Section 22 does not establish the asserted third-party bar from this payment

**Holding:** Payment of this alternative-theory compromise does not itself establish a taking, extinguishment of tribal title or release of dependent reserved-water claims against all persons under section 22 of the Indian Claims Commission Act. The distinct statutory discharge asserted by the State parties therefore does not bar the Fort Yuma claim on this record; differently supported judgments with an actual title or extinguishment predicate remain open for their own analysis.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Stevens's Opinion of the Court, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the disposition of this independent statutory component and adds no ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 22(a) gives a Commission report determining entitlement to recovery, when filed with Congress, the effect of a final Court of Claims judgment and authorizes payment; payment discharges the United States from claims and demands touching the controversy. Section 22(b) separately makes an adverse final determination, made and reported under the Act, a bar to further claims against the United States arising from that matter. The recovery/payment trigger and the adverse-determination trigger are distinct, and both provisions identify claims against the United States.

##### IV. The two submitted settlements are approved on their particular terms

**Holding:** The Court approves the Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation agreements recommended by the Master and permits their incorporation into implementing decree provisions at the terms stated below. Approval rests on these agreements and this record under the Court's existing original-jurisdiction authority; it establishes no general approval test and resolves no additional title or water question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of Stevens's Opinion of the Court, unanimous on both independent settlement approvals.

**Current-force modification:** The supplemental decree of October 10, 2000 gives these approvals operative decree provisions, including the amended aggregate entitlements, California present-perfected-right entries and confined boundary-proviso exception. The approved releases and reservations remain intact; the decree adds no Fort Yuma award and adjudicates none of the reserved title or jurisdictional claims. The original settlement-approval holding remains controlling.

**Material application:** **Fort Mojave terms:** The agreement specifies the reservation boundary near the Hay and Wood Reserve. It permits the lesser of an additional 3,022 acre-feet or enough water for 468 acres. The United States and Tribe may make no further reserved Colorado River water claims for lands within the Hay and Wood Reserve. Competing claims to title and jurisdiction over the bed of the last natural course of the Colorado River within that boundary remain reserved, as do private land claims, title and jurisdiction. Approval does not leave the specified reservation boundary wholly undetermined, but it does not decide those reserved riverbed and private rights.

**Colorado River Reservation terms:** The agreement permits the lesser of an additional 2,100 acre-feet or enough water to irrigate 315 acres. The United States and Tribes may make no further reserved Colorado River water claims for reservation lands in California. The disputed boundary remains unadjudicated in this proceeding, including the competing ambulatory-west-bank and fixed-line theories; competing riverbed-title and jurisdiction claims remain reserved. The agreement requires both the Master's and Court's approval; the Court supplies its approval here on the Master's recommended agreement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No new release, additional water number, private-title adjudication, universal settlement framework or present final Fort Yuma decree follows. The Master shall continue the unresolved Fort Yuma proceedings and provide appropriate implementing decree provisions for the approved agreements, subject to the Court's further action. No objection deadline or final supplemental-decree text is entered by this decision.

For the separate Arizona I forfeiture, Docket 320 preclusion holdings and Fort Yuma return, see Arizona v. California, No. 8, Original, June 19, 2000, under Federal Courts, preclusion in continuing original actions.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Arizona v. California, No. 8, Original, October 10, 2000, conforming implementation and preservation of decree condition (5); see Federal Indian Law.

### Reserved water rights and implementing decrees

#### Arizona v. California, 531 U.S. 1

**Docket or dockets:** No. 8, Original.
**Decided:** October 10, 2000.

##### Implementation of the approved Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation agreements

**Holding:** The implementing decree must conform to the quantities, releases, and limits approved June 19, 2000, while preserving the reserved claims. Clauses A-G satisfy that requirement and amend the existing decrees without creating a new allocation rule or adjudicating the reserved rights.

**Proposition-level authority:** The Court's unsigned supplemental decree, acting under retained original jurisdiction. The instrument publishes no numerical tally or individual joins.

**Material application:** Article II(D)(4) now limits the Colorado River Reservation to the lesser of 719,248 acre-feet of annual mainstream diversions or the water required for irrigation of 107,903 acres and related uses. Its priority dates remain March 3, 1865, November 22, 1873, November 16, 1874 (except as later modified), May 15, 1876, and November 22, 1915, for the corresponding reserved lands. Article II(D)(5) limits Fort Mojave to the lesser of 132,789 acre-feet or the water for 20,544 acres and related uses, with September 19, 1890 and February 2, 1911 priorities for the corresponding lands. The separate California present-perfected-right entries remain distinct: Colorado River, 10,745 acre-feet/1,612 acres with November 22, 1873 priority; 40,241/6,037 with November 16, 1874 priority; and 5,860/879 with May 15, 1876 priority. Fort Mojave's California entry is 16,720 acre-feet/2,587 acres, with September 18, 1890 priority. The different September dates are inherited and preserved; their source or resolution is not adjudicated.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Fort Mojave's approved increment remains the lesser of 3,022 acre-feet or water for 468 acres; the agreement fixes the specified boundary near the Hay and Wood Reserve and bars further reserved Colorado River water claims for those lands by the United States and Tribe. Claims to title and jurisdiction over the bed of the river's last natural course within that boundary, and private land claims, title, and jurisdiction, remain reserved. The Colorado River Reservation's increment remains the lesser of 2,100 acre-feet or water for 315 acres; the United States and Tribes may bring no further reserved Colorado River water claims for California reservation lands. Its disputed boundary, including ambulatory-west-bank and fixed-line theories, and competing riverbed-title and jurisdiction claims remain unadjudicated. Lack of objections enlarges no consent. Fort Yuma receives no additional award; title, cession, boundary, irrigable-acreage, and water-quantity predicates still require adjudication before additional water can be awarded.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The supplemental decree takes effect October 10, 2000. Unamended portions of the 1964, 1979, and 1984 decrees remain operative. The Court retains jurisdiction to effectuate and supplement the decree; no new objection deadline or final Fort Yuma decree is entered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Arizona v. California, June 19, 2000, 530 U.S. 392, is implemented with its approvals, releases, reservations, and separate Fort Yuma holdings intact. The March 9, 1964 decree, 376 U.S. 340, is amended only in the specified general-entitlement provisions; the January 9, 1979 decree, 439 U.S. 419, only in the identified proviso and California entries; and the April 16, 1984 decree, 466 U.S. 144, only to incorporate the specified Fort Mojave adjustment. Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993, supplies the distinction between implementing established entitlements and seeking a new allocation requiring an injury showing.

##### Scope of the exception to the 1979 boundary-adjustment proviso

**Holding:** Clause C removes only the western California boundaries of the Fort Mojave and Colorado River reservations from the concluding boundary-adjustment proviso in the first sentence of introductory condition (5). It neither enlarges the settlement releases nor displaces that condition's remaining shortage, acreage-calculation, use, or priority rules.

**Proposition-level authority:** Clauses C and F of the unsigned supplemental decree; no individual joins or tally are published.

**Material application:** If the Secretary determines under Article II(B)(3) that mainstream water cannot satisfy present perfected rights, the specified rights of the Chemehuevi, Cocopah, Fort Yuma, Colorado River, and Fort Mojave reservations must first be satisfied in full, before other nonmiscellaneous present perfected rights. Those other rights retain priority-date order across state lines; miscellaneous rights 7-21 and 29-80 retain their unaffected priority order. Outside clause C's exception, boundary adjustments continue to use the Master's December 5, 1960 methods for net practicably irrigable acreage and diversion factors of 6.37 acre-feet per acre for Cocopah, 6.67 for Colorado River, 5.97 for Chemehuevi, 6.46 for Fort Mojave, and 6.67 for Fort Yuma. Irrigation measures quantity but does not require agricultural use. Other uses cannot exceed the consumptive use attributable to the specified irrigation diversions and equivalent supplements for that acreage and related uses. No priority is established for unspecified Indian claims.


### Contractual waiver for arbitral confirmation

#### C & L Enterprises, Inc. v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe of Oklahoma, 532 U.S. 411

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-292.
**Decided:** April 30, 2001.

##### Whether the integrated arbitration and judicial-enforcement undertaking clearly waives tribal immunity

**Holding:** This agreement clearly consents to the contemplated state-court confirmation proceeding because it combines binding arbitration, express judicial entry of the award, incorporated enforcement rules and selection of a regime authorizing entry by a competent state court. No prescribed sovereign-immunity formula is necessary, but commercial activity, generic choice of law or arbitration alone does not supply this waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The waiver is confined to the contemplated confirmation action. It creates no otherwise absent federal jurisdiction, execution authority against particular assets or consent to unrelated claims. Signatory authority, former 25 U.S.C. section 81, parity with state-waiver doctrine and delegation of arbitrability are not decided. Refusal to participate in arbitration establishes neither default nor correctness of the award.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand. Immunity does not bar confirmation, but preserved award defenses and ordinary confirmation prerequisites remain; the Court confirms no particular award item and expands no recovery.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kiowa's immunity for commercial and governmental contracts on or off reservation land remains, subject to clear waiver. Santa Clara Pueblo requires unequivocal relinquishment, not mandatory vocabulary; Potawatomi keeps substantive obligation separate from amenability to suit. First Options requires actual assent and instrument-specific scope, without enlarging its delegation holding.

### Tribal taxation of nonmember fee-land transactions

#### Atkinson Trading Co. v. Shirley, 532 U.S. 645

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-454.
**Decided:** May 29, 2001.

##### Whether either Montana exception authorizes the guest occupancy tax on these fee-land transactions

**Holding:** Montana governs a tribal occupancy tax on nonmember guest transactions at a nonmember hotel on non-Indian fee land. Without superior federal authorization, the Tribe must show a qualifying consensual relationship meaningfully connected to the levy or conduct threatening or directly affecting tribal political integrity, economic security, health or welfare; neither exception is established here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court; Kennedy, Souter and Thomas also join it fully. Their additional all-land starting-rule view has no controlling majority.

**Material application:** Commercial dealings, contracts, leases or similar arrangements must connect meaningfully to the tax. General police, fire and medical services do not supply universal consent; a federal trader license is not a tribal tax agreement. The operator's unrelated business contacts fail even if considered. Ordinary revenue needs, services and business presence alone do not establish the second exception's qualifying tribal effect.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court requires neither express tax consent nor privity universally and does not decide whether sufficient operator contacts could ever support a tax imposed on guests. A properly grounded particular-service fee is not adjudicated. No quantified tribal-effect threshold or reduction of every limb of the exception to a single formula is adopted. Reservation boundaries, taxes on members, qualifying tribal leases or transactions, and superior federal authority remain intact; no controlling all-land Montana rule is announced.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Montana v. United States supplies the fee-land framework and distinct exceptions. Merrion v. Jicarilla Apache Tribe preserves sovereign taxation rather than merely ownership rights, but its tribal-land lease setting supplies no unlimited fee-land tax power. Strate v. A-1 Contractors requires a governing source, meaningful nexus and separate tribal-effect analysis; title does not establish disestablishment or replace those inquiries.

##### Whether the absence of taxing authority itself establishes unrestricted injunctive or monetary relief

**Holding:** Relief implementing the no-authority ruling must satisfy the actual claims, lawful parties, immunity and ordinary equitable requirements. Reversal establishes neither an automatic refund cause of action nor every predicate for an injunction, and commerce, location or receipt of services establishes no general waiver of tribal immunity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Tenth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for conforming lawful relief; no automatic refund, unrestricted injunction or new tax arrangement is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Strate keeps sovereign governing power distinct from amenability to suit. C & L Enterprises, Inc. v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe of Oklahoma enforces its particular integrated contractual waiver; no comparable undertaking is present here.



### Prestatehood retention of reservation submerged lands

#### Idaho v. United States, 533 U.S. 262

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-189.
**Decided:** June 18, 2001.

##### Whether Congress retained the unceded Coeur d'Alene submerged estate against Idaho statehood

**Holding:** Federal retention against future state ownership requires both clear inclusion of the submerged lands and clear intent to defeat future state title for an appropriate public purpose. With inclusion and congressional knowledge conceded here, Congress's enacted prestatehood treatment of the defined Coeur d'Alene Reservation as an estate protected pending consensual, compensated acquisition clearly retained its unceded beds and banks; Idaho did not acquire the disputed estate at admission on July 3, 1890.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer; six direct votes for retention and the bounded decree.

**Material application:** The 1886 cession authority concerned land outside the existing reservation and required ratification. The 1888 railroad enactment preserved tribal tenure and conditioned rights on filed and approved survey plats, fixed and paid compensation, and tribal consent. The 1889 authority allowed consensual purchase only of nonagricultural portions chiefly valuable for minerals and timber, on just and equitable terms and subject to ratification. Together these enacted protections retained the identified unceded remainder. The admission disclaimer corroborates that result; neither unratified negotiations, water uses nor executive geography independently establishes it. The Senate's June 1890 vote was not law, and the 1891 ratification confirms rather than retroactively creates the retention.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No title is adjudicated to the ceded northern lake, other aboriginal territory or the excluded Heyburn estate. The unadjudicated aboriginal-title theory and proposed ratification relation-back theory supply no alternative holding. Title does not establish fishing, taxing, environmental or nonmember regulatory jurisdiction; no general reservation exception or retroactive divestiture is recognized.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit's affirmance of the August 14, 1998 decree stands. United States trustee title, the Tribe's beneficial interest and exclusive use, occupancy and quiet enjoyment, the injunction against Idaho's adverse title claim, and denial of its counterclaim apply only within the boundaries described by the March 3, 1891 and August 15, 1894 Acts. Waters and submerged lands claimed by Idaho within Heyburn State Park are expressly excluded. The Tribe's existing costs award and the United States' and Idaho's agreement to bear their own costs remain unchanged. No new boundary, conveyance, damages or general regulatory decree issues.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** United States v. Alaska retains the independent clear-inclusion and clear-title-defeat requirements; its operative interim segregation supports examination of actual legal effect. Shively v. Bowlby supplies territorial retention power for a public purpose. Montana v. United States and Utah Division of State Lands v. United States preserve the state-title presumption and separate timely-intent requirement, without requiring fixed words. Idaho v. Coeur d'Alene Tribe decided immunity rather than this ownership or United States suit. Atkinson Trading Co. v. Shirley concerns nonmember fee-land taxation and supplies no regulatory consequence from title.

### Tribal tort authority over state enforcement searches

#### Nevada v. Hicks

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1994.
**Decided:** June 25, 2001.

##### Whether retained trust land dispenses with the nonmember authority inquiry for these searches

**Holding:** Montana's nonmember framework applies to the claimed civil authority over these state officers' execution of process on Hicks's retained trust allotment for an off-reservation state offense. Land status is material to that inquiry but does not by itself establish adjudicatory authority; the actual source and relationship must be examined.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Scalia's opinion of the Court, unanimous at this case-specific starting point; no unanimous ultimate-authority or immunity conclusion follows.

**Material application:** The first Montana exception concerns a qualifying consensual commercial dealing, contract, lease or other arrangement connected to the authority claimed. The second concerns conduct threatening or directly affecting tribal political integrity, economic security, health or welfare. These are distinct inquiries; land status matters without supplying automatic authority.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding does not decide every private nonmember transaction on retained land or establish universal state exclusivity.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Strate's public-highway setting is distinguished; Montana's source-and-relationship analysis applies here. Bourland preserves its specific displacement and residual authority. Atkinson's fee-land tax holding supplies meaningful-connection discipline without having already decided all retained-land activity.

##### Whether the accepted tribal warrants establish authority over these personal-capacity tort claims

**Holding:** The Tribe lacks civil authority over the personal-capacity tribal tort claims challenging these officers' execution of state process to investigate an off-reservation state offense. Their use of the identified tribal warrants does not establish a qualifying first-exception regulatory relationship for these claims, and the asserted injuries do not establish the qualifying tribal threat or direct effect under the second exception; the approval of tribal tort jurisdiction must be reversed.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts II and IV of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; five direct rationale votes. Stevens, O'Connor and Breyer would require a different Montana and early-defense remand, while Stone-Zsela would sustain the connected tort jurisdiction. Their positions do not expand this coalition.

**Material application:** The officers actually sought and used tribal process; the first state warrant required tribal approval. The first tribal approval limited the search to the exterior and vehicles, while the second approval imposed no modification. This sovereign cooperation does not establish the qualifying regulatory undertaking, and alleged individual injury does not satisfy the separate tribal-threat exception. No universal express-consent-to-suit formula is imposed.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule concerns execution of state process for an off-reservation offense. Express cooperative contracts, superior federal delegation and unrelated private conduct by officers remain outside it. The Court does not determine search legality, warrant excess, damage, causation, personal immunity or the Director's conduct; a motion to drop him is not a completed dismissal.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Montana's two exceptions remain independent. Strate's authority-source and meaningful-nexus requirements apply without equating factual warrant connection with a qualifying regulatory relationship. Personal-capacity pleading under Hafer avoids conflation with sovereign immunity but does not create a competent tribunal.

##### Whether further tribal defense litigation is required after final rejection of authority

**Holding:** The Ninth Circuit's approval of tribal tort jurisdiction and its direction for further tribal exhaustion of the officers' immunity objections are reversed at the scope of Part II. On remand the federal courts must give the no-authority conclusions their proper declaratory and prospective effect, subject to the actual claims, lawful parties, immunity limits and ordinary equitable requirements; this judgment does not itself issue an injunction or award personal immunity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts II and IV of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; five direct rationale votes. Stevens, O'Connor and Breyer would require a different Montana and early-defense remand, while Stone-Zsela would sustain the connected tort jurisdiction. Their positions do not expand this coalition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Eight Justices reject unconditional further tribal exhaustion, but the three supporting an early-defense remand do not join the five's final no-authority rationale. No general merits-first immunity sequence, replacement immunity rule or immunity award follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Federal declaratory or prospective relief must implement the settled authority conclusions with proper parties and applicable immunity and equitable predicates. Neither a further unresolved Montana inquiry nor defense litigation in the unauthorized forum is required. There is no automatic injunction, damages, State-immunity waiver, guaranteed alternate forum, tolling or new filing period.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** National Farmers and Iowa Mutual retain ordinary initial tribal examination where authority may exist, but cannot confer absent authority. Strate supports ending litigation in the unauthorized forum. Neztsosie's source-specific federal priority does not create general removal or automatic relief. Saucier leaves Harlow and Anderson's objective defense and conduct-date inquiry intact; later forum decisions do not establish notice for the 1990-1991 searches.

For the section 1983 forum question in Nevada v. Hicks, No. 99-1994, decided June 25, 2001, see Federal Courts — Federal civil-rights claims in tribal tribunals.

### IGRA reporting parity and substantive wagering taxes

#### Chickasaw Nation v. United States, 534 U.S. 84

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-507.

**Decided:** November 27, 2001.

**Companion or consolidated matters:** The same construction and disposition govern the Chickasaw and Choctaw Nations.

##### Whether IGRA imports chapter 35’s state wagering-tax exemption

**Holding:** For Indian gaming conducted under IGRA or an effective Tribal-State compact entered under §2710(d)(3), §2719(d)(1) directs state-equivalent treatment of the Code provisions concerning reporting and withholding of taxes on gaming or wagering winnings. Its parenthetical chapter 35 reference does not extend that defined subject to chapter 35's substantive wagering excise and occupational taxes. Section 2719(d)(2) makes the subsection govern notwithstanding other law unless that law specifically cites the subsection; this priority applies only within (d)(1)'s scope.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stevens, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies six direct votes for the complete construction and both affirmances.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Indian-law canon remains available for genuine residual ambiguity but does not overcome the resolved reporting-and-withholding scope. Chapter 35's independent definitions, exclusions, tax base and occupational-tax linkage retain their own operation; the judgment recognizes no general tribal federal-tax immunity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both tribes' judgments rejecting the asserted IGRA exemption are affirmed, 6–3.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States National Bank of Oregon v. Independent Insurance Agents of America (June 7, 1993): applied to read the whole enacted provision; no general authority to rewrite statutory text is adopted.
- Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation (June 14, 1995): its state-tax incidence, residence, source and treaty rules are distinguished from this federal statutory exemption.
- Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band of Chippewa Indians (March 24, 1999): its protective interpretation of retained tribal rights remains; it creates no exemption outside the scope of this resolved federal text.

## Holdings volume: federal-taxation

# SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES: HOLDINGS REGISTER

**Last completed October Term:** 2001
**Processed through:** June 28, 2002, after all 127 completed OT2001 Court events.
**Edition:** November 11, 2026

## Federal Taxation

### Bankruptcy fiduciaries’ filing and payment duties

#### Holywell Corp. v. Smith, 503 U.S. 47 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-1361 and 90-1484.
**Decided:** February 25, 1992.
**Companion or consolidated matters:** *United States v. Smith*, No. 90-1484, consolidated with *Holywell*; both challenge the declaration of no filing or payment duty.

##### Whether the assignee of substantially all corporate property must file corporate returns and pay the associated tax

**Holding:** An assignee holding all or substantially all of a corporation’s property must make its income-tax return under §6012(b)(3), whether or not the corporation dissolves or the assignee continues its business. Smith’s receipt of the corporate estate property satisfies that category, and §6151(a) requires payment of the tax due with those returns.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous statutory construction.

**Material application:** The plan assigned substantially all corporate property to Smith for liquidation and distribution. The filing duty follows the statutory assignee category and does not depend on operating the business or dissolving the corporations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No alternative statutory classification, exact income, deduction, penalty or tax amount is decided. The duties concern returns and accompanying payment after appointment, not taxes already due before appointment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand in both dockets for Smith to file the prescribed corporate returns as assignee and pay the tax due with them under §6151; amounts and implementation are left to ordinary tax and bankruptcy procedures.

##### Whether Smith must file and pay as fiduciary of the separate liquidating trust holding Gould’s former estate property

**Holding:** Smith must file the separate liquidating trust’s return under §6012(b)(4) and pay the associated tax under §6151: the plan vested Gould’s estate property directly in a separate trust organized to liquidate and distribute the transferred assets and authorized Smith to perform those functions, making him its fiduciary despite limits on his discretion. Because the property did not first revest in Gould for contribution to the trust, the asserted grantor-trust theory does not displace Smith’s duty.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous trust-specific construction and application.

**Material application:** The plan created a separate trust to liquidate and distribute transferred assets within Treasury Regulation §301.7701-4(d); Smith’s powers satisfy §7701(a)(6) and Regulation §301.7701-6 despite limits on his discretion. The estate transferred Gould’s property directly to the trust; Gould did not first recover and contribute it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal tax treatment for all liquidating trusts is adopted. Grantor provisions retain their operation when their predicates exist; the result of a different revesting-and-contribution arrangement is not decided. The Court computes no tax amount.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand in both dockets for Smith to file as trust fiduciary and pay the associated tax, with computation and implementation left to ordinary procedures.

##### Whether confirmation of a plan silent about taxes and the United States’ failure to object excuse later statutory duties

**Holding:** Confirmation under §1141(a) of a plan silent about taxes, coupled with the United States’ failure to object, does not excuse the statutory return and payment obligations imposed on this assignee and trust fiduciary after appointment. The binding treatment of properly encompassed preconfirmation claims does not establish an exemption from these later obligations.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous rejection of the confirmation defense.

**Material application:** The government seeks recognition of Smith’s postappointment duties, not payment from him of taxes already due before appointment. The plan neither stated nor adjudicated an exemption; §6151 connects payment to the required return’s due date.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court assumes, without deciding more broadly, that confirmation can bind creditors concerning properly encompassed preconfirmation claims. Preappointment taxes and express adjudication of existing tax claims remain outside the disposition.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The no-duty declaration is reversed in both dockets; confirmation supplies no defense to implementation of the postappointment filing and payment obligations.

### Capital expenditures

#### INDOPCO, Inc. v. Commissioner, 503 U.S. 79 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1278.
**Decided:** February 26, 1992.

##### Whether a separate asset is necessary for capitalization and whether these acquisition expenses are currently deductible

**Holding:** Creation or enhancement of a separate and distinct asset is a sufficient ground for capitalization under §263, not an indispensable condition; the character, duration and extent of a transaction’s benefits also matter. The professional expenses facilitating this acquisition’s established enduring corporate restructuring benefits are capital expenditures, not immediately deductible §162(a) operating expenses; mere incidental future benefit alone does not compel capitalization.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous statutory holding and application.

**Material application:** The investment-banking, legal and related expenses facilitated a negotiated acquisition producing established corporate benefits beyond the acquisition year: access to Unilever’s resources, simplified ownership and conversion from independent public corporation to subsidiary. Those benefits do not rest solely on a shareholder’s estate-planning preference or speculative profit estimates.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No automatic capitalization rule for every future benefit, acquisition or professional fee is adopted. Hostile acquisitions, failed transactions, unrelated fees and later cost-recovery periods are not decided; the ordinary taxpayer burden to establish a deduction remains unchanged.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of the disputed immediate deductions and preserve capital classification. No new amortization schedule or additional tax assessment is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Commissioner v. Lincoln Savings & Loan Association*, 403 U.S. 345, is clarified: creating a separate asset is sufficient but not necessary for capitalization. *Welch v. Helvering*, 290 U.S. 111, supplies the contextual distinction between necessary business outlays and current expenses. *Woodward v. Commissioner*, 397 U.S. 572, and *United States v. Hilton Hotels Corp.*, 397 U.S. 580, connect professional costs to the capital acquisition they facilitate. *Commissioner v. Idaho Power Co.*, 418 U.S. 1, preserves capitalization despite an otherwise available expense category. *Interstate Transit Lines v. Commissioner*, 319 U.S. 590, leaves the taxpayer’s deduction burden unchanged.

### Income exclusions

#### United States v. Burke, 504 U.S. 229 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-42.
**Decided:** May 26, 1992.

##### Whether the identified pre-1991 Title VII settlement payments are excluded from gross income under §104(a)(2)

**Authority of the judgment:** The Sixth Circuit’s exclusion ruling is reversed, 6–3: White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter support reversal; Stone, O’Connor and Thomas oppose it. The identified payments may not be excluded in the refund action. No controlling statutory rationale exists under *Marks*. Blackmun, White, Stevens and Kennedy rely on the old Title VII remedial scheme; Scalia separately construes the statute around physical or mental health; Souter accepts the regulation, recognizes competing tort and contract analogies, rejects making intangible remedies indispensable, and relies on narrow construction of uncertain exclusions. Neither concurrence is established as a logical subset of the plurality’s remedial classification.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The judgment creates no general remedial-range test, health-injury requirement or discrimination-recovery exclusion. No separate FICA wages issue, punitive-damages classification, ordinary wage settlement, distinct tort allocation, amended statutory regime or Title VII jury-trial question is decided. The settlement establishes no adjudicated discrimination finding. No independently sufficient alternative holding has five votes. Further proceedings must give effect to reversal, without enlarging the recovery or valuation; no tax amount, separate withholding theory or reopening of the settlement is ordered.

#### Commissioner v. Schleier

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-500.
**Decided:** June 14, 1995.

##### Receipt-specific causation under the return-year exclusion

**Holding:** Under the applicable § 104(a)(2), a taxpayer must satisfy both the regulation's tort-or-tort-type-right requirement and the statute's independent requirement that the particular damages be received on account of personal injuries or sickness; amounts attributable to deductions allowed under § 213 in a prior year remain excepted from the exclusion. Assuming the ADEA claim satisfies the first requirement, these backpay and willfulness-based liquidated damages fail the second and must be included in gross income.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; five directly support the causal holding. Scalia concurs only in the judgment and supplies no vote for this explanation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The return-year provision concerns personal injuries or sickness without the later physical-injury amendment. Backpay here replaces earnings lost through employment deprivation, not inability to work from a demonstrated personal injury; ADEA liquidated damages punish knowing or reckless willfulness. Tort-type status is assumed. No categorical ADEA classification, exclusion of all wage-measured injury damages, general punitive-damages rule, physical/mental-health-only test or other settlement allocation is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Burke — its fractured judgment remains; no general remedial-range rule is treated as controlling and no new tort-classification alternative is adopted.
- Trans World Airlines, Inc. v. Thurston, 469 U.S. 111 — applied for the punitive purpose of ADEA liquidated damages.
- Hazen Paper Co. v. Biggins — applied for knowing or reckless willfulness; it does not itself decide tax treatment.
- Overnight Motor Transportation Co. v. Missel, 316 U.S. 572 — distinguished because its FLSA compensatory account does not characterize this ADEA multiplier.
- McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co. — preserved as distinguishing liability and employment remedy; it supplies no tax-exclusion holding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse both exclusions and remand for any necessary allocation and lawful tax calculation including the ADEA backpay and liquidated damages. No exact deficiency, refund, withholding amount or new settlement valuation is ordered.

**Later-authority backlinks:** O’Gilvie v. United States, December 10, 1996, Nos. 95-966 and 95-977: extends receipt-specific causation to the noncompensatory punitive award under the former exclusion; both statutory conditions and the prior-§213-deduction exception remain intact.

### Intergovernmental tax immunity

#### Barker v. Kansas, 503 U.S. 594 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-611.
**Decided:** April 21, 1992.

##### Whether Kansas may tax military retirement while exempting comparable state and local retirement benefits

**Holding:** For 4 U.S.C. §111, the military retirement benefits at issue are deferred compensation for past service, comparable to exempt Kansas state and local retirement benefits. Continued military status, restrictions and possible recall do not justify taxing the entire military retirement stream while exempting those comparable benefits; the asserted difference must directly relate to and justify the unequal tax treatment.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas; nine votes control. Stevens and Thomas also concur separately without limiting their joins.

**Material application:** Rank and completed years of service principally determine these payments. Continued military status, discipline, employment restrictions and possible recall do not explain taxing the entire pension stream while exempting comparable state pensions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Compensation specifically tied to new service, other military-status consequences, materially different tax classifications and preserved retroactivity or refund issues remain undecided. Other constitutional and state-law claims supply no independent holding; McKesson and Beam are not modified.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Kansas Supreme Court judgment is reversed and remanded for compliance with §111 and consideration of preserved retrospective-relief questions under then-governing law. No refund amount is awarded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Davis v. Michigan Department of Treasury, 489 U.S. 803 — applied: retirement income receives §111 protection and a tax-relevant significant difference must justify unequal treatment.
- Phillips Chemical Co. v. Dumas Independent School District, 361 U.S. 376 — applied through Davis: the difference must directly relate to and justify the disparity.
- United States v. Tyler, 105 U.S. 244 — confined to its statutory pay-increase context; continuing military status does not establish current compensation for all purposes.
- McCarty v. McCarty, 453 U.S. 210 — distinguished: it reserved the characterization question and its community-property prohibition was displaced by the Uniformed Services Former Spouses’ Protection Act; no surviving rule authorizes this tax disparity.

### National Firearms Act making tax

#### United States v. Thompson/Center Arms Co., 504 U.S. 505 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-164.
**Decided:** June 8, 1992.

##### Whether the genuine unassembled dual-use Contender package incurs the short-barreled-rifle making tax

**Holding:** The National Firearms Act does not clearly treat the segregated Contender pistol and conversion package, which has genuine lawful pistol and long-rifle uses and has not been assembled as a short-barreled rifle, as making that regulated firearm. Because the same definition carries criminal consequences, the remaining ambiguity is resolved for the manufacturer in this civil refund action as well.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's plurality opinion joined by O'Connor, Scalia's concurrence joined by Thomas, and Stone's concurrence expressly share this case-specific construction and uniform residual lenity. These five form an actual majority at this level of generality; no complete opinion controls and no synthetic *Marks* rationale combines their different accounts of the ambiguity.

**Current force:** Remains controlling for the identified genuine dual-use package and the shared residual-lenity proposition.

**Material application:** Section 5845's rifle definition addresses design, making and intended shoulder firing, while other firearm definitions expressly address combinations of parts. The language and related definitions leave ambiguity for this package's lawful pistol and long-rifle configurations alongside its regulated short-rifle capability.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No complete opinion controls the broader division between permissible and regulated combinations. Actual short-rifle assembly, pure regulated-firearm kits, sham lawful configurations, miscellaneous or incomplete parts, unrelated firearm categories and separate criminal offenses remain unadjudicated. No general intent-to-assemble element, new criminal proof burden, storage protocol or exemption for all parts kits is created. Souter's broader utility taxonomy, Scalia and Thomas's separate shoulder-firing account and Stevens's civil-notice exception remain noncontrolling.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Federal Circuit's refund judgment, 5–4, with Stone, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas supporting affirmance and White, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy opposing. The $200 making tax is not owed on the segregated unassembled unit; no broader injunction or new remand command issues.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Crandon v. United States*, 494 U.S. 152, supplies one construction of criminally consequential language in civil litigation without changing criminal proof. *United States v. R. L. C.*, March 24, 1992, preserves its adult-Guidelines ceiling without establishing a shared new lenity method or firearms rule. *Marks v. United States*, 430 U.S. 188, supplies no synthesized broader rationale where the exact case-specific proposition already has five express supporters.

##### Whether making a firearm invariably requires final mechanical assembly

**Holding:** The National Firearms Act's references to manufacturing and otherwise producing a firearm are not confined to the final mechanical act of putting its parts together. The Federal Circuit's universal actual-assembly prerequisite is rejected without deciding tax liability for an unpresented pure-parts or sham arrangement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter and O'Connor expressly adopt the construction; White's dissent, joined by Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy, adopts the same predicate; and Stone's concurrence expressly rejects a universal final-assembly requirement while reserving pure-parts and sham liability. These seven directly support the exact proposition across writings. Scalia and Thomas disagree. This is an actual majority proposition, not a *Marks* synthesis or a join in the plurality's broader taxonomy.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated, without a categorical rule for the reserved arrangements.

**Material application:** Section 5845(i) separately identifies manufacturing, otherwise producing and putting together. Requiring final assembly for every form of making would deprive the broader production language of work. Production must still meet the enacted definition; regulatory purpose alone does not impose a tax.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No holding classifies every miscellaneous collection, incomplete kit or genuine alternative-use set as a firearm. Liability for pure-parts kits, sham configurations, actual short-rifle assembly and separate offenses remains outside the decision.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The narrow refund judgment remains affirmed; the Federal Circuit's universal actual-assembly rationale does not survive as the governing construction.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *United States v. Drasen*, 845 F.2d 731, supports the manufacturing construction without adjudicating its kits or adopting its broader notice and criminal-responsibility discussion. *Thompson/Center Arms Co. v. United States*, 924 F.2d 1041, is affirmed in judgment but its universal actual-assembly rationale is rejected.

### Assessment limitations for pass-through income

#### Bufferd v. Commissioner of Internal Revenue, 506 U.S. 523 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-7804.

**Decided:** January 25, 1993.

##### Which return starts limitations on the shareholder’s individual assessment

**Holding:** For an assessment of an S corporation shareholder’s individual income tax, the return that starts the ordinary § 6501(a) limitations period is the shareholder’s return, including applicable extensions and statutory exceptions. Expiration of the period associated with the corporation’s § 6037 information return does not bar correcting pass-through items in a timely shareholder assessment; the corporate return remains operative for corporate-level taxes to which it applies.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s opinion for the Court, joined in full by the other eight Justices. All nine Justices adopt this rule and its explanation; it controls by a direct majority.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Automobile Club of Michigan v. Commissioner, 353 U.S. 180, 188 (1957), applied by analogy: a filing insufficient to compute the relevant taxpayer’s liability does not trigger that taxpayer’s assessment period; the holding is not a rule that informational completeness alone transforms one person’s return into another’s.
- Germantown Trust Co. v. Commissioner, 309 U.S. 304 (1940), distinguished in scope: a sufficient return for the same taxpayer may trigger limitations despite its form; it supplies no corporate-return substitute for a shareholder’s own return.
- Holywell Corp. v. Smith, February 25, 1992, left undisturbed: distinct fiduciary and trust filing duties do not decide the limitations question here.
No Supreme Court precedent is overruled. The contrary corporate-period rule applied in Kelley v. Commissioner, 877 F.2d 756 (9th Cir. 1989), cannot govern this shareholder-assessment question. No broader C corporation rule is decided.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ordinary three-year period remains subject to the shareholder’s applicable extensions and statutory exceptions. The corporate return retains its corporate-tax function. Special audit regimes, the unpressed wording objection to the individual extension and any general C corporation rule are not decided; later unified-audit rules do not govern the 1979 liability.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Second Circuit: the timely shareholder assessment is not barred by expiration of the corporate-return period.

### Depreciation of acquired intangible assets

#### Newark Morning Ledger Co. v. United States, 507 U.S. 546 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1135.

**Decided:** April 20, 1993.

##### Proof controls depreciation of the acquired customer intangible

**Holding:** Under section 167 and Regulation §1.167(a)-3, a taxpayer that proves an acquired intangible’s ascertainable value and limited useful life estimable with reasonable accuracy may depreciate it despite its resemblance to expected continued patronage. The taxpayer bears the substantial proof burden; unsupported assertions, indefinite or self-regenerating value and double allocation do not qualify, and goodwill remains nondepreciable.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Massey Motors, 364 U.S. 92 — applied: depreciation matches capital cost with useful contribution.
- Houston Chronicle, 481 F.2d 1240; Miller, 537 F.2d 446; Donrey, 809 F.2d 534 — persuasive proof-based reasoning adopted, not a categorical customer-list exemption.
- INDOPCO, February 26, 1992 — distinguished: capitalization and later cost recovery are separate questions.
- Red Wing Malting, 15 F.2d 626 — preserved for nonwasting goodwill; not extended to defeat proof of a distinct wasting asset.

##### The established subscriber findings satisfy the rule

**Holding:** The finite acquired subscriber cohort here satisfies the separate-value and reasonably measurable limited-life requirements on the existing trial findings. The useful-life and straight-line stipulation becomes operative only after those prerequisites are met; reversing the categorical goodwill rule neither supplies an unconditional concession nor requires proof of the entire case anew.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, II; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Houston Chronicle, 481 F.2d 1240 — applied through its evidentiary requirements; actual findings matter.
- Golden State Towel, 373 F.2d 938 — distinguished: the acquired cohort is found not to be a self-regenerating mass.
- Massey Motors, 364 U.S. 92 — applied to the proved finite-life asset; no universal valuation method adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The finite purchased subscriber cohort excludes later automatic replacement customers; indefinite or self-regenerating goodwill remains nondepreciable. The useful-life and straight-line stipulation is conditional on the proved prerequisites. No universal valuation method, customer-list exemption or duplicate basis allocation is authorized.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the categorical goodwill judgment and remand consistently with the existing separate-value and finite-life findings and conditional stipulation. No later amortization regime or new tax amount is imposed.

### Home-office deductions

#### Commissioner v. Soliman, 506 U.S. 168 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-998.

**Decided:** January 12, 1993.

##### Comparative principal-place-of-business inquiry under former §280A(c)(1)(A)

**Holding:** Under then-effective §280A(c)(1)(A), identifying the principal place of business requires comparing the relative importance of activities performed at each business location and the time spent there; necessary administration or the lack of another office alone does not make a home office principal. Customer contact or service delivery receives great but nonconclusive weight, special facilities may add weight, time is particularly useful when the functions do not settle the comparison, and a home office does not qualify by default when no place emerges as principal.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O’Connor and Souter: six votes for this formulation and its application. Thomas and Scalia agree only with the judgment on their separately stated approach; Stevens dissents. The Court opinion controls without a fractured-decision inquiry.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Malat v. Riddell, 383 U.S. 569 (1966), and Crane v. Commissioner, 331 U.S. 1 (1947): applied for ordinary meaning in revenue statutes; neither is displaced.
- Welch v. Helvering, 290 U.S. 111 (1933): retained for the fact-sensitive character of deduction questions; it does not prevent articulating consistent statutory considerations.
- The Fourth Circuit’s necessity/substantial-time/no-other-office test is rejected because it fails to compare locations; no earlier Supreme Court principal-place test is overruled.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Exclusive and regular business use remain prerequisites. Patient or client visits are not indispensable under subsection (A); subsection (B)’s normal-course meetings and subsection (C)’s unattached structures remain separate unadjudicated alternatives. The employer-convenience condition applies to employee use, not this self-employed taxpayer. No fixed arithmetic weighting or default home-office qualification is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Fourth Circuit and disallow the 1983 principal-place home-office deduction on the hospital-service and time facts. Other deductions, including unappealed travel determinations, are untouched.

### Minimum-tax depletion basis
