#### United States v. Hill, 506 U.S. 546 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1421.

**Decided:** January 25, 1993.

##### Mineral-interest basis under former §57(a)(8)

**Holding:** For tax years governed by former § 57(a)(8), the adjusted basis offset against percentage depletion is the adjusted basis of the taxpayer’s § 614 interest in the mineral deposit, determined without that year’s depletion deduction; it excludes separately depreciable tangible drilling and development improvements identified in Treasury Regulation § 1.612-4(c)(1). Lawfully capitalized intangible costs recoverable through depletion remain in depletable basis; this classification does not erase distinct statutory provisions governing other costs or later taxable years.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s opinion for the Court, joined in full by the other eight Justices. All nine Justices adopt this rule and its explanation; it controls by a direct majority.

**Current force:** Remains controlling for liabilities governed by former §57(a)(8), including the 1981–1982 years adjudicated. The Energy Policy Act of 1992, Pub. L. 102-486 §1915(a), (d), enacted October 24, 1992, removes §613A(c) depletion deductions from amended §57(a)(1) for taxable years beginning after December 31, 1992. Its coordinated §56(g)(4)(F)(ii) exception likewise removes those deductions from clause (i) and subparagraph (C)(i) for those taxable years; clause (i)’s general cost-depletion rule retains its separate condition for property placed in service in a taxable year beginning after December 31, 1989. The holding does not supply a contrary rule for the amended years.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Energy Policy Act of 1992, Pub. L. 102-486 §1915(a), (d), 106 Stat. 3023–3024; enacted October 24, 1992; applicable to taxable years beginning after December 31, 1992; prospective depletion-preference and adjusted-current-earnings exceptions. This is noncase-law scope information, not a judicial holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- INDOPCO, Inc. v. Commissioner, February 26, 1992, left undisturbed and distinguished by issue: capitalization can be required without creating a separate asset; Hill concerns allocation among already distinct depreciation and depletion accounts. No restrictive separate-asset prerequisite is adopted.
The controlling classification follows the coordinated statutory and regulatory text. No Supreme Court precedent is overruled or made into a general rule against multiple tax benefits. Technical Advice Memorandum 8314011 supplies no precedent under then-§ 6110(j)(3); its § 616 deferred-development setting also has an express basis direction different from the costs here. The memorandum is not a judicial holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The former-law classification does not combine depreciation and depletion accounts because assets are commercially integrated. Properly capitalized depletable intangible costs remain in mineral basis; no general ban on multiple tax benefits is adopted. Technical Advice Memorandum 8314011 is nonprecedential and its §616 provision has a distinct express basis direction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Federal Circuit and remand for implementation of the mineral-interest basis calculation for the 1981–1982 liabilities. The prospective 1992 amendment does not govern those years; no new factual hearing is directed.

### Priority of federal tax liens

#### United States v. McDermott, 507 U.S. 447 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1229.

**Decided:** March 24, 1993.

##### Federal notice precedes choateness in after-acquired property

**Holding:** A filed federal tax lien takes priority over a previously docketed judgment lien in land acquired only after federal notice, because the judgment lien becomes choate in that land only upon acquisition. Both liens attach when the debtor acquires the property; priority here follows the federal notice rule, not fictional earlier attachment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- New Britain, 347 U.S. 81 — applied: choateness requires established lienor, amount and property.
- Glass City Bank, 326 U.S. 265 — applied: federal liens reach after-acquired property upon acquisition.
- Vermont, 377 U.S. 351 — distinguished: no separate levy is required for a lien already fixed in existing property; future acquisition remains different.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The priority concerns the September 23 land and substituted proceeds; statutory protections for other creditors and financing interests remain. An asserted earlier contract interest was not preserved within this dispute and is not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Tenth Circuit and implement federal-lien priority in the escrowed proceeds; no unrelated lien or property is adjudicated.

### Pension property transfers and prohibited exchanges

#### Commissioner v. Keystone Consolidated Industries, Inc., 508 U.S. 152 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1677.
**Decided:** May 24, 1993.

##### The established tax meaning includes property exchanged for release of a funding duty

**Holding:** In §4975(c)(1)(A), the established tax meaning of sale or exchange includes an employer’s transfer of property in satisfaction of an enforceable pension-funding obligation. An unencumbered transfer meeting that condition is not excluded merely because it is called a contribution.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Part III-A; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Helvering v. Hammel, 311 U.S. 504: settled tax treatment of obligation-discharging transfers applied.
- Sorenson v. Secretary of Treasury, 475 U.S. 851: consistent tax-language construction applied.
- Atlantic Cleaners & Dyers, Inc. v. United States, 286 U.S. 427: contextual qualification preserved; it does not justify a different meaning here.
- Commissioner v. Lester, 366 U.S. 299: internal consistency of tax language supports the established-meaning rationale.
- Albernaz v. United States, 450 U.S. 333: prior settled construction informs the meaning Congress selected.

##### Discharge supplies consideration even without importing a technical tax meaning

**Independent alternative holding:** Independently of a settled technical tax meaning, transferring property to discharge an employer’s pension-funding duty is an exchange under §4975(c)(1)(A): the plan receives property and the employer receives release from its obligation. Section 4975(f)(3)’s inclusion of property subject to a mortgage or similar lien assumed by the plan or placed by a disqualified person within ten years does not exclude other exchanges supported by consideration.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Blackmun’s Parts III-C and III-D; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The settled-tax-meaning and ordinary-exchange grounds are independently sufficient. Gratuitous contributions, unpresented exemptions, correction conditions, valuation and computation remain distinct; no agency-deference holding is necessary.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand under the correct transfer classification and applicable tax conditions; unencumbered status alone supplies no exclusion. No automatic second-tier assessment or final tax computation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- This independently sufficient ground rests directly on §4975(c)(1)(A) and (f)(3); it requires no additional treatment of an earlier decision.

### Gift-tax disclaimers

#### United States v. Irvine, 511 U.S. 224

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1546.

**Decided:** 1994-04-20.

##### Federal law taxes the later gratuitous transfer, independently of state relation back

**Holding:** Subject to the gift-tax chapter's limitations, §§2501(a)(1) and 2511(a) reach a later direct or indirect gratuitous transfer of a valuable property interest, including this delayed disclaimer directing part of a remainder to the disclaimant's children. State law defines the interests and the disclaimer's effectiveness, but its relation-back fiction does not control federal taxability unless federal law makes that consequence dependent on state law; even if the regulatory disclaimer exception does not apply to this 1917 interest, the statutory transfer rule independently reaches the 1979 disposition.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's statutory-transfer portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; all eight participating Justices adopt this statutory ground and the alternative for regulatory inapplicability.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dickman v. Commissioner, 465 U.S. 330 (1984): applied for the comprehensive reach of the federal gift-transfer provisions.
- Jewett v. Commissioner, 455 U.S. 305 (1982): applied to the indirect transfer through a delayed disclaimer and the absence of a state-law right to federal tax immunity.
- Morgan v. Commissioner, 309 U.S. 78 (1940), and United States v. Pelzer, 312 U.S. 399 (1941): applied to distinguish state property interests from their federal tax treatment unless the federal provision makes state law controlling.

**Independent alternative holding:** If Treasury Regulation §25.2511-1(c)(2) applies to this pre-1977 interest, its disclaimer exception requires an unequivocal refusal effective under local law, within a reasonable time after knowledge of the transfer creating the interest and before acceptance; a partial refusal must be complete and unqualified in light of the facts and local law. With knowledge and capacity present by 1931, Irvine's 1979 refusal fails the reasonable-time requirement whether time runs from that knowledge or from enactment in 1932; no fixed deadline or obligation to disclaim before enactment is established, and applicability to this pre-Act inter vivos trust remains undecided.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Souter's conditional reasonable-time portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; seven Justices adopt this independently sufficient conditional ground and its explanation. Scalia does not join this portion, though he agrees with taxability on his separate acceptance-and-transfer rationale.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Jewett v. Commissioner: applied conditionally to the knowledge measure and the gift-tax/estate-tax relationship; its rule is not limited to the date an interest becomes possessory.
- Estate of Sanford v. Commissioner, 308 U.S. 39 (1939): applied through Jewett for the gift tax's complementary role in preventing avoidance of transfers-at-death taxation.

##### The 1932 limitation protects earlier transfers, not every later disposition of an old interest

**Holding:** Revenue Act of 1932 §501(b) excludes transfers made on or before June 6, 1932; it does not exempt a later gratuitous transfer merely because the transferred interest was created before that date. The relevant taxable transfer here occurred through the 1979 disclaimer, so the statutory prospective limitation does not bar the tax; no ruling requires a pre-enactment disclaimer or approves retroactive Treasury lawmaking generally.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's prospective-application portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices adopt this answer to the independent statutory defense.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Jacobs, 306 U.S. 363 (1939): applied to distinguish a tax on a later transfer from retroactive taxation of an earlier event.
- Jewett v. Commissioner: applied to reject state relation back as the federal tax date.
- Untermyer v. Anderson, 276 U.S. 440 (1928): distinguished; this case taxes the later disposition and does not adjudicate a retroactive tax on the 1917 transfer.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This is a tax on the 1979 disposition, not the trust's 1917 creation. Regulatory applicability to the pre-Act inter vivos interest remains open; the Court sets no numerical reasonable-time deadline and requires no pre-enactment disclaimer. A prompt refusal before acceptance or absence of a reasonable post-enactment opportunity presents different facts.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Eighth Circuit's 1979 refund judgment is reversed, 8–0; Blackmun does not participate. No special valuation remand, fixed payment amount or disposition of the separate 1980 assessment is ordered.

### Railroad property-tax exemptions

#### Department of Revenue of Oregon v. ACF Industries, Inc., 510 U.S. 332

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-74.

**Decided:** 1994-01-24.

##### The ordinary property-tax comparison excludes exempt property

**Holding:** For §11503(b)(1)–(3), the §11503(a)(4) commercial-and-industrial comparison class consists of property devoted to commercial or industrial use and actually subject to a property-tax levy, excluding transportation property and land used primarily for agriculture or timber growing. Property exempt from that levy is outside the comparison class; these paragraphs therefore do not require ordinary exemptions granted to nonrailroad property to be extended to railroad property.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices adopt the statutory definition as the premise of the exemption ruling.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sorenson v. Secretary of Treasury: applied; repeated statutory levy language bears a consistent meaning in the coordinated provisions.
- Burlington Northern Railroad Co. v. Oklahoma Tax Commission: preserved; its assessment protection does not itself decide exemptions or enlarge the defined comparison class.

##### Ordinary exemptions do not become forbidden discrimination under the catchall

**Holding:** Section 11503(b)(4) does not permit invalidation of railroad-property taxation solely because a generally applicable property tax based on value exempts ordinary categories of nonrailroad property. The Court leaves open a scheme under which railroads alone, or railroads with only an isolated and targeted group, bear the tax; it establishes neither a percentage threshold nor a rule that every tax labeled a property tax is beyond paragraph (4).

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices adopt this construction and the reversal of the exemption requirement.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mountain States Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Pueblo of Santa Ana: applied; one provision should not be read to make another inoperative.
- United Savings Assn. of Texas v. Timbers of Inwood Forest Associates: applied; the statute's coordinated structure resolves the ambiguity of an isolated phrase.
- Davis v. Michigan Department of Treasury: distinguished; exemptions can discriminate, but its different statute does not displace the 4-R Act's structure.
- Burlington Northern Railroad Co. v. Oklahoma Tax Commission: preserved for the separate assessment protection; no new intent requirement, exemption percentage or rate rule is created.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No numerical exemption safe harbor, universal property-tax exclusion from paragraph (4), or constitutional validation of exemptions is adopted. Railroad-only or isolated targeted-group taxation remains reserved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded, removing the automatic total-exemption requirement. No tax amount, assessment or new finding of targeted taxation is ordered; an unasserted assessment or rate claim is not reopened.

### Retroactive estate-tax amendments

#### United States v. Carlton, 512 U.S. 26

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1941.

**Decided:** 1994-06-13.

##### The enacted amendment governs the earlier transaction

**Holding:** Section 10411(b) expressly makes the 1987 amendment to §2057 effective as if included in the original 1986 enactment, so its decedent-ownership requirement reaches the estate's December 1986 transaction, subject to constitutional review. The original statute contained no such condition; neither later legislative explanation nor an agency announcement supplies a preexisting ownership requirement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Ginsburg. These six Justices adopt the statutory holding; the judgment concurrences accept the express temporal direction without joining the opinion.

**Material application:** The Government stipulated that the estate qualified under the original text. The express 1987 effective-date provision, rather than an earlier agency notice or later account of original purpose, supplies the new ownership condition's retrospective operation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Landgraf v. USI Film Products, April 26, 1994: applies its priority for express temporal direction, subject to constitutional limits.
- Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., April 26, 1994: distinguishes enacted change from judicial construction; corrective purpose alone does not provide temporal reach.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, June 18, 1993: preserves its rule for judicial decisions in open direct review; that rule does not displace Congress's express amendment.

##### The limited retroactive ownership amendment satisfies due process

**Holding:** Retroactive economic legislation satisfies due process when its retroactive operation rationally serves a legitimate legislative purpose. Applying this prompt correction of the unexpectedly broad estate-tax deduction to the short interval since its enactment meets that standard despite the executor's established reliance, lack of advance notice and actual loss; this decision creates neither a fixed one-year limit, an automatic reliance exemption nor an unlimited revenue-purpose safe harbor.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Ginsburg. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg supply six direct joins in this constitutional rationale; O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas agree only with the judgment on their distinct grounds.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides neither the abandoned Contract Clause and Takings theories nor a new tax-loss compensation claim. No allocation failure, agency power to amend the original statute, fixed constitutional lookback limit or new dollar assessment is adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Pension Benefit Guaranty Corp. v. R. A. Gray & Co., 467 U.S. 717: applies rational-purpose review to the retroactive operation itself and explains the harsh-and-oppressive formulation.
- General Motors Corp. v. Romein, March 9, 1992: applies its rational-justification rule despite actual reliance, without treating all retroactivity as valid.
- Usery v. Turner Elkhorn Mining Co., 428 U.S. 1: preserves legislative power to adjust economic obligations retrospectively for a rational legitimate purpose.
- Welch v. Henry, 305 U.S. 134, and United States v. Hemme, 476 U.S. 558: preserve context-sensitive tax review; their harsh-and-oppressive language creates no automatic reliance veto.
- United States v. Darusmont, 449 U.S. 292, and Milliken v. United States, 283 U.S. 15: preserve permissible tax retroactivity in their settings, without a universal advance-notice condition.
- Untermyer v. Anderson, 276 U.S. 440: distinguishes its wholly new gift tax from this amendment to a deduction within an existing estate-tax regime.
- United States v. Irvine, April 20, 1994: distinguishes its later taxable transfer; the present amendment is genuinely retroactive and receives constitutional review.
- Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994, permits the bounded congressional reinstatement of completed securities limitations dismissals under an expressly retrospective statutory rule preserving independent judicial adjudication. The present case concerns a substantive amendment to the estate-tax law and its application to an earlier transaction, not the reinstatement of a completed federal judicial judgment. Neither decision establishes unlimited constitutional permission for retrospective legislation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for judgment applying the amended ownership requirement. No refund follows from the rejected due-process theory and no compensation is awarded for the transaction loss. The Court fixes no new assessment, penalty, valuation, allocation failure or refund amount.

### Federal obligations and private repurchase income

#### Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-823.
**Decided:** December 12, 1994.

For the other questions in this decision, see Constitutional Structure — Federal borrowing and state taxation (The borrowing-power objection is not established by evidence about bankruptcy classification).

##### The private repo return is outside the federal-obligation exemption

**Holding:** Section 3124(a) does not exempt the return on these repurchase agreements because it is owed under a separate private financing obligation, rather than on the United States' securities; actual book-entry delivery does not change the source of the negotiated earnings. The statute continues to protect direct and indirect taxation of federal obligations and their interest, except nondiscriminatory corporate franchise or substitute corporate nonproperty taxes and estate or inheritance taxes; the State prevails because this income is outside the protected subject, not because an exception covers this individual income tax.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Rockford Life Insurance Co. v. Illinois Department of Revenue, 482 U.S. 182 (1987): applies its distinction between an actual federal obligation and separate private earnings; its GNMA facts are not substituted for these delivered federal securities.
- Smith v. Davis, 323 U.S. 111 (1944): applies the boundary between the Government's own direct obligation and a separate private obligation supported by federal assets or credit.
- Frank Lyon Co. v. United States, 435 U.S. 561 (1978): distinguishes respect for a genuine transaction's form from identifying the obligation producing income under §3124; no sham or universal form-disregard rule is adopted.
- American Bank & Trust Co. v. Dallas County, 463 U.S. 855 (1983), and First National Bank of Atlanta v. Bartow County Board of Tax Assessors, 470 U.S. 583 (1985): preserve protection when the tax computation actually reaches protected obligations or their income; neither classifies this private repo return.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Subsection (b) sends the tax status of interest on Government or agency obligations, income from evidences of ownership, and disposition gain or loss to the Internal Revenue Code. An obligation that the Federal Housing Administration agreed, under a contract made before March 1, 1941, to issue at a future date retains the tax-exemption privileges provided by the authorizing law at the time of that contract. Subsection (b) does not apply to obligations and evidences of ownership issued by the District of Columbia, a territory or possession, or their departments, agencies, instrumentalities or political subdivisions; it does not erase subsection (a). No separate construction, application or validity ruling concerning a Seller-Borrower's §77-2716(1)(e)(i) add-back is made.

##### The asserted discrimination lacks a supported comparator policy

**Holding:** A ruling that expressly addresses federal-obligation repos does not, without an identified favorable policy for comparable state or local repos, establish the discriminatory taxation alleged here. The discrimination objection therefore supplies no ground to invalidate Revenue Ruling 22-85-1 on this record; the Court does not approve actual discriminatory treatment or find every Nebraska practice neutral.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Memphis Bank & Trust Co. v. Garner, 459 U.S. 392 (1983): preserves the prohibition of actual discriminatory taxation favoring state obligations; an unproved comparator policy does not establish its predicate.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Nebraska Supreme Court is reversed as to Revenue Ruling 22-85-1 and the case remanded to uphold the challenged tax on this repo income and conduct consistent remaining proceedings. No separate Seller-Borrower add-back construction, assessment, refund or recomputation is ordered.

### Refund suits by nonassessed property owners

#### United States v. Williams

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-395.
**Decided:** April 25, 1995.

##### Express refund consent includes this lien-backed collection claim

**Holding:** Section 1346(a)(1) permits a refund suit by a person who, although not personally liable for the assessment against another, paid under the coercive force of a federal tax lien to free property claimed as her own and alleges that the Government wrongfully collected that payment. Section 7422(a)'s duly filed administrative claim and §6511's applicable timing requirements remain mandatory; §6511's taxpayer terminology does not implicitly exclude this timely collection claim from the express waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's express-waiver ground, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer: seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., February 25, 1992: applies its demand for unequivocal monetary consent and distinguishes its text permitting nonmonetary readings; the present refund waiver expressly authorizes monetary recovery.
- United States v. Dalm, 494 U.S. 596 (1990): applies its connected claim-and-timing requirements, while distinguishing its untimely action from this timely nonassessed claimant.
- United States v. California, April 26, 1993: preserves its refusal to create a common-law refund remedy; this action rests on the express congressional remedy it left open.
- Stahmann v. Vidal, 305 U.S. 61 (1938): applies its distinction between collection from one's property and a volunteered payment, while retaining its different statutory incidence and collector-action limits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 6511(a) allows, for a tax requiring a return, a claim within three years of filing the return or two years of payment, whichever expires later; if no return was filed, the period is two years from payment. Its separate stamp-tax clause allows three years from payment. No lateness is alleged here. Section 7422(b) permits suit whether or not payment was under protest or duress. The coercive lien and property connection identify the facts and limited plaintiff category decided; they do not create a universal protest or duress prerequisite or decide every other claimant's eligibility.

**Independent alternative holding:** Independently, a person whose claimed property and own payment are subjected to the lien-backed tax collection presented here is a person subject to an internal-revenue tax within §7701(a)(14) for the connected refund provisions, even though she is not personally liable for the assessment. This construction allows her timely administrative refund claim; it neither makes every payer a taxpayer for all Code purposes nor adjudicates the debt or lien's validity.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Ginsburg's independent taxpayer-definition ground, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Breyer: five Justices; Stone-Zsela and Scalia do not join.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dalm: applies the integrated treatment of waiver, claim, and timing provisions without converting its late-claim holding into a decision about this plaintiff category.
- Nordic Village: applies clear-consent analysis to the actual refund text and its connected definition; no general jurisdictional or equitable label supplies consent.

##### Distinct third-party mechanisms do not foreclose this refund action

**Holding:** The cited quiet-title, wrongful-levy, and substitute-fund provisions do not make those mechanisms exclusive of this §1346(a)(1) collection claim or require their exhaustion before her duly claimed refund suit. Their own triggers and restrictions remain in force: a lien is not a levy, discharge into a substitute fund is permissive and requires an agreement, and the fund remedies presuppose the corresponding fund.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's third-party-remedies ground, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer: seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dalm: preserves the statutory refund-claim requirement without adding an exhaustion duty for quiet-title, levy, or substitute-fund proceedings.
- United States v. California: preserves the distinction between an express remedy and one newly created by a court; the practical limitations of other remedies corroborate statutory construction but do not create consent.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding is jurisdictional. Lien validity and priority, purchaser status and consideration, the effect and scope of any binding Government concession, wrongfulness of collection, payment components, and recoverable amount remain for further proceedings as necessary. No interest, fees, damages, or unrestricted challenge to Rabin's underlying assessment is awarded or authorized by this decision.

Section 2410 permits quiet-title proceedings against a claimed federal lien, without automatically creating a refund action after payment. Section 7426(a)(1) requires a levy; none occurred here. Section 6325(b)(3) permits discharge of sold property upon an agreement holding the proceeds in a substitute fund with the same lien priority; it neither requires an agreement nor establishes that Williams obtained one. Sections 7426(a)(3) and (b)(4) presuppose such a fund. Section 7426(c)'s conclusive presumption that the assessment is valid remains confined to proceedings under that section and is not silently transferred to §1346.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Refund jurisdiction is affirmed, 7–2, leaving the Ninth Circuit's further-proceedings remand in force. Lien validity and priority, purchaser status, any binding concession, wrongfulness, payment components and amount remain as necessary. No refund, interest, fees, damages or unrestricted challenge to Rabin's assessment is awarded.

### State-tax refund procedures

#### Reich v. Collins

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-908.
**Decided:** December 6, 1994.

##### A State may not withdraw its apparent postpayment remedy after payment

**Holding:** When a State holds out a clear postpayment remedy for illegally assessed and collected taxes, including voluntary payments, due process forbids withdrawing that remedy after payment for the constitutional invalidity asserted and then relying on the taxpayer's failure to pursue a different prepayment route. Georgia must afford Reich a clear and certain opportunity for the refund or constitutionally adequate equivalent relief due under federal law, subject to ordinary proof of payment and amount; it may prospectively establish an adequate prepayment system with fair notice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), unanimous.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- McKesson Corp. v. Division of Alcoholic Beverages and Tobacco, 496 U.S. 18 (1990): applies its clear-and-certain-remedy requirement to withdrawal of an apparently available postpayment route; adequate prepayment systems and lawful remedial choices remain permissible.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993): applies the distinction between the federal rule governing open direct review and the lawful remedy; no automatic refund or reopening of final judgments follows.
- Barker v. Kansas, 503 U.S. 594 (1992): preserves its separate reservation of refund questions; substantive pension-tax invalidity does not fix the amount or form of relief.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The substantive tax invalidity is already accepted. The Court neither declares every Georgia prepayment avenue inadequate nor converts discretionary stays into guaranteed stays. It creates no general government-estoppel doctrine, exemption from clearly announced reasonable requirements, fixed refund, interest award, or reopening of final judgments. Prospective procedural changes remain permissible with adequate review and fair notice.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Georgia Supreme Court's denial of adequate backward-looking relief is reversed and remanded for a clear and certain lawful remedial opportunity. No refund amount, interest award or reopening of final judgments is ordered.

### Tax Court refund lookback periods

#### Commissioner v. Lundy, 516 U.S. 235

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1785.

**Decided:** January 17, 1996.

##### The notice-date hypothetical selects and measures the payment window

**Holding:** Under §6512(b)(3)(B), a hypothetical refund claim filed on the deficiency-notice date both selects and measures the applicable §6511(b)(2) payment lookback. For the ordinary withholding claim here, no return existed at that date, so the two-year window applies; a later return cannot bring earlier payments into that window.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices.

**Material application:** No return existed at the September 26, 1990 deficiency notice; withholding deemed paid April 15, 1988 lies outside the two-year window. Section 6511(a) ordinarily uses three years after the return or two years after payment, whichever ends later, and two years after payment if no return is filed. Section 6511(b)(2)(A) covers payments within three years plus any filing-extension period before a claim within the three-year period; subparagraph (B) instead uses two years. Section 6512(b)(3)(B)'s hypothetical supplies a claim, not a return.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Deficiency jurisdiction is distinct from refund authority. Section 6512(b)(3)(A)'s postnotice-payment route and (C)'s qualifying actual-prenotice-claim route remain separate. No filing extension, assessment-extension agreement, special-period item or qualifying alternative is established. The Court does not classify every tax deadline as jurisdictional or decide every nonfiler's rights.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand; these withholding payments cannot be refunded under the presented §6512(b)(3)(B) route. Remaining deficiency computations remain available; no fixed refund or separate remedy is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Dalm's statutory-condition rule and Williams's distinct payer-eligibility holding remain. Reich's state-remedy protection is distinguished from Congress's Tax Court mechanism.

### Firearm taxation and registration

#### United States v. Hale, judgment below 978 F.2d 1016

**Docket or dockets:** Eighth Circuit No. 91-3830; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

**Decided:** February 12, 1996.

##### The registration prohibition has an independent tax-administration basis

**Holding:** Congress may require registration within a genuine firearm making-and-transfer tax system and prohibit receipt or possession of a covered firearm not registered to its possessor when that prohibition supports assessment, collection and tracing of taxable transactions. This independent taxing and necessary-administration ground defeats the presented power objection to the three §5861(d) convictions, without deciding prosecution for nonregistration of a firearm that could not lawfully be approved or taxed.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight rationale joins. Stone-Zsela joins the judgment only.

**Material application:** Section 5811 taxes transfers at $200, or $5 for an any-other-weapon under §5845(e), payable by the transferor; §5821 taxes making at $200, payable by the maker, subject to applicable exemptions not established here. Under §5841 the transferor registers a transferred firearm to the transferee. Sections 5812 and 5822 require advance approval and denial if the transfer, receipt or possession, or making or possession, respectively, would violate law. Section 5861(d) concerns registration to the possessor, not a universal late-application duty.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No unlawful-registration-impossibility predicate, refused application or exemption is established; no remand to construct that different claim is ordered. An additional commerce ground is unnecessary.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Sonzinsky sustains genuine taxation and supporting registration despite regulatory effects; Miller rejects the presented general police-power objection; Freed explains lawful-transfer registration without deciding later §922(o). Staples's knowledge requirement remains distinct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The three §5861(d) convictions remain affirmed; no further proceedings follow from the power objection.

### Export-transit insurance

#### United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843

**Docket or dockets:** 95-591
**Decided:** June 10, 1996

##### May a generally applicable federal insurance tax reach insurance of goods in export transit?

**Holding:** Nondiscrimination does not remove a federal tax on goods in export transit from the Export Clause's prohibition. Under Thames & Mersey, 26 U.S.C. §4371 cannot tax the premiums for the export-transit insurance at issue; the rule does not exempt every activity or expense indirectly affecting exports.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer; six of eight participants. Stevens did not participate.

**Material application:** The policies attached to identified outward shipments, and premiums reflected cargo value and shipment-specific risk. Risk-sensitive pricing did not distinguish the insurance category already protected. The challenged 1975–1984 assessments remained governed by the period-specific tax provisions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary pre-export burdens and genuinely distinct or tangential transactions remain outside this holding. The Court reserved comprehensive classification of other services and announced no facial invalidation of §4371, new exemption, or new review-scope restriction. Lebron permits consideration of an included argument without requiring its decision; the Government's presentation supported prudential reservation of the separate goods/services inquiry.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Federal Circuit affirmed, 6–2; the refund judgment stands within the preserved claim and amount, with no new computation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Thames & Mersey Marine Insurance Co. v. United States, 237 U.S. 19, is retained and applied; nondiscrimination alone does not displace it. Fairbank v. United States, 181 U.S. 283, and United States v. Hvoslef, 237 U.S. 1, protect the export transaction through its integral documents and carriage arrangements. Michelin Tire Corp. v. Wages, 423 U.S. 276, concerns state taxation outside transit; Washington Stevedoring, 435 U.S. 734, concerns services and reserves direct goods-in-transit taxation. Itel Containers addresses a distinct local container transaction; Jefferson Lines and Fulton retain transaction-specific Commerce Clause rules rather than changing the federal Export Clause. No precedent is overruled.

### Estate-administration expenses and beneficial-interest valuation

#### Commissioner v. Estate of Hubert

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1402.
**Decided:** March 18, 1997.

##### Whether income-funded administration expenses invariably reduce the estate-tax deduction

**Holding:** Administration expenses charged to postmortem income of a marital bequest do not automatically reduce its estate-tax deduction dollar for dollar. Under the then-operative §20.2056(b)-4(a), valuation must reflect a material limitation on the spouse’s income rights; the effect on the qualifying beneficial interest governs rather than the income-allocation label alone.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Kennedy), joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; seven Justices directly adopt both holdings, without a fractured-rationale synthesis.

**Material application:** The regulation’s net-value direction and material-limitation qualification both apply. Its administration-income example permits application rather than demanding automatic subtraction; neither lawful allocation nor exclusion of postmortem income from the gross estate proves immateriality. The Commissioner’s categorical interpretation would remove the operative qualification.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No valuation-date-only theory, complete calculation method, numerical threshold, percentage safe harbor or mandatory factor list is adopted. Principal-funded expenses retain their separate reduction treatment, and §642(g) and distinct qualification rules remain unresolved. The charitable deduction receives the same disposition because the parties treat this issue alike, not because §§2055 and 2056 or their regulations are identical.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Thomas Jefferson University and Auer require weight for a fair, considered and permissible own-regulation interpretation, but not removal of an operative qualification. Smiley preserves delegated interpretive authority within statutory limits without supplying a tax formula. Revenue Ruling 93-48 informs the deferred-interest comparison without deciding every administration expense or wholly withdrawing Revenue Ruling 73-98.

##### Whether the Commissioner’s burden and preserved categorical theory permit reversal

**Holding:** Tax Court Rule 142(a) places the burden on the Commissioner for this expense adjustment omitted from the original deficiency notice. Because the Commissioner pursued inevitable reduction rather than maintaining a quantitative-materiality ground against the Tax Court’s finding, failure of that legal theory supplies no basis to reverse or reopen proof.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Kennedy), the same seven-Justice coalition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This does not reverse the ordinary taxpayer burden, make total estate value the decisive denominator or deem approximately $1.5 million immaterial in every estate. Trust qualification was stipulated and is not reopened.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment sustaining both deductions is affirmed without a new calculation, hearing, deficiency amount or evidentiary opportunity.

### Income exclusions and erroneous refunds

#### O'Gilvie v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 95-966 and 95-977.

**Decided:** December 10, 1996.

For the other questions in O'Gilvie v. United States, Nos. 95-966 and 95-977, decided December 10, 1996, see Federal Courts - Appellate jurisdiction and notices of appeal (The Government's appeal in the father's action was timely).

##### These noncompensatory punitive receipts are taxable

**Holding:** Under the applicable pre-1989 §104(a)(2), the taxpayer must satisfy both the tort-or-tort-type-right requirement and the independent requirement that the particular damages be received on account of personal injuries or sickness; amounts attributable to §213 deductions allowed in a prior year remain excepted. The Kansas punitive component serves punishment and deterrence rather than compensation for injury, fails the independent causal requirement, and is included in gross income under §61.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Compensatory receipts, mixed allocations, materially different payment functions and later statutory texts remain outside the holding; Burke supplies no general remedial-menu or health-only test.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Schleier's receipt-specific causal rule is extended to this wholly punitive Kansas award, without pretending its reserved question was already decided. Burke's fractured judgment remains without a general controlling statutory rationale.

##### Receipt of the refund checks starts the children's recovery period

**Holding:** For these refund checks, a refund is made for §§6532(b) and 7405(b) when the taxpayers receive payment, rather than when the Government merely writes or mails the checks. Receipt on July 9, 1990 makes the recovery actions filed July 9, 1992 timely under the two-year period.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six Justices. The three tax dissenters do not reach this defense.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule concerns these received checks, not every payment mechanism. No equitable-tolling rule or new deadline exception is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Wurts's payment-and-recovery reasoning is applied to the receipt question it did not itself decide; administrative allowance remains distinct from payment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm both dockets, 6-3: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine find the father's appeal timely; only the six tax-majority Justices reach the children's refund defense. No amount, additional recovery or required recomputation is ordered.

### Refund limitations and equitable tolling

#### United States v. Brockamp and United States v. Scott

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1225.
**Decided:** February 18, 1997.

##### The enacted filing scheme rebuts nonstatutory equitable suspension

**Holding:** Section 6511's integrated filing, allowance and amount scheme does not permit the requested nonstatutory equitable extension for mental incapacity or extraordinary inability to file. Assuming Irwin's rebuttable presumption and the taxpayers' private-collector analogy apply, the scheme rebuts that presumption; incapacity and absence of prejudice supply no additional period.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the Opinion of the Court (Breyer), unanimous.

**Material application:** Return taxes use three years from actual return filing or two years from payment, whichever is later, and two years from payment without a return; stamp-tax claims separately use three years from payment. Section 6511(b)(1) independently prohibits late allowance. Scott's incapacity is accepted, while Brockamp's remains disputed; neither requires a new tolling trial.

**Independent alternative holding:** Even assuming an otherwise timely return-based claim under §6511(a), §6511(b)(2)'s separately prescribed payment window is not enlarged by the requested mental-incapacity or extraordinary-inability tolling. These tax payments fall outside the applicable ordinary windows, including any supported return-filing extension; this amount limit independently defeats the ordinary tax-refund recoveries without deciding whether McGill's disputed remittance was instead a deposit.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Part II of Breyer's opinion, unanimous; the payment-window ground is expressly independently sufficient.

**Limits, remedy, transition, or precedent treatment:** Section 6511(b)(2)(A) reaches payments within three years plus a supported return-filing extension; its stamp-tax proviso uses three years. Subparagraph (B) uses two years for other claims, and (C) measures allowances without claims from the allowance date. Return-filing extensions differ from assessment agreements under (c). No qualifying agreement, special-period item or mitigation ground is established. The Court announces no universal jurisdictional classification or ruling on fraud, governmental inducement, separate recoupment or constitutional disability claims. Irwin is assumed applicable and rebutted by this scheme; Dalm, Lundy, Williams, Henderson and O'Gilvie retain their distinct consent, route, window, service and payment-trigger holdings.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both appellate judgments are reversed and remanded. Scott's $30,096 award and dependent interest cannot stand on tolling, and earlier interest is not reopened. The lawful consequences of Brockamp's preserved deposit alternative remain for appellate consideration under Part III's eight-Associate rationale; Stone-Zsela agrees with that disposition only. No deposit classification, refund amount or unconditional restoration of the district judgment is ordered.

### Export loading and compensatory service charges

#### United States v. United States Shoe Corp.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-372.

**Decided:** 1998-03-31.

##### The value-based charge on export loading is an export tax, not a compensatory service fee

**Holding:** The Export Clause prohibits this Harbor Maintenance Tax as applied to exports because its ad valorem assessment does not fairly measure services or facilities used by the exporter. A genuine compensatory charge may use reasonable administrative approximations, but dedicated revenue and a general connection to useful harbor work do not convert a cargo-value export tax into a permissible fee.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg’s opinion of the Court, joined by all eight other Justices, unanimously controls the merits rule and export-refund affirmance.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Import and domestic applications, separately lawful inspection, storage, wharfage and facility charges, and taxes on legally independent transactions remain outside the decision. No benefit ratio or finding that each payment was excessive is required to identify this structural defect.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The existing export-refund judgment is affirmed unanimously for the claim and payments before the Court; no new calculation, interest or limitations ruling, or disposition of another taxpayer’s claim follows. The exclusive-jurisdiction holding appears under Federal Courts.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **United States v. International Business Machines Corp.:** Applied to reject nondiscrimination as a defense to an actual federal export tax; its reservation for genuinely distinct service transactions remains intact.
- **Pace v. Burgess:** Applied to preserve genuine compensation for governmental services and safeguards through reasonable approximation; a service label or dedicated fund alone is insufficient.
- **Northwest Airlines, Inc. v. County of Kent:** Distinguished as construction of the Anti-Head Tax Act; its facility-use analysis supplies an analogy, not a substitute constitutional test.

### Reserve-strengthening transition rules

#### Atlantic Mutual Insurance Co. v. Commissioner of Internal Revenue

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-147.

**Decided:** 1998-04-21.

##### The transition permits Treasury's specified net-addition rule

**Holding:** Section 1023(e)(3)(B) does not confine reserve strengthening to increases caused by changes in computational methods or assumptions; Treasury may implement the fresh-start exclusion through the specified net-addition rule. That permission depends on the actual transitional statute and its interpretive room, not a general power to deny generous deductions or protect revenue.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part I, joined by all eight other Justices, supplies a unanimous controlling statutory proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The statute permits this construction without making it uniquely correct or authorizing general revenue-based denial of deductions. No misconduct, artificial reserve or changed-method prerequisite is imposed.

##### The reasonable construction receives controlling deference

**Independent alternative holding:** Within the interpretive room left by this transition provision, Treasury's reasonable specified net-addition construction controls under Chevron. Deference supplies no authority to contradict a clear statutory command, disregard the regulation's exceptions or reopen unrelated final tax determinations.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Souter's Part II has the eight Associate Justices' direct joins and independently controls this rationale.

##### The calculation retains its exceptions and timing conditions

**Holding:** For an unpaid-loss reserve for an accident year before 1986, strengthening equals the excess of the reserve at the end of the taxable year beginning in 1986 over the preceding year-end reserve reduced by attributable claims and loss-adjustment payments during that 1986-beginning year; the amount by which that reserve is reduced as a result of reinsurance ceded in that year counts as a loss payment. Subject to the exceptions and timing conditions below, net strengthening is excluded from the fresh start and treated as occurring in the first taxable year beginning after December 31, 1986, without an additional requirement of unreasonable reserves or taxpayer discretion.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part III, joined by all eight other Justices, controls the rule's application and limits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The calculation is confined to the specified pre-1986 accident-year reserve and transitional taxable years. No exception is established on this record; no new accounting facts, alternative hypothetical calculation, unrelated liability or avoidance finding is adjudicated.

The regulation measures the specified reserve rather than wrongdoing. It excludes a loss amount reported from a mandatory state or federal assigned-risk pool when that amount is not discretionary with the taxpayer. It also excludes payments concerning reinsurance assumed during a taxable year beginning in 1986 and additions for a line of business's reinsurance assumed then, but only up to a hypothetical reserve calculated with the assumptions, other than assumed interest rates, used for that line's reinsurance assumed in a taxable year beginning in 1985. Reinsurance assumed or ceded in a 1985-beginning year is treated as occurring in the succeeding year if the appropriate unpaid-loss reserve is not adjusted until then. Section 845 separately applies when avoidance of the strengthening limitation is one purpose of a reinsurance transaction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment sustaining the challenged construction is affirmed unanimously; the objection supplies no basis to defeat the reported deficiency. No penalty, new hearing or independent recalculation is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Hill is applied for the distinction between a federal tax category and commercial accounting labels; it supplies no general ban on multiple tax benefits.
- Sweet Home preserves statutory-boundary analysis and the separately sufficient deference ground within actual ambiguity; its two grounds remain distinct.
- MCI requires enacted authority before deference and remains a bar to replacing clear statutory commands with agency policy.
- Chevron and Smiley govern the eight-Justice deference rationale for this permissible classification; neither makes the agency's reading uniquely correct.
- Cottage Savings supports reasonable administration of the tax laws within their statutory terms.
- Regions Hospital supplies a statute-specific comparison and retains its closed-payment finality limit; this decision creates no general reopening power.
- Auer is distinguished because interpretation of an agency's own regulation is not the statutory-delegation question presented here.

### Protected judgment liens and insolvent estates

#### United States v. Estate of Romani

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1613.

**Decided:** 1998-04-29.

##### The specific tax-lien protection governs this insolvent-estate conflict

**Holding:** When a judgment lien on identified property qualifies for §6323(a)’s protection against the Government’s later federal tax lien, §3713(a)(1)(B) does not permit the same tax claim to be paid first from that property merely because the deceased debtor’s estate in the administrator’s custody cannot pay all debts. This rule gives effect to the particular tax-lien allocation; it neither repeals §3713 nor exempts every tax claim or every earlier secured debt from federal priority.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela’s opinion is joined in full by all eight other Justices; the statutory rule and application control unanimously.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The §6323 concession does not establish additional title, possession or perfection required for a distinct §3713 exception, which is unnecessary to decide. Unsecured creditors, other protected classes and other property retain their own predicates; no freestanding equitable exception or implied repeal follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The authorized transfer to the judgment creditor is affirmed unanimously against the federal-priority objection. No distribution amount, representative personal liability or other claim is adjudicated. Living-debtor triggers and the title-11 exclusion remain unchanged.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **United States v. McDermott:** Distinguished. Federal notice before acquisition retains priority at that scope; it does not defeat this lien already attached to existing property.
- **United States v. New Britain:** Its lienor, property and amount requirements remain; they do not collapse §6323 protection into the distinct §3713 perfection question.
- **Morton v. Mancari:** Applied to read the specific and general provisions together without unnecessary implied repeal.
- **United States v. Randall:** Applied by analogy to the displacement of general priority at a concrete conflict with a specific statutory distribution rule.
- **United States v. Kimbell Foods, Inc.:** Its account of the tax-lien scheme supports the statutory context; its consensual-lending holding does not itself resolve insolvent-estate distribution.
- **Thelusson v. Smith; Conard v. Atlantic Insurance Co.:** Read together; the unperfected interest discussed there does not establish a categorical answer for every later protected real-property lien.
- **United States v. Key; United States v. Emory:** Distinguished as unsecured-claim settings that do not resolve this protected-property collision.
- **Holywell Corp. v. Smith; United States v. Hill:** Preserved for their distinct filing/payment and tax-classification rules, without converting tax liability into universal priority. No precedent is overruled.


### Federal contracts on a reservation do not themselves exempt the contractor

#### Arizona Department of Revenue v. Blaze Construction Co.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-1536
**Decided:** 1999-03-02.

##### Federal contracts on a reservation do not themselves exempt the contractor

**Holding:** A State may impose a nondiscriminatory gross-receipts tax whose legal incidence falls on an independent nonmember contractor performing ordinary federal construction contracts on a reservation, absent applicable constitutional immunity or a controlling federal exemption. Federal payment, supervision, reservation location and benefit to tribes do not themselves supply that exemption or require Bracker/Ramah interest balancing for the federal transaction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion has all nine Justices' direct joins in this rule, its application and the stated reservations.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **United States v. New Mexico, 455 U.S. 720:** Applied; ordinary independent federal contractors do not share federal tax immunity merely because federal money pays the tax's economic burden.
- **United States v. California:** Applied to distinguish legal incidence from reimbursement and economic effect.
- **Chickasaw Nation v. Oklahoma Tax Commission:** Preserved for actual tribal incidence; its reserved nonmember questions do not create an exemption here.
- **White Mountain Apache Tribe v. Bracker, 448 U.S. 136:** Distinguished from this ordinary federal contract; its particular tribal timber-operation holding remains.
- **Ramah Navajo School Board v. Bureau of Revenue, 458 U.S. 832:** Distinguished as a tribally controlled undertaking; its holding is not extended to every federal reservation supplier.
- **Cotton Petroleum Corp. v. New Mexico, 490 U.S. 163:** Applied to reject indirect economic effect or tax/service equivalence as sufficient grounds for immunity.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary federal construction contracts do not acquire a tax exemption from reservation location. The legal taxpayer and any actual federal exemption control this transaction. Tribal-incidence immunity and the distinct law governing tribal contracts remain intact. The amount of Blaze's liability awaits resolution of preserved credits.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Reversed and remanded, 9-0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The State may apply the nondiscriminatory gross-receipts tax to these federal contracts. The state courts must address Blaze's unresolved claims for credits for taxes paid by subcontractors and suppliers. The Court fixes no net assessment and restores no excluded tribal-contract receipts.

### The levy operates as compensation taxation within §111

#### Jefferson County v. Acker

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-10; October Term 1998.
**Decided:** 1999-06-21.

##### The levy operates as compensation taxation within §111

**Holding:** The levy asserted in these collection actions operates as a tax on compensation for federal personal services within 4 U.S.C. §111, notwithstanding its occupational-license label. Section 111's consent does not authorize discrimination because of the compensation's federal source or a local requirement of permission to exercise federal office.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven directly adopt this compensation-tax rule on the jurisdictional predicate stated above.

##### The presented source-discrimination defense fails

**Holding:** On this ordinance and record, taxing the respondent judges' compensation while exempting occupations subject to other specified state licensing charges does not discriminate because their compensation comes from a federal source. The intergovernmental-immunity judgment must be reversed, with remand consistent with that determination and the reservation of the separate Compensation Clause question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, supplies seven direct votes for the source-discrimination determination and limited remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Mesa v. California:** Applies the distinct federal-defense requirement; federal office alone is insufficient and merits success is not required to establish a colorable defense.
- **Willingham v. Morgan:** Applies the protective federal-officer forum and the connection to official duties without demanding advance victory on the defense; the particular nexus remains necessary.
- **Arkansas v. Farm Credit Services of Central Arkansas:** Distinguishes its affirmative injunction/declaration action by private associations and conceded adequate state remedy. Its separation of substantive immunity from forum entitlement remains intact; no universal federal-officer exception is created.
- **Graves v. New York ex rel. O'Keefe:** Applies the rejection of categorical immunity for federal compensation taxation and the need to assess the tax's actual operation, subject to governing nondiscrimination and federal-function limits.
- **Davis v. Michigan Department of Treasury:** Applies federal-source nondiscrimination and relevant comparator analysis; it does not require identical treatment of every differently licensed occupation regardless of the reason for classification.
- **Arizona Department of Revenue v. Blaze Construction Co.:** Preserves legal-incidence analysis and the distinction from indirect economic effect; its independent-contractor result is not extended to erase the separate protection of federal judges or direct federal functions.
- **United States v. X-Citement Video and United States v. American Railway Express:** Apply the prevailing respondent's ability to renew a record-supported alternative for the same favorable judgment without seeking enlarged relief.
- **Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp.:** Preserves bounded review discretion and limits concerning distinct claims, questions, relief and missing facts; no universal duty to consider abandoned grounds follows.
- **Zafiro v. United States:** Distinguishes a petitioner's personal unpursued claim from this renewed respondent alternative; its prudential preservation rule remains.
- **AT&T v. Central Office Telephone:** Distinguishes refusal to construct an independent theory that the respondent had not advanced; this defense was adjudicated below and renewed here.
- **Dandridge v. Williams and West v. Gibson:** Preserve lawful remand for appropriate unresolved alternatives without compelling remand of this already adjudicated, record-supported discrimination question.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Effective June 21, 1999, these federal judicial officers may defend the County's work-linked collection suits in the federal forum because the office nexus and colorable federal defense are established. Section 1341 does not bar the defensive adjudication in that collector-initiated posture. The particular occupational levy is compensation taxation within §111 and the presented source-discrimination defense fails. No categorical exemption for federal judges or local power to license their duties follows. The independent Compensation Clause question and proper calculation or procedural matters remain for their lawful treatment below. The discrimination issue is resolved, not remanded for reopening. Existing limits on affirmative taxpayer suits, actual federal-function restraints, source discrimination and distinct review questions retain their force; no precedent is overruled.

**Operative remedy or transition:**

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices or qualification |
|---|---|---|---|
| Federal-officer removal | Federal forum sustained, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Souter, Thomas would return the collection actions to state court for lack of the required nexus. |
| Tax Injunction Act | No bar to adjudication of the County's collection action and defenses, 9–0 | All nine Justices | Scalia, Souter and Thomas join after accepting the Court's resolution of removal as the governing predicate. |
| Compensation-tax characterization, renewed source-discrimination defense and limited remand | Intergovernmental-immunity judgment reversed and remanded, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | O'Connor and Breyer would affirm on direct federal-function taxation; Scalia, Souter and Thomas join reversal on the Court's resolution of removal. |

The case returns for proceedings consistent with the compensation-tax and nondiscrimination holdings. The separate Compensation Clause ground remains undecided for proper treatment below. The discrimination defense has been reached and rejected; it is not reopened. The Court directs no unconditional collection, fixes no amount, supplies no new calculation finding and requires no new evidence. Any remaining calculation or procedural issue retains its own lawful scope.


For the other questions in Jefferson County v. Acker, decided 1999-06-21, at the dockets stated above, see Federal Courts.

### Alabama's franchise-tax bases discriminate against foreign corporations

#### South Central Bell Telephone Co. v. Alabama

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-2045.
**Decided:** 1999-03-23.

##### Alabama's franchise-tax bases discriminate against foreign corporations

**Holding:** Alabama's franchise-tax structure discriminates against interstate commerce by taxing foreign corporations on actual capital employed in the State while allowing comparable domestic corporations to use a par-value base that may be set well below actual value. The favorable domestic base cannot be justified by comparing nominal rates or tax labels while disregarding the actual competitive burden.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court, Parts I–V.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Oregon Waste's origin-discrimination rule, West Lynn Creamery's practical comparison and Fulton's treatment of corporate tax preferences are applied. Tracy's distinct actual-service comparison is distinguished, not displaced.

##### The domestic shares tax does not establish a compensatory defense

**Holding:** A compensatory-tax defense requires an identified actual intrastate burden, an interstate charge that roughly approximates and does not exceed that burden, and substantially equivalent taxable events capable of functioning as mutually exclusive proxies. Alabama's domestic shares tax reaches a different event and does not establish the equivalence needed to compensate for the discriminatory foreign-corporation franchise tax.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court, Parts I–V.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Oregon Waste and Fulton supply the cumulative actual-burden, nonexcess and equivalent-event requirements. Their allowance for genuinely complementary taxes survives; fiscal correlation alone remains insufficient.

**Independent alternative holding:** Alabama also fails to establish an actual matching domestic burden and a roughly equivalent, nonexcessive interstate burden by invoking other domestic taxes or an aggregate fiscal balance. This separate deficiency defeats the compensatory defense even apart from the absence of equivalent taxable events.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court, Part IV.

##### Alabama must provide adequate relief through lawful state remedial choices

**Holding:** The judgment is reversed and the case remanded for constitutionally adequate relief under state law. When payment was required before an adequate opportunity to contest the tax, McKesson requires meaningful backward-looking correction; independently, a State may not withdraw a clearly held-out postpayment remedy after payment and substitute an unpursued prepayment route.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's unanimous opinion of the Court, Parts I–V.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McKesson's conditional duty of backward-looking correction and Reich's independent no-withdrawal rule govern the remand. Fulton confirms the distinction between invalidating a discriminatory tax and choosing its precise lawful remedy.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No exact refund, replacement assessment, severability ruling or particular taxpayer's satisfaction of payment and preservation conditions is decided. Ordinary valid preclusion and genuinely equivalent compensatory taxes remain possible under their own prerequisites. The Court does not reopen otherwise binding judgments or decide a new federal damages action.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court determines no exact refund, replacement assessment, severability result or taxpayer's satisfaction of payment and preservation conditions. It does not reopen binding judgments or decide a new federal damages action.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for constitutionally adequate relief through lawful state mechanisms; the conditional backward-looking duty and independent prohibition on withdrawing a held-out postpayment remedy remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

McKesson supplies authority to review the federal questions in a State's own judgment and the conditional duty of backward-looking relief. Richards defeats the asserted nonparty bar while preserving genuine representative and party preclusion. Oregon Waste, West Lynn Creamery and Fulton govern the practical tax comparison; Oregon Waste and Fulton require all three compensatory predicates and preserve genuinely equivalent taxes. Tracy's regulated-service distinction does not control this classification. Reich supplies the independent prohibition against withdrawing a clearly held-out postpayment remedy. No Supreme Court precedent is overruled.

For the other questions and complete operative scope in South Central Bell Telephone Co. v. Alabama, 1999-03-23, No. 97-2045, see Federal Courts.

### Tax liens and state-created property rights

#### Drye v. United States, 528 U.S. 49 (1999)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-1101.
**Decided:** December 7, 1999.

##### Whether a federal tax lien reaches an existing power to accept or redirect an inheritance

**Holding:** A legally effective state-created power to accept an inheritance or channel its value to another by disclaimer is a right to property within 26 U.S.C. §6321 on this record. State law defines the control, but federal law determines whether the lien reaches it; state relation back cannot erase control that already existed for federal lien purposes.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not reach a disclaimer completed before legally cognizable control arises, every prior expectancy, a spendthrift-trust interest, valuation, the recipient's liability, or any competing priority.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the lien judgment on the established state-created control. No new valuation, distribution or recipient-liability ruling is entered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** United States v. National Bank of Commerce is applied for state definition of rights followed by federal lien classification. United States v. Irvine is applied by analogy to the distinction between local disclaimer effects and federal tax consequences; its gift-tax reasonable-time exception is not imported into §6321. United States v. Estate of Romani preserves specific statutory priority protections and is distinguished from the present attachment question. No precedent is overruled.

### Refund payment dates and recoverable amount

#### Baral v. United States, 528 U.S. 431

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-1667.

**Decided:** February 22, 2000.

##### Withholding and estimated income tax have their statutory payment dates

**Holding:** For sections 6511 and 6512, income tax actually deducted and withheld under chapter 24 is deemed paid by the income recipient on the fifteenth day of the fourth month following the taxable year for which it is allowable as a section 31 credit; estimated income tax is deemed paid on the last day prescribed for filing that year's return without regard to filing extensions. A later return or assessment does not postpone either date.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, joined in full by all eight Associate Justices, supplies nine direct votes for each proposition; no fractured rationale is involved.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decision does not classify remittances outside section 6513 or displace the distinct treatment of a genuine deposit.

##### A timely claim remains subject to the separate recoverable-amount ceiling

**Holding:** For a claim within section 6511(a)'s return-based period, section 6511(b)(2)(A) limits a credit or refund to tax paid during the three years immediately preceding the claim plus any extension for filing the return. Compliance with the claim-filing period does not bring payments outside that window within the recoverable amount.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, joined in full by all eight Associate Justices, supplies nine direct votes for each proposition; no fractured rationale is involved.

**Material application:** The withholding and estimated payment are deemed paid April 15, 1989. The June 1, 1993 claim's three-year window, enlarged by the four-month filing extension, begins February 1, 1990; neither payment falls within it. The extension enlarges the amount window without changing the estimated-payment date.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 6511(a) timeliness is conceded, not adjudicated. No Tax Court hypothetical, incapacity, fraud, inducement, special period or general jurisdictional classification is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The denial of the requested $1,175 credit is affirmed because the recoverable amount is zero, not because the claim was filed late. No refund, new assessment or further hearing is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Rosenman v. United States: distinguished as a suspense-account estate-tax deposit; its deposit rule survives for its proper setting.
- Commissioner v. Lundy: applied for the distinction between claim timing and payment lookback; its notice-date hypothetical does not govern this actual claim.
- United States v. Brockamp: applied for the independent recovery-amount ceiling; its reservation of genuine deposits is preserved.



### Religious objections to tax collection and additions

#### Adams v. Commissioner of Internal Revenue

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-798; Third Circuit No. 98-7200.

**Decided:** March 6, 2000.

##### Whether a conceded collection interest alone disposes of the federal RFRA defense
