- Rose is applied to the first federal merits opportunity following complete-exhaustion procedure; exhaustion itself remains required on its actual terms.
- Stewart is used as a limited first-review analogy, preserving its presented, premature and expressly reserved Ford-claim boundary. The additional-claim exhaustion-return rule is adopted here, not attributed to that earlier holding.
- Lonchar is applied to require actual statutory or Rule predicates for dismissal; no general equitable disapproval or automatic entitlement to a hearing replaces them.
- Sanders and McCleskey retain their structured abuse inquiries. Their pleading and excuse principles do not supply the missing successive category for this exhaustion return.
- Weeks retains independent statutory eligibility, constitutional claim and relief inquiries; a successful procedural challenge alone grants no writ.
- Michael Wayne Williams retains applicant-attributed failure and actual hearing prerequisites; an undeveloped record here does not itself trigger a hearing bar or compel a hearing.
- Terry Williams retains only its adopted statutory propositions, separate constitutional entitlement and remedy. Neither four-Justice exclusive clause construction is imported, and its post-enactment application does not change Slack's district-law temporal premise.
- Edwards retains the actual adequate, independent and properly applied state-default condition for an assistance claim used as cause, with its recognized excuses. It establishes no Nevada default and does not equate federal successive classification with state default.

### Federal habeas review of capital parole information

#### Ramdass v. Angelone, 530 U.S. 156 (2000)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-7000.
**Decided:** June 12, 2000.

##### I. State-law conviction status and federal review remain distinct

**Holding:** Virginia's definition of a qualifying conviction governs whether the unentered Domino's verdict already counted under its parole statute. Disagreement with that legal definition is not an unreasonable determination of fact under section 2254(d)(2), and a disagreement about state law alone supplies no federal custody violation under section 2254(a).

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Justice Stevens's opinion, joined by all eight other Justices, supplies nine direct votes. The appellate rejection of the District Court's factual-ground rationale remains in place.

##### III. Statutory eligibility and retroactivity require their own determinations

**Holding:** Amended section 2254(d)(1) applies to the February 14, 1997 application, and its constitutional-law benchmark is the holdings clearly established on November 4, 1994. The lower court's categorical treatment of the asserted completed-adjudication claim as necessarily an impermissible extension under section 2254(d)(1) and Teague cannot stand without examining that actual claim; neither statutory eligibility nor retroactive availability is established by today's remand.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Stevens's Opinion of the Court has nine supporters for applicability, the cutoff and the common statutory propositions. Part III's rejection of the categorical statutory and Teague grounds and remand is joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Williams v. Taylor (Terry Williams) requires independent work by the contrary-to and unreasonable-application clauses. Conflict with a governing legal rule can satisfy the first without identical facts, as can an opposite result on materially indistinguishable facts. Unreasonable application is an objective legal inquiry, and a rule's generality does not alone exclude it from clearly established law. These common holdings do not adopt either competing exclusive allocation of the clauses or a universal refusal-to-extend rule.

O'Dell makes the confined effective-response protection available against Teague; it does not classify every proposed entitlement to predicted events as old. A claim within that protection receives its existing treatment. A materially different proposed rule requires its own finality and exception analysis if those questions become necessary. The Court adds no Teague exception and substitutes no retroactivity ruling for statutory eligibility. Allridge and O'Dell are present-law authorities, not constitutional holdings retroactively placed in the November 1994 benchmark. Williams supplies the present statutory construction without moving that benchmark.

##### IV. The mandate returns unresolved predicates without awarding sentencing relief

**Holding:** The lower courts must determine whether the Domino's proceeding had reached a completed substantive adjudication with only entry remaining, whether the actual opportunity conveyed the required accurate information, whether section 2254(d)(1) permits relief under the November 4, 1994 holdings, and any material Teague question. Only if those conditions permit relief must the error's effect be decided; the Court enters no present writ or sentence relief and leaves rejection of the factual-ground rationale intact.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of Stevens's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices. All nine agree that disagreement with the state-law definition supplies no factual-ground relief.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court makes no whole-record harm finding, adopts no structural-error classification, and chooses no standard, burden or treatment of uncertainty for this distinct collateral error category. Brecht, O'Neal, Roy and Calderon v. Coleman retain their own defined applications; their differing settings do not automatically supply a universal rule here. An available violation requires the applicable effect inquiry before relief. The return permits further judicial consideration, but compels no new evidence and reinstates no prior sentencing clock. The conviction, unrelated claims and independent lawful custody remain undisturbed.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment does not reinstate the District Court's sentencing order or vacate the sentence. It orders no present writ, new penalty proceeding, evidentiary hearing or other evidentiary command, deadline, release, stay or execution-related relief. The conviction and unrelated claims remain untouched. Denial on a properly supported independent ground remains available on remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **O'Dell v. Netherland:** Applies its confined retroactivity protection and separation of that protection from actual predicates and relief. Neither its old-rule holding nor its alternative watershed holding creates a new exception for predicted future adjudications; its later date remains intact for statutory review.
- **Williams v. Taylor (Terry Williams):** Applies the common holdings on independent clauses, governing legal rules, the state-decision cutoff and objective unreasonableness. Neither four-Justice exclusive general allocation becomes controlling here.
- **Weeks v. Angelone:** Applies the separation of constitutional error, statutory permission and causal entitlement; no conditional separate-writing effect proposal becomes Court law.
- **Teague v. Lane:** Preserves the distinction between dictated applications and new procedural rules and the applicable exception inquiry when material; it is not interchangeable with section 2254(d)(1).
- **Brecht v. Abrahamson:** Preserves State-borne Chapman review for preserved Doyle error, where the prosecution uses silence after Miranda warnings to impeach the defendant; no automatic extension to this parole-information claim follows.
- **O'Neal v. McAninch:** Preserves State-borne Chapman review for its preserved personal-intent instructional and prosecutorial-argument category; it does not establish a universal collateral-error standard.
- **California v. Roy:** Preserves the extension to a preserved personal-intent omission without accompanying prosecutorial argument; it supplies no automatic capital-information extension here.
- **Calderon v. Coleman:** Preserves State-borne Chapman review for its properly available capital-selection commutation-instruction claim and the distinction between error and actual effect; that defined extension does not decide this different parole-information category.

For the substantive due-process rule concerning a completed adjudication awaiting entry, see Ramdass v. Angelone, No. 99-7000, June 12, 2000, under Criminal Procedure, capital parole information.

### Savings-clause review and residual writ authority

#### United States v. Barrett

**Docket or dockets:** First Circuit No. 96-2355, 178 F.3d 34; no Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** May 22, 2000.

##### I. An omitted available claim does not establish an inadequate section 2255 remedy

**Holding:** Section 2255 is not inadequate or ineffective merely because a prisoner omitted an available claim in an earlier merits-adjudicated collateral proceeding and cannot satisfy the separate successive-motion conditions. Barrett's previously available Jencks Act claim cannot proceed under section 2241 on that ground; the Court leaves unresolved the exact savings treatment of an intervening substantive rule showing noncriminal conduct where no earlier reasonable opportunity existed.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Ginsburg's opinion has all nine Justices' direct joins in this necessary rule, application and bounded affirmance.

**Material application:** The Jencks Act claim was available during an earlier collateral proceeding decided on its merits. Its mention in a reply did not amend the pleaded Brady claim. Excluding testimony for a Jencks violation would not itself establish noncriminal conduct or factual innocence.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The grant reaches the independently reviewable collateral judgment identified by the First Circuit, not its concurrent authorization refusal. Felker, Triestman and Dorsainvil preserve the prohibition on ordinary review of that separate refusal; those cases establish no affirmative savings-clause test. The Court can therefore decide the asserted route without affirming authorization denial or deciding the assumed custodian forum. No intervening statutory-innocence claimant is before it. Meaningful-review problems on materially different facts, a genuinely independent authorized remedy and substantive Jencks liability remain unresolved.

##### II. The All Writs Act supplies no substitute for the failed statutory route

**Holding:** Section 1651 does not authorize Barrett to evade the statutory procedure governing this conviction challenge merely because he cannot now obtain relief under section 2255. A genuinely independent remedy must satisfy its own authority, forum and procedural conditions; none is established or ordered here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer with its author Ginsburg, eight Justices. Stone-Zsela supports this component's judgment without joining its ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decision rejects this substitute use of section 1651; it does not abolish independent writs or decide their hypothetical availability, and it does not reach the underlying evidentiary merits.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The ordinary collateral dismissal is affirmed. The distinct successive-motion authorization refusal is neither affirmed nor vacated. No writ, transfer, release, hearing, reopening or new merits adjudication is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Felker v. Turpin:** Applied to preserve the distinction between barred authorization review and an independently invoked collateral channel; retained jurisdiction remains separate from entitlement to relief.
- **Triestman v. United States** and **In re Dorsainvil:** Their authorization-review prohibitions remain intact; neither supplied a Supreme Court savings-clause holding.
- **Bousley v. United States:** Applied to separate substantive offense error, procedural default and factual innocence; exclusion of testimony alone is not factual innocence.
- **Schlup v. Delo:** Its evidentiary innocence gateway remains distinct from statutory successive-motion requirements.
- **Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal:** Distinguished because its actually presented premature claim remained reserved for first merits consideration.
- **Slack v. McDaniel:** Distinguished because its exhaustion return followed no first federal merits adjudication; it does not excuse Barrett's omission here.
- **Carlisle v. United States:** Applied to the residual-power boundary without eliminating genuinely independent remedies on their own predicates.

### Habeas relief after a missing-element determination

#### Fiore v. White, 531 U.S. 225

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-942
**Decided:** January 9, 2001.

##### Constitutional error does not dispense with applicable habeas prerequisites

**Holding:** The Third Circuit's rejection of the constitutional claim is reversed and the case remanded to resolve any properly preserved, actually undecided statutory prerequisite to habeas relief under the applicable post-April 24, 1996 regime. If those prerequisites permit relief, the habeas order must remove both invalid no-permit convictions and end custody attributable to them, preserving independent convictions and lawful custody.

**Proposition-level authority:** Unanimous per curiam opinion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The later-filed application is subject to the applicable post-April 24, 1996 amendments. Section 2254(b) requires exhaustion, no available state corrective process, or circumstances making that process ineffective. Nonexhaustion permits merits denial but does not require it; only counsel's express waiver waives exhaustion. If the claim received a state merits adjudication, section 2254(d) applies its independent contrary-to and unreasonable-application inquiries objectively using this Court's holdings at that adjudication. The separate factual-review provisions retain their own conditions. The Court finds no unresolved prerequisite satisfied or breached.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Third Circuit must decide real preserved prerequisites without reopening the state-law answer, conceded permit possession, or constitutional ruling. Any available writ must remove both no-permit convictions and attributable sentence portions while preserving unrelated convictions and lawful custody. For the constitutional rule, see Criminal Procedure - Proof of the statutory offense, Fiore v. White, January 9, 2001, No. 98-942.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Fiore v. White, November 30, 1999: completes the reserved federal inquiry after the authoritative state answer; the earlier certification itself supplied neither state-law meaning nor a federal merits judgment.
- Lindh v. Murphy: preserves its filing-date distinction; this post-enactment application falls outside its pre-enactment exemption.
- Williams v. Taylor (Terry Williams): applies its independent statutory clauses and objective, date-bounded Supreme Court-law inquiry if an actual state merits adjudication triggers section 2254(d); no exclusive allocation between the clauses is created.

### Section 2255 attacks on expired state predicates

#### Daniels v. United States, 532 U.S. 374

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-9136.
**Decided:** April 25, 2001.

##### Whether a later federal enhancement reopens expired counseled state convictions after available original review

**Holding:** Section 2255 does not reopen an expired counseled state conviction for ineffective-assistance or involuntary-plea claims solely because it enhances a current federal sentence when meaningful original review was available and no external obstacle is identified. Unexcused loss of a usable opportunity does not make review unavailable; this restriction decides access, not the old conviction's constitutional validity.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; five direct joins.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No controlling rule resolves faultless unavailability or frustration of meaningful original review. Stone-Zsela's affirmative exception, Scalia's additional statutory-forum objection, three dissenters' broader current-sentence route and Breyer's sentencing-stage proposal do not yield a common five-Justice exception through Marks. Predicate classification, counting, statutory exclusions and a current sentencing-fact jury claim are not decided. A later independently invalidated predicate requires its own authorized vehicle, prerequisites and demonstrated enhancement effect, with no guaranteed reopening.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of the motion, leaving the 176-month sentence effective against these claims; no predicate invalidation, new hearing, resentencing or reduction is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Custis's sentencing-channel distinction extends to these available-review section 2255 circumstances, without overruling Custis or treating its expired-custody reservation as previously resolved. Parke preserves rebuttable regularity and proof, not conclusive validity. Maleng distinguishes expired custody from consequences; Garlotte's unexpired consecutive series does not encompass these independent expired sentences. Nichols supplies no review forum, and Fiore, Glover and Almendarez-Torres do not remove the distinct access conditions.

##### Whether unconstitutional complete deprivation of required counsel remains reviewable

**Holding:** Section 2255 remains available to challenge a predicate obtained through complete unconstitutional denial of required counsel without valid waiver, subject to applicable procedure and proof that it adversely affected the current sentence. Ineffective representation and involuntary counseled pleas do not qualify; ordinary default rules remain, generally requiring presentation at federal sentencing or a legally sufficient excuse.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same five-Justice Court opinion directly preserves this exception and its procedural limits.

**Material application:** Daniels alleges defective counseled proceedings, not complete deprivation, so this exception supplies no route for his motion.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Gideon supplies the entitlement and waiver boundary; Burgett, Tucker and Custis preserve the distinct rule for unconstitutional complete denial of counsel without converting all deficient representation into that category.

### Indirect review of expired convictions and a conditional appellate mandate

#### Lackawanna County District Attorney v. Coss, 532 U.S. 394

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1884.
**Decided:** April 25, 2001.

##### What the fractured judgment establishes for this section 2254 current-sentence challenge

**Authority of the judgment:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer vacate the Third Circuit judgment and support the precise conditional remand; O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas oppose that mandate. Breyer's lead explanation has only Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg's joins; Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment alone. No rationale controls under Marks: the affirmative no-fault route and the three-Justice broader current-sentence route supply only four possible access votes, while Breyer declines to decide the proposed exception. Neither a general indirect-review bar nor an additional exception or exclusive faultlessness requirement is established.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Court of Appeals must decide whether any pursued objection that the old convictions did not increase the sentence chosen within the range is reviewable or waived and, if reviewable, apply proper appellate review. Only an identified requirement of former section 2254(b) shown actually unresolved is to be examined, using the relevant materials. The former provision requires exhaustion, no available state corrective process, or circumstances rendering that process ineffective to protect the prisoner's rights. Existing deficient-performance, trial-prejudice and consideration determinations retain their lawful scope unless lawfully displaced.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The judgment replaces the lower court's retry-or-resentence direction; it orders no automatic relief, unconditional responsibility inquiry, evidentiary hearing, fixed sentence reduction or independent vacation of an expired judgment. No present finding decides waiver, fault for the unadjudicated state petition, adverse sentencing effect or a contrary merits fact. Removal of prior-record points does not itself settle influence on the within-range sentence, and consideration does not conclusively prove an increase. The September 1994 filing retains the former habeas framework. Newly discovered actual innocence is outside the claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** No majority enlarges Custis's sentencing rule into a section 2254 bar or resolves its meaningful-review reservation. Maleng and Garlotte retain their distinct custody rules; Strickland's performance and trial-prejudice inquiries and the existing determinations are preserved. Tucker and Glover do not supply a factual effect finding; Parke does not supply constitutional validity. Lindh preserves the filing-date distinction, and Fiore does not transfer later-filed habeas conditions to this petition. Marks supplies no rationale by adding a refusal to decide to an affirmative exception. No precedent is overruled.

### Capital habeas certificates and issue-specific appellate return

#### Penry v. Johnson, 532 U.S. 782

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-6677.
**Decided:** June 4, 2001.

##### Whether the Court can review the certificate denial and what the established mitigation gateway requires

**Holding:** Section 1254(1) permits review of the court of appeals' judicial denial of a certificate of appealability. The established mitigation violation and unreasonable state application require reversal of that component and an issue-specific appellate return, but certification and the settled gateway do not themselves authorize a present writ or immediate sentence vacatur before the remaining effect inquiry.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part I reviewability framework is unanimous. Part II's mitigation certification and corrective return are joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Part III's separate five-Justice effect standard governs the further inquiry, as stated under Criminal Procedure.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the adverse psychiatric certificate disposition and reverse the mitigation disposition. Constitutional error and statutory eligibility on the mitigation claim bind the return; the lower court must conduct the remaining whole-record effect review rather than relitigate those gateway conclusions. No writ or immediate penalty correction is entered before that inquiry.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hohn v. United States supplies review of a judicial certificate denial. Slack v. McDaniel preserves issue-specific certification and separates the substantial-showing threshold from ultimate relief.

For the psychiatric statutory ground, contradictory mitigation mechanism and State-borne Chapman effect category, see Criminal Procedure - Capital mitigation mechanisms and confined habeas effect review, Penry v. Johnson, No. 00-6677, decided June 4, 2001.

### Federal habeas pendency and statutory limitation periods

#### Duncan v. Walker, 533 U.S. 167

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-121.
**Decided:** June 18, 2001.

##### Whether federal habeas pendency qualifies for section 2244(d)(2) exclusion

**Holding:** The word State in 28 U.S.C. §2244(d)(2) modifies both postconviction review and other collateral review. A federal habeas application's pendency therefore is not excluded from the limitation period by that subsection, even when the application was timely and dismissed without prejudice because exhaustion was unestablished; a civil-rights pleading accompanying it does not change that classification.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven direct votes for both questions.

**Material application:** State modifies both forms of collateral review. The separate phrase other collateral review accommodates different state procedures and custody outside ordinary criminal convictions. A timely original federal filing, its nonmerits exhaustion dismissal, and a later application's non-successive status do not permanently satisfy the later application's deadline. Qualifying state review still requires proper filing, a connection to the pertinent judgment or claim, and actual pendency.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Rose v. Lundy supplies exhaustion's state/federal distinction without creating compulsory stays. Lindh v. Murphy protects its identified pre-enactment application, not all later filings about the conviction. Slack v. McDaniel preserves the exhaustion-return classification while retaining independent timing requirements. Artuz v. Bennett preserves the difference between filing conditions and claim-relief bars, without changing a federal filing's sovereign category.

##### What timeliness adjudication follows rejection of the federal-pendency exclusion

**Holding:** The Second Circuit's reinstatement cannot rest on excluding Walker's first federal proceeding under §2244(d)(2). On remand the courts must calculate timeliness without that exclusion and adjudicate any independently preserved, legally available commencement or equitable ground under the applicable law and actual record; if none makes the application timely, it must be dismissed as untimely.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven direct votes for both questions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The April 24, 1997 transitional deadline is this review's premise, not a universal commencement rule. General equitable-tolling availability and conditions, mixed-petition stays, the constitutional merits, and ultimate habeas entitlement remain undecided. Section 2254(b)(2) permits, but does not compel, merits denial of an unexhausted application. No universal warning duty, election, dismissal deadline, stay-and-abeyance procedure or automatic extension follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Second Circuit judgment is reversed and remanded. The equitable argument actually raised but not reached below receives its own lawful adjudication; the Court neither finds diligence or extraordinary impediment nor grants tolling, a writ, release or resentencing. Dismissal as untimely is required if no independently available ground establishes timeliness.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Irwin v. Department of Veterans Affairs and United States v. Brockamp preserve provision-sensitive analysis of equity but settle neither Walker's entitlement nor categorical unavailability of equitable protection.

### Habeas review of current immigration custody authority

#### Zadvydas v. Davis; Ashcroft v. Ma

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 99-7791 and 00-38.
**Decided:** June 28, 2001.

##### Whether habeas may determine current authority to detain or resume immigration custody

**Holding:** Section 2241 permits these independently proper challenges to the legal authority for detention, including the government's asserted authority to resume custody, without reopening the removal orders. The court must be able to determine that authority and afford effective relief when warranted; the decision does not authorize general factual retrial of removability or reweighing of lawful immigration discretion.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part I, joined by all eight other Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The actual custody, respondent, forum, exhaustion and other independent habeas predicates remain required; no supported defect is identified here. No comprehensive new Suspension Clause holding or general merits retrial of removability follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The governing detention limits and remand rules are recorded under Immigration — Removal-purpose limits on detention after the removal period. Effective relief must be available if lawful custody authority is absent.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Calcano-Martinez preserves independent §2241 review of substantial legal-authority objections with actual predicates and effective relief. St. Cyr applies that channel to independently filed habeas proceedings; its plea-specific temporal rule is not extended to every earlier custody episode.

### Independent immigration habeas and legal eligibility

#### INS v. St. Cyr

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-767.
**Decided:** June 25, 2001.

##### Whether the retained habeas channel reaches St. Cyr's actual legal-eligibility action

**Holding:** Section 2241 supplies jurisdiction over St. Cyr's properly brought substantial legal claim that the agency erroneously denied authority to consider former §212(c) relief. The permanent review provisions and repeal of the separate immigration-specific habeas provision do not foreclose this action; the court may decide the governing eligibility law and afford effective relief without reweighing immigration discretion.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Stevens's opinion of the Court, unanimous. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas apply Calcano as binding precedent without newly endorsing its original statutory construction.

**Material application:** The actual district-court action satisfies the presented channel requirements; no supported predicate defect or different claim type appears. The challenged categorical eligibility rule concerns legal authority, not the conviction or the exercise of immigration discretion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Custody, respondent, forum, exhaustion and other applicable conditions remain. Section 1252(c) requires the order and prior-validity-review disclosure; section 1252(d) preserves exhaustion of remedies available as of right and its earlier-review bar, except for previously unavailable grounds or an inadequate or ineffective earlier remedy. These provisions do not independently grant jurisdiction. Expedited removal is different; section 1252(g) governs its named acts rather than every completed-order challenge. General factual retrial, discretionary reweighing and a comprehensive Suspension Clause holding are excluded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Calcano-Martinez applies to the independently proper action rather than ordinary appellate petitions. Kolster and Goncalves preserve effective review of legal eligibility distinct from waiver equities. Felker, McCardle and Yerger retain their channel-specific background without a new constitutional minimum or required repeal formula.

For the other questions in INS v. St. Cyr, No. 00-767, decided June 25, 2001, see Immigration — Former-law eligibility after preenactment guilty pleas (the temporal rule and lawful-consideration remedy).

### Permanent immigration review restrictions and independent habeas

#### Calcano-Martinez v. INS

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-1011.
**Decided:** June 25, 2001.

##### Whether the permanent criminal-removability provision bars ordinary review of legal eligibility

**Holding:** When the permanent review provisions apply and the alien concedes removability for a covered aggravated felony, §1252(a)(2)(C) bars an ordinary petition for review seeking correction of the Board's legal exclusion from former §212(c) eligibility. The three petitions must therefore remain dismissed; their challenge to eligibility does not negate the conceded criminal-removability predicates.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, unanimous. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join only this part and oppose the complete affirmance on a different forum ground.

**Material application:** The May and June 1997 proceedings use the permanent system effective April 1, and each petitioner concedes the covered aggravated-felony predicate. An eligibility objection does not undo that concession. The specific criminal-removability restriction, rather than section 1252(a)(2)(B)'s separate discretionary-relief categories, controls; the latter retains its exception concerning section 1158(a) grants.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No disputed alienage or offense classification is presented. Pleaded due-process and equal-protection theories are not adjudicated substantial or meritorious, and the government's recognition of some constitutional and threshold review concedes no ordinary jurisdiction over these statutory claims.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kolster v. INS preserves the distinction between the withdrawn ordinary-review channel and the independent habeas question.

##### Whether the permanent scheme preserves effective section 2241 review of legal authority

**Holding:** The permanent provisions do not clearly displace §2241 jurisdiction over a substantial statutory or constitutional objection that executive custody or removal rests on an unlawful eligibility rule, when custody, respondent, forum, exhaustion and other applicable predicates are satisfied. That jurisdiction must be capable of deciding the legal-authority objection and affording effective relief when warranted; it does not authorize general factual retrial or reweighing of lawful discretionary judgments.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts II and III of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six direct votes, without combining separate rationales.

**Material application:** Sections 1252(a)(1), (a)(2)(C) and (b)(9) regulate and consolidate ordinary review without clearly repealing the independent writ. Section 1252(c)'s express habeas filing requirements confirm the distinction but confer no jurisdiction. The limited expedited-removal provision in section 1252(e) concerns section 1225(b)(1), not these proceedings. Repeal of former section 1105a's immigration-specific writ does not itself repeal section 2241. No indispensable section-number reference or verbal formula is required for a clear withdrawal.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A legal eligibility bar precedes discretionary weighing. Retained review must actually reach the authority objection and permit effective relief; detention-only review compelled to accept the disputed rule as valid is insufficient. No general factual retrial, review of lawful discretionary balancing, or comprehensive Suspension Clause minimum is established. A clear elimination of every effective route remains unadjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kolster's channel principle and Reno v. Goncalves's legal-eligibility distinction extend to this permanent configuration, without extending Goncalves's temporal holding. Felker, Ex parte McCardle and Ex parte Yerger require channel-specific repeal analysis; Heikkila distinguishes ordinary review from habeas. Reyes-Hernandez and American-Arab Anti-Discrimination Committee preserve section 1252(g)'s three-named-action limit, without creating a second jurisdictional ground.

##### What the direct-review dismissal determines about the independently filed habeas actions

**Holding:** The Second Circuit's dismissal of the three direct petitions is affirmed without prejudice to independently proper §2241 proceedings. Each separately filed habeas action remains for its own jurisdictional, procedural and merits adjudication under Part II; no individual eligibility, waiver, custody predicate or entitlement to interim relief is decided here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts II and III of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six direct votes, without combining separate rationales.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The three litigants' different histories, actual habeas predicates and temporal or constitutional merits require their own adjudication. Section 1252(c) requires the removal order and disclosure of earlier judicial validity decisions. Applicable exhaustion remains; section 1252(d)'s prior-validity-review restriction excepts grounds previously unavailable and an inadequate or ineffective earlier remedy. These jurisdictional dismissals do not decide a removal order's validity on the merits.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The direct petitions remain dismissed, without conversion or transfer. Independently proper habeas actions may receive effective relief on their actual predicates, but this affirmance awards no eligibility determination, waiver, release, agency reconsideration, mandatory hearing, removal, new stay or dissolution of a stay.

### Supreme Court retroactivity holdings and successive habeas review

#### Tyler v. Cain

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-5961.
**Decided:** June 28, 2001.

##### What Supreme Court action satisfies the successive-application retroactivity condition

**Holding:** Section 2244(b)(2)(A) is satisfied by an express Supreme Court retroactivity holding or by Supreme Court holdings whose necessary legal propositions jointly compel retroactivity; a lower court's persuasive extension, prediction or independent assessment does not suffice. The bar on review of an authorization order does not bar review of this later final judgment, and preliminary appellate permission does not excuse the district court's obligation to verify the statutory conditions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, joined by all eight other Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This is review of a final judgment after filing, not review of advance authorization. The new-rule and previous-unavailability conditions remain distinct generally but are conceded here. No factual-innocence, original-habeas or preserved-premature-claim exception is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Felker preserves separate authorization and district verification and the authorization-order review bar. Stewart’s previously presented and expressly preserved premature claim is distinguished; Schiro and Caspari retain their distinct ordinary nonretroactivity rules.


##### Whether existing holdings establish retroactivity for the verdict-wide reasonable-doubt defect

**Holding:** Teague's preserved exception for watershed procedures and Sullivan's necessary holding that a verdict-wide unconstitutional reasonable-doubt charge leaves no valid jury verdict jointly establish retroactivity for that confined Cage rule. This claim therefore satisfies section 2244(b)(2)(A)'s Supreme-Court-retroactivity requirement without a separate prior opinion reciting an express declaration about Cage.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five direct joins.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule is confined to destruction of the reasonable-doubt standard throughout the jury’s verdict. Structural classification, importance, or inability to apply harmless-error review alone does not make every rule retroactive. The decision does not find Tyler’s charge unconstitutional or change the constitutional standard governing the earlier state adjudication.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Teague supplies the contemporary fundamental-accuracy and bedrock-procedure exception. Sullivan’s necessary absence-of-a-valid-verdict holding, together with Teague, resolves the separately reserved collateral-gate question for this Cage rule; Sullivan alone had not decided it. Cage identifies the confined underlying rule.


##### What follows after satisfaction of the Supreme-Court-retroactivity gate

**Holding:** The Fifth Circuit's sole ground for refusing this application is erroneous; the case returns for review of the complete instruction and the district court's separate section 2254(d) merits disposition. A new retroactivity declaration is unnecessary because existing holdings already supply the required status, and passing this gate neither establishes a constitutional violation nor itself satisfies section 2254(d).

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five direct joins.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for review of the district court’s distinct merits disposition using the complete charge, actual state adjudication and Supreme Court law established at the legally relevant time. Newness and previous unavailability are not reopened. No immediate release, acquittal, retrial or evidentiary hearing is ordered. If the confined defect and all applicable merits requirements are ultimately established, conditional relief permits lawful retrial or termination of custody resting on the invalid conviction; terms remain for the lawful merits proceeding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No independent prerequisite is preserved merely through a generic formula; an additional matter requires an actually raised and legally available basis. Other rejected claims and unrelated instructional theories are not reopened. No new retroactivity declaration, advisory ruling on a later application, freestanding innocence claim or Suspension Clause determination is needed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Felker keeps actual statutory satisfaction distinct from authorization. Sullivan bars a harmless-error replacement for an established absent valid verdict; neither case makes passage through this gate equivalent to merits relief.

### Adequacy of state continuance requirements as applied

#### Lee v. Kemna, 534 U.S. 362

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-6933.
**Decided:** 2002-01-22.

##### Exceptional inadequacy of Missouri's written-motion and missing-witness rules

**Holding:** A normally valid state procedural rule does not bar federal review when its application is exorbitant in the particular circumstances. Here the combined unexpected disappearance of subpoenaed witnesses who had attended trial, substantial prior presentation of the alibi and anticipated return, brief requested delay, and the trial judge's stated scheduling impediments make the additional written and repeated showings inadequate as a federal-review bar; the holding creates no general oral-motion or emergency exemption.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's opinion speaks for Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, six Justices, on the complete rule and application.

**Material procedural requirements:** Missouri Rule 24.09 requires a written motion and affidavit unless the adverse party consents to an oral application. Rule 24.10 separately requires materiality and diligence; the witness's identity and residence if known, or diligence to learn them; reasonable grounds to expect attendance or testimony within a reasonable time; the particular expected facts and absence of another available person who could prove them as fully; and absence not procured, consented to or contrived by the applicant, with good faith rather than vexation or delay. If an affidavit is considered insufficient, the court must permit amendment. The oral-application exception does not erase these information requirements, and opponent consent is not inferred from silence.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The combined circumstances control; neither an unexpected absence, a scheduling observation nor prior testimony descriptions alone establishes this result. The Court finds no literal compliance and requires no judicial announcement of every defect.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Coleman v. Thompson, 501 U.S. 722: applies its requirement of an independent and adequate state ground and preserves exceptions for genuinely adequate defaults; the present inadequacy ruling precedes those excuses.
- Osborne v. Ohio, 495 U.S. 103: applies its distinction between useful presentation and purposeless repetition in the exceptional circumstances here; it supplies no general waiver of continuance requirements.
- Ford v. Georgia, 498 U.S. 411: preserves fair notice and regular application as adequacy safeguards without making them conclusive in every application.

##### Consequences of removing the inadequate default ground

**Holding:** With this state ground inadequate, Lee need not additionally establish cause and prejudice to overcome that ground; the federal court must adjudicate his continuance claim under due process and any independently applicable habeas restriction. The state appellate court's procedural ruling does not become an adjudication of the federal claim's merits under 28 U.S.C. §2254(d) merely because it resolved the appeal.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same six Justices join the bounded remand and its separation of adequacy from entitlement to relief.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The default-based judgment is vacated 6–3. The continuance claim returns for its due-process merits and any independently applicable habeas limitation. The conviction remains effective; separate cause and innocence gateway rulings are not affirmatively reversed.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The relatives' explanation for leaving and the truth of their proposed alibi remain disputed. Removing this default barrier establishes no underlying due-process violation, entitlement to a hearing or habeas relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Trest v. Cain (December 9, 1997): applies its distinction between procedural default and jurisdiction; it supplies neither an automatic hearing entitlement nor the underlying due-process violation.
- O'Sullivan v. Boerckel (June 7, 1999): preserves ordinary exhaustion through a state's normal review opportunities; the present adequacy holding does not dispense with otherwise applicable habeas requirements.

### Pre-AEDPA review and a dictated proportionality application

#### Henderson v. Norris

**Docket or dockets:** Supreme Court docket not supplied; Eighth Circuit No. 00-2203, 258 F.3d 706 (2001).
**Decided:** May 13, 2002.

For Henderson's substantive offense-specific sentence holding, May 13, 2002, Eighth Circuit No. 00-2203, see Criminal Procedure: Sentencing and Capital Punishment — Gross disproportionality of permanent imprisonment for a first tiny-sale conviction.

##### Pre-AEDPA independent legal review and Teague remain distinct inquiries

**Holding:** Henderson's noncapital federal habeas application, filed before April 24, 1996, is governed by pre-AEDPA law rather than amended section 2254(d); supported factual findings retain the treatment required by the former law, while the federal constitutional legal question receives independent review. Teague does not substitute general deference to every reasonable state legal interpretation for that review, but relief still requires a result dictated by precedent when the conviction became final or an actually applicable retroactivity exception.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Justice Souter's Opinion of the Court is joined by all eight other Justices. All nine adopt this temporal and review distinction; only the five identified in Parts II–III adopt the separate old-rule and sentence-relief conclusions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The temporal holding concerns the ordinary chapter 153 amendments at issue, not every AEDPA provision. Rejection of the State's general-deference theory abolishes no Teague requirement and finds no independent waiver of a specifically developed new-rule defense.

##### Existing law dictates the sentence-specific result

**Holding:** The finding of gross disproportionality for this first conviction, this exceptionally small completed sale and life imprisonment without ordinary parole applies the law already established by Rummel, Solem and Harmelin before Henderson's conviction became final. Relief therefore depends on no new categorical first-offender exemption, drug-weight ceiling or retroactivity exception.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Justice Souter's Opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer. All five support the complete old-rule conclusion and its stated boundaries. The four Justices who reject relief do not join it merely because they accept independent pre-AEDPA review.

**Material application:** An old general principle alone is insufficient: the specific result must be dictated. Rummel's repeated convictions and meaningful parole are absent; Solem distinguishes executive grace from parole; Harmelin's large-scale quantity does not equate every retail sale with that offense. The comparative evidence corroborates an exceptional mismatch without creating a schedule.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The conditional sentence-only writ is affirmed. Arkansas has ninety days after the Eighth Circuit issues its implementing mandate, subject to ordinary mandate administration, to impose a lawful replacement sentence before unconditional relief becomes operative. The conviction remains intact.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Lindh v. Murphy: applied to exclude amended section 2254(d) from this pre-April 24, 1996 noncapital petition; no exemption from every AEDPA provision is announced.
- Miller v. Fenton: applied to separate independent constitutional legal review from the former treatment of supported facts.
- Teague v. Lane: applied as a distinct nonretroactivity limit, not a general deference standard; relief rests on an old-rule application, without reliance on an exception.
- Graham v. Collins: applied only for the distinction between dictated application and extension of old law; its capital-mitigation result does not govern imprisonment duration.

### Capital representation, complete adversarial failure and collateral review

#### Bell v. Cone, 535 U.S. 685

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-400.
**Decided:** May 28, 2002.

##### Which section 2254 and Strickland standards govern the sentencing claim

**Holding:** The July 1, 1997 federal application is governed by amended §2254. Clearly established constitutional law is measured at the relevant state merits adjudication; a state application must be objectively unreasonable, not merely incorrect, and ordinary Strickland prejudice requires a reasonable probability of a different result without an additional fundamental-unfairness or unreliability requirement for lawful mitigation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part I, unanimous at the framework level; Stevens does not join the later applications.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Contrary-to and unreasonable-application inquiries remain distinct. Judicial disagreement alone neither satisfies nor defeats objective unreasonableness. Later opinions create no professional duty at the April 1982 trial or constitutional law unavailable at the state merits date.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lindh v. Murphy, June 23, 1997, supplies the application-date distinction. Williams v. Taylor (Terry Williams), 529 U.S. 362 (2000), supplies objective statutory review and rejection of an extra Fretwell hurdle for lawful mitigation. Strickland v. Washington, 466 U.S. 668 (1984), retains its separate performance and ordinary-prejudice requirements.

##### Whether the mitigation and closing omissions amount to Cronic's complete failure

**Holding:** Counsel's failure to call additional mitigation witnesses and waiver of closing do not amount to a complete failure of adversarial testing in this sentencing proceeding, where counsel substantively presented mitigation already in evidence, sought mercy, obtained favorable cross-examination and resisted damaging prosecution proof. The challenged choices require Strickland analysis; the conclusion neither excuses abandonment nor eliminates other recognized circumstances in which Cronic presumes prejudice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts II–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight direct votes. Stevens dissents.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding concerns actual substantive sentencing advocacy, not physical presence or a numerical count of acts. It does not make any activity sufficient to defeat every Cronic category.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** United States v. Cronic, 466 U.S. 648 (1984), is applied to the claimed complete-failure category; its other recognized grounds for presumed prejudice remain. Strickland governs the particular alleged errors within this represented proceeding.

##### Whether the confined tactical claim fails on an independently sufficient performance ground

**Holding:** On the reviewed available-mitigation and closing-argument record, the state court's rejection of deficient performance was not an objectively unreasonable application of Strickland. That sufficient ground defeats this confined claim without an actual-prejudice determination; it does not decide distinct allegations of failure to investigate or develop mitigation, their default treatment, or every remaining sentencing ground.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts II–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight direct votes.

**Material application:** The supported assessment includes reliance on experts already heard, the mother’s ineffective appearance, interviews, the absence of helpful omitted evidence on the reviewed record, and avoiding a particular unanswered prosecutorial rebuttal. A strategy label is not conclusive. Cozzolino permits actual rebuttal of mitigation, not indiscriminate bad-acts proof; Pritchett did not guarantee resentencing whenever any aggravator failed. Cone’s later direct-appeal ruling is not attributed to counsel’s trial-date knowledge.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No witness-specific admissibility finding, endorsement of deliberately created appellate error, determination of actual prejudice, or resolution of distinct investigation and default allegations follows. Lee does not resolve the independent Tennessee procedural-ground and funding-cause questions.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Cronic-based sentence writ is reversed and the confined ordinary tactical claim rejected; the Court of Appeals must adjudicate the remaining properly pending sentence claims and distinct investigation/default issues under their own legal requirements. The convictions remain undisturbed, and no blanket reinstatement of the district court's entire sentencing judgment follows. Ordinary procedure governs necessary further proceedings; no evidentiary hearing or investigative funding is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Williams and Strickland are applied to an independently sufficient, context-specific performance ground. Cozzolino v. State, 584 S.W.2d 765 (Tenn. 1979), and State v. Pritchett, 621 S.W.2d 127 (Tenn. 1981), retain their actual relevance and aggravator limits; Cone v. State, 665 S.W.2d 87 (Tenn. 1984), remains separately dated.

#### Carey v. Saffold, 536 U.S. 214

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-301.
**Decided:** 2002-06-17.

##### Timely ordinary state review includes its intervals

**Holding:** Under 28 U.S.C. §2244(d)(2), a properly filed state collateral application remains pending during timely continuation through the State's ordinary review process, including the interval before a timely filing at the next available level. California's successive-level original petitions qualify when they perform that review function; an abandoned or completed process, an unreasonable gap or an unrelated later attack does not receive interval exclusion merely because another collateral petition is possible.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Breyer; six direct votes for both holdings.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Proper filing and actual pendency remain separate requirements. The rule covers timely continuation of ordinary state review rather than every extraordinary, repetitive or possible future collateral application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Artuz and Duncan are applied without alteration; Boerckel supplies ordinary-review context. The previously open interval question is resolved at this functional, timely-continuation scope.

##### State timeliness and the actual federal calculation remain necessary

**Holding:** An alternative merits discussion does not establish timely state filing or override a valid independent state determination that the relevant interval was unreasonable. The Ninth Circuit must determine the California order's actual meaning, the applicable state timing standard, the supported reasons for this interval and the exact elapsed time, then recalculate the federal limitation without excluding any interval that fails the governing requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same six-Justice Court opinion. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas also reject treating alternative merits language as conclusive of timeliness, but do not join the functional-pendency rule or mandate.

**Material application:** The California Supreme Court's order referred both to merits and lack of diligence after an interval of roughly four and one-half months. No nationwide deadline or presumption that this particular interval is reasonable or late replaces the state-law inquiry.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Certification or other lawful clarification may be used if needed; it is not automatically required. The decision does not grant equitable tolling, resolve the constitutional claims, or answer Artuz’s separate question whether an exception to an actual filing deadline can preserve proper filing.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate and remand for the state-timeliness determination and exact federal calculation. Independently preserved lawful commencement or equitable grounds remain subject to their own requirements. If none makes the application timely, dismissal as untimely is required; other habeas restrictions remain.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Artuz's filing-deadline-exception reservation remains; Duncan's independent timing rules remain. Lee's adequate-state-ground inquiry and Young's bankruptcy tolling do not determine statutory pendency here.

#### Horn v. Banks, 536 U.S. 266

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-1385.
**Decided:** 2002-06-17.

##### A preserved nonretroactivity defense must be resolved before granting relief

**Holding:** A federal habeas court may not grant a writ while leaving unresolved a properly preserved, applicable Teague defense capable of defeating relief. Section 2254(d) adds a condition to relief; satisfaction of that condition does not remove nonretroactivity review merely because the state court did not discuss it, and the requirement to justify a grant does not impose an inflexible sequence before every denial.

**Proposition-level authority:** Unanimous per curiam opinion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The court must identify finality and the precise rule sought and decide whether prefinality precedent dictated it. A later publication date, changed facts or judicial disagreement alone does not establish newness. If the rule is new, both applicable exceptions remain: rules removing conduct, persons or punishments from the State’s substantive power, and watershed procedures essential to accuracy and fundamental fairness. The Court decides neither Mills’s newness nor either exception’s application.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the omitted-defense ruling and set aside the resulting death-sentence habeas relief. Before a renewed grant, resolve Pennsylvania’s preserved nonretroactivity defense and any genuinely remaining requirement. The instructional merits, convictions and unrelated claims remain unadjudicated; no new penalty trial, sentence affirmance on an unexamined ground or execution is ordered.

**Material application:** Pennsylvania preserved the defense in both federal courts. Satisfying §2254(d) could not replace that independent requirement. Nonretroactivity remains nonjurisdictional; this decision does not revive a forfeited defense or require its resolution before a denial on another sufficient ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Caspari and Williams are applied to distinct conditions for relief; Schiro and Lambrix retain their preservation and sequencing limits; Tyler's separate statutory gate is distinguished.

#### Stewart v. Smith, 536 U.S. 856

**Docket or dockets:** No. 01-339.
**Decided:** June 28, 2002.

##### The clarified waiver rule classifies rights without deciding their violation

**Holding:** Under Arizona's authoritative construction of Rule 32.2(a)(3) for the 1995 petition, whether a claim requires personal, knowing, voluntary and intelligent waiver depends on the kind of right implicated; rights within that heightened-waiver category are not forfeited by mere omission. Identifying the category does not itself adjudicate the alleged federal violation, although a state procedural determination that actually depends on a federal constitutional ruling is not independent.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court accepts Arizona’s construction without converting state waiver categories into federal merits law. It assumes for decision that Ake’s direct-review independence standard applies to habeas and does not establish universal identity between those settings.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Ake excludes a procedural ground that actually depends on federal merits; Coleman retains separate adequacy and independence. Prouse distinguishes identifying a right from resting on a federal determination. Trest and Lambrix preserve procedural default’s nonjurisdictional character.

##### The actual state order rests on omission and consideration of an excuse

**Holding:** The state order applying Rule 32.2(a)(3) rests on Smith's omission of the sentencing ineffective-assistance claim from his earlier petitions and its rejection of the asserted office-affiliation excuse, rather than on adjudication of trial counsel's constitutional performance. Its colorability reference, read in that context, does not make this procedural ruling dependent on federal merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Rejecting the stated office-affiliation explanation for purposes of independence does not establish loyal representation, harmlessness of every institutional conflict or failure of every otherwise available cause theory.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Carey directs attention to the state order’s actual meaning, not an isolated merits reference. Mickens’s victim-counsel conflict setting supplies neither an automatic office-affiliation conflict nor a default excuse.

##### Independence is settled, while genuinely remaining requirements retain their own force

**Holding:** The Ninth Circuit's nonindependence ruling is reversed, and the case is expressly remanded for proceedings consistent with the independent character of this state ground. Adequacy was accepted below and is not affirmatively reopened; any properly preserved, legally available cause-and-prejudice or miscarriage-of-justice question retains its own requirements, and a valid, applicable, preserved and unexcused procedural bar must be enforced.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of the per curiam opinion, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No valid-cause determination or underlying sentencing ineffective-assistance merits decision is made. Further proceedings do not automatically require an evidentiary hearing or reopen settled matters, and no writ, new sentencing proceeding or execution authority follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the nonindependence judgment and remand, 9–0, preserving the accepted adequacy determination and only legally available remaining requirements and excuses.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Coleman’s cause-and-prejudice and miscarriage-of-justice exceptions retain independent requirements. Lee’s exceptional adequacy application does not reopen this accepted adequacy determination. Bell preserves genuinely pending issues without mandating inquiry, and Horn confirms that satisfying one habeas condition does not eliminate others.

## Standards and Tests selection: Delegated Customs interpretation and independent tariff review

### Delegated Customs interpretation and independent tariff review

**Governing question:** Delegated interpretive force of Customs regulations and classification letters in tariff litigation.

**Current rule:** A formally promulgated Customs regulation implementing actual congressional delegation receives Chevron review in tariff litigation. Courts first enforce unambiguous statutory requirements and delegation boundaries, then assess a reasonable agency choice within genuine ambiguity. The trade court’s duty to decide on its own record does not remove this legal framework. Agency labels and notice and comment alone establish neither authority nor validity; statutory construction, regulatory validity and application to found facts remain distinct.

Controlling Chevron treatment requires congressional conferral, expressly or through the statutory scheme, of authority to make interpretations with the force of law and an interpretation issued in exercise of that authority. A transaction-specific, revisable Customs classification letter issued through the Mead mechanism does not satisfy the requirement. Administrative responsibility, expertise and official institutional endorsement do not themselves supply the missing legal force. Formal rulemaking or adjudication may evidence delegation, but neither notice and comment nor a document label is an exclusive prerequisite.

**Authority by component:**

- United States v. Haggar Apparel Co. — Canonical Decision Record, Holdings, “Trade-court factfinding does not eliminate lawful delegated interpretation”, 1999-04-21; Kennedy Parts I–III, all nine Justices.

- Delegated-force threshold and classification-letter exclusion: Canonical Decision Record `United_States_v_Mead_Corp_merits_2001-06-18.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / I. This ruling mechanism does not exercise authority to bind the reviewing court, 2001-06-18; Kennedy with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Present operation effective:** Haggar, April 21, 1999 for promulgated regulations and own-record factfinding; Mead, June 18, 2001 for the delegated-force threshold and the specified classification-letter mechanism.

**Open question:** Mead resolves the informal-letter reservation for the specified mechanism, not every informal interpretation. Other statutory mechanisms and interpretation of an agency's own regulations retain their separate inquiries. The particular Haggar regulation's ultimate reasonableness and importer's exemption are not established by its delegation holding; Mead likewise adopts no ultimate tariff classification. Reviewability of guidance does not itself establish controlling interpretive force.

**Holdings navigation:** [United States v. Haggar Apparel Co. — Trade-court factfinding does not eliminate lawful delegated interpretation](HOLDINGS.md#trade-court-factfinding-does-not-eliminate-lawful-delegated-interpretation-1). [United States v. Mead Corp., 533 U.S. 218 — Customs classification letters and judicial interpretive authority](HOLDINGS.md#united-states-v-mead-corp-533-us-218).


**Related current rules:** Persuasive weight of a nonbinding agency advisory interpretation governs independent judicial consideration when controlling force is unavailable.

## Standards and Tests selection: One-year state-custody habeas filing period

### One-year state-custody habeas filing period

**Governing question:** The limitation period under amended §2244(d).

**Current rule:** A person in custody pursuant to a state-court judgment has one year to apply for habeas relief, running from the latest of: finality by completion of direct review or expiration of time to seek it; removal of a State-created filing impediment violating federal law that actually prevented filing; initial Supreme Court recognition of the asserted constitutional right, if newly recognized and made retroactively applicable on collateral review; or when due diligence could have discovered the claim’s factual predicate. Time during which a properly filed application for state postconviction or other collateral review of the pertinent judgment or claim is pending is excluded.

Proper filing concerns compliance with requirements for delivery and acceptance of the application, rather than the success or relief eligibility of its individual claims. Claim-relief bars therefore do not invalidate an otherwise proper filing. Clerk acceptance cannot cure a genuine filing defect. Form, deadlines, the proper receiving court or office, fees and filing preconditions must be distinguished from rules requiring denial of relief on a claim.

The state-proceeding limitation applies to both postconviction review and other collateral review. Pendency of a federal habeas application is not excluded under section 2244(d)(2), even if that application was timely and dismissed without prejudice for unestablished exhaustion. Accompanying civil-rights allegations do not convert it into state review. A later application's non-successive status and an earlier application's timely filing do not themselves make the later application timely; proper filing, actual pendency and relation to the pertinent judgment or claim remain independent conditions.

A properly filed state collateral application remains pending through timely continuation of the ordinary state-review process, including qualifying intervals before filing at the next available level. Successive-level original petitions qualify when they perform that review function. Completion, abandonment, an unreasonable interval or an unrelated later attack does not receive exclusion merely because another petition is possible. A state merits discussion does not override an independent valid untimeliness determination. Where the order is ambiguous, the federal court must determine its actual meaning, the applicable state timing rule, the supported explanation for the interval and elapsed time; lawful clarification may be used but is not invariably required. No national fixed deadline or automatic reasonableness presumption applies.

**Authority by component:**

- Four alternative latest-event triggers and pending-state-review exclusion: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §101, 28 U.S.C. §2244(d), enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.

- Proper application filing distinguished from claim-relief bars: Canonical Decision Record `Artuz_v_Bennett_merits_2000-11-07.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / Filing an application is distinct from entitlement to relief on its claims, 2000-11-07; Ginsburg for all nine Justices.
- State-only collateral pendency, independent timing and preservation of actual alternative grounds: Canonical Decision Record `Duncan_v_Walker_merits_2001-06-18.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / 1. Federal habeas pendency is outside section 2244(d)(2); 2. The statutory error requires reversal, with actual independent alternatives preserved, 2001-06-18; seven direct joins to O’Connor’s opinion.

- Canonical Decision Record `carey-v-saffold_merits_2002-06-17.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / Timely ordinary state review includes its intervals; State timeliness and the actual federal calculation remain necessary; June 17, 2002; Ginsburg with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Breyer (six direct joins).

**Present operation effective:** Carey v. Saffold, June 17, 2002, for ordinary-review interval pendency and state-timeliness limits. Duncan, Artuz and the enacted commencement triggers retain their earlier effective dates and distinct functions.

**Effective transition:** Enacted April 24, 1996. “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” states Lindh's noncapital chapter 153 application-date construction, effective 1997-06-23. Separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case clause and Felker's successive-petition treatment remain distinct; Lindh does not settle every other Title I provision's application to pending proceedings. Authority: Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg's six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

**Holdings navigation:** Lindh v. Murphy (1997-06-23) — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application. [Artuz v. Bennett, 531 U.S. 4 — Habeas limitations and state collateral review](HOLDINGS.md#artuz-v-bennett-531-us-4); [Duncan v. Walker, 533 U.S. 167 — Federal habeas pendency and statutory limitation periods](HOLDINGS.md#duncan-v-walker-533-us-167).

**Open question:** Artuz leaves unresolved whether an exception to a filing deadline can make a late application properly filed. Duncan does not decide the general availability or conditions of equitable tolling or stays of mixed petitions. Preserved, legally available commencement or equitable grounds require their own actual predicates; neither an automatic extension nor categorical foreclosure follows. Section 2254(b)(2) permits, but does not require, merits denial of an unexhausted application.

## Standards and Tests selection: Habeas and §2255 appeals and appellate certificates

### Habeas and §2255 appeals and appellate certificates

**Governing question:** Appeal from district habeas and §2255 orders and the certificate procedures in amended §2253 and Rule 22.

**Current rule:** District final habeas and §2255 orders are reviewable in the appropriate circuit, except that §2253(b) supplies no right of appeal from a final order testing a federal criminal removal warrant or detention pending removal. An applicant’s appeal from a final state-process habeas order or §2255 order requires a certificate of appealability from a circuit justice or judge, issued only upon a substantial showing of denial of a constitutional right and identifying the qualifying issues. Rule 22(a) directs original applications to the appropriate district court; a circuit judge must transfer an application there, and after district denial the applicant may pursue an authorized appeal rather than renew before a circuit judge. For state-process habeas appeals, Rule 22(b) permits a district or circuit judge’s certificate; the district judge must issue it or explain refusal and send the certificate/reasons with the notice and file to the appellate court. After district refusal, the applicant may request a circuit judge’s certificate; a request addressed to the court is addressed to its judges, and a notice of appeal without an express request serves as that request. No certificate is required for an appeal by a State or its representative. An appellate court’s judicial denial of a certificate of appealability is a case in that court reviewable by certiorari under §1254(1), although it prevents the merits appeal from proceeding. Review or vacatur of a certificate denial does not itself issue a certificate or satisfy its substantial-showing and issue-identification conditions.

An authorized judge’s actually issued, issue-specific certificate admits the identified claim to appeal even when the judge allegedly erred in assessing §2253(c)(2)’s constitutional showing. That alleged assessment error does not itself nullify the existing appellate authorization. The substantial-constitutional-showing condition remains necessary for proper issuance; this rule neither finds a bare rules claim sufficient nor authorizes future certificates without the statutory showing. When dismissal rests on procedure without reaching the constitutional claim, certification requires reasonable debate both over whether the petition states a valid constitutional claim and over the correctness of the procedural ruling. Both are threshold showings; the court may address the more apparent one first and must identify any certified issues. Mere nonfrivolousness, a debatable procedural question alone or a bare constitutional label is insufficient; ultimate merits success is not required. For an actual merits rejection, reasonable jurists must be able to debate the assessment or find it wrong.

**Authority by component:**

- Appeal, certificate, district/circuit application and transfer procedures: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §§102–103, 28 U.S.C. §2253 and Fed. R. App. P. 22, enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.

- Hohn Holdings I–II: certiorari review and distinct certificate requirements: Hohn v. United States — controlling Holdings, 1998-06-15.

- Issued authorization distinguished from correctness of the constitutional-showing assessment: Canonical Decision Record `peguero_v_united_states_merits_1999-03-02.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / The issued certificate supplied the appellate authorization in this case, 1999-03-02; eight Associate joins.

- Post-enactment appeal transition: Slack v. McDaniel — II. The post-enactment appeal requires the amended certificate, April 26, 2000; Ginsburg with O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.
- Constitutional and procedural debate, no fixed order, and issue identification: Slack — III. A procedural-dismissal certificate requires both constitutional and procedural showings, April 26, 2000; all nine directly adopt the construction.

**Present operation effective:** Slack, April 26, 2000, for the appellate transition and two-component procedural-dismissal showing; the earlier issued-certificate and reviewability rules retain their authority.

**Effective transition:** Amended §2253(c) governs an applicant’s appeal initiated after April 24, 1996 even when identified pre-AEDPA law governs district adjudication of the earlier application. A CPC label or an earlier contrary legal position does not supply missing authorization. This does not apply amended §2254(d) to the earlier application or establish a universal temporal rule for every amendment. Authority: Slack v. McDaniel, Part II, April 26, 2000. The distinct timing rules in “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” retain their scope.

**Exceptions and limits:** Rule 22’s circuit-judge transfer language does not repeal the Supreme Court’s distinct original habeas authority; see “Supreme Court original habeas after an authorization-review bar.”
